#5940. At least 23 states considered laws governing ICE and schools this year

Catch up on what you missed with our Education newsletter, sent weekly.

Andrea Garza says it's not unusual or alarming when U.S. Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents are spotted near a school in Laredo, Texas, a border town.

In other parts of the country, "seeing the presence of border patrol would be, you know, different, maybe shocking. Just something from another world, right?" she said. "It's something very normal here."

Garza is a school leader with the United Independent School District , which serves Laredo. Federal data shows 94% of her district's community is Hispanic. Garza said some families have been in Laredo for generations, while others came more recently, and the federal government is one of her area's largest employers.

She said she knows what she would do if ICE comes knocking: If they don't have a warrant or subpoena, they don't get in.

"We serve 'in loco parentis' for students," she said, meaning "in place of a parent" when the students' rights are challenged.

"It's not interpreted, in the way we see it, as interfering with the law," Garza explained. "It is more just verifying that we are all following the law."

Texas state law prohibits local entities, including campus police, from obstructing federal immigration enforcement. But NPR found no state laws that directly address how much access these officers should have in K-12 public schools.

And it's not just Texas. Many states lack clear policies governing what these interactions should look like. Recent federal changes haven't helped.

At the beginning of President Trump's second term, the administration rescinded a policy that protected schools, among other "sensitive" places, from immigration enforcement.

"Criminals will no longer be able to hide in America's schools and churches to avoid arrest," a statement from the U.S. Department of Homeland Security said at the time. "The Trump Administration will not tie the hands of our brave law enforcement, and instead trusts them to use common sense."

In some places, school leaders and researchers say Trump's immigration policies have led to enrollment drops.

DHS, which houses ICE, has said that ICE does not raid or target schools.

But reporting from K-12 Dive shows the last academic year still saw several incidents of ICE agents on school grounds. When that happens, education leaders often rely on a patchwork of state and district policies to know what to do.

This year, many state legislatures tried to update those policies.

An NPR analysis found that, so far this year, at least 23 states have considered new legislative proposals — in some cases multiple proposals — governing federal immigration officers and K-12 public schools.

The vast majority of the state proposals NPR reviewed — 35 out of 40 — sought to limit federal officers' access to K-12 campuses and students; only one proposal — in Mississippi — sought to ensure that schools cooperate with ICE. The rest didn't explicitly limit or increase access or cooperation.

Thomas Saenz is president and general counsel of the Mexican American Legal Defense and Educational Fund, an organization that advocates for Latinos living in the U.S. He said the elimination of the federal sensitive locations policy is likely driving many states to establish their own protections, "belts and suspenders, as they say."

But while lawmakers seem to agree there's a need for clearer policies, in many places, they've struggled to find consensus over what those policies should be. Of the 40 proposals NPR reviewed, only 11 became laws by the beginning of this school year.

Minnesota, Arizona, Delaware, Georgia, Maine and Nebraska are among the state legislatures that considered restricting ICE access to schools.

Several of these proposals specified the need for a warrant. Others aimed to restrict what school police and school resource officers, or SROs, can and can't do, including when it comes to immigration-related arrests.

On the other side of the spectrum, Mississippi considered and passed a law requiring government entities, including school districts, to cooperate with federal immigration enforcement.

And a few states considered requiring school boards to adopt policies outlining how they would respond to immigration authorities on school property.

At least eight states already had laws governing immigration enforcement and K-12 schools at the start of 2026. A few of those states — including Connecticut, Maryland and Oregon — considered additional legislation this year.

Recent state proposals have also aimed to allow public schools to charge tuition for students who aren't U.S. citizens or legal immigrants. Such proposals would challenge the decades-old landmark Supreme Court ruling Plyler v. Doe, which established that all children, regardless of immigration status, have the right to a free K-12 public education.

NPR did not include these proposals in its analysis. According to reporting from Education Week, as of July, at least two states were considering proposals challenging Plyler. Similar efforts failed in at least five other states.

Following the Trump administration's decision to rescind the policy protecting "sensitive" places, some states have been trying to set their own rules around where immigration enforcement can and can't go.

A few states — including West Virginia, Hawaii and Kansas — made a push this year to restrict ICE access by designating school campuses as sensitive locations. None of those three states' proposals passed.

Some states already have laws that explicitly ban or restrict these types of sanctuary policies. That didn't stop Missouri from considering a proposal this spring to limit school cooperation with immigration enforcement. An existing state law prohibiting municipalities from adopting sanctuary policies and requiring cooperation calls into question how far such a proposal could go.

Lawmakers in Tennessee considered multiple proposals this year from across the political spectrum, including one to track K-12 students' legal status, which opponents argued was part of an effort to challenge Plyler, and another that would require schools to immediately notify all students, parents and employees if immigration officers were on school grounds.

"As legislators, we cannot make informed decisions without data, and right now we simply do not know what the attendance is in our school systems across the state, regarding children who are here illegally," Tennessee Rep. Jason Zachary (R-Knoxville) said in a March hearing, where he spoke in favor of the measure that would have required schools to report students who are undocumented.

The bill did not make it to a final vote.

Meanwhile, another Tennessee proposal this year would have prohibited certain sensitive areas, including schools, from being used as staging areas, processing locations or operational bases for immigration enforcement.

"Everybody deserves to have a great learning environment, and it's hard to teach when you are worried that one of your students is going to be taken," Tennessee Rep. Gabby Salinas (D-Memphis), who sponsored that bill, told NPR. The proposal ultimately failed.

NPR was not able to find a pre-existing state law in Minnesota. But after the immigration crackdown in Minneapolis earlier this year, lawmakers considered at least five different proposals around ICE and schools.

They ranged from stricter warrant requirements to mandating that schools develop procedures for handling requests from law enforcement. None of the five proposals made it to a final vote.

That means school leaders like Principal Kenny Newby are left waiting.

Newby leads Justice Alan Page Elementary School in Maplewood, Minn., just outside the Twin Cities. He said if ICE ever comes to his school, his goal is to be kind, stay calm and be firm.

In the absence of state laws, he said he has his own district's policies to lean on: Staff members are supposed to welcome the ICE officers, start a conversation, ask for required paperwork and notify school-level administrators. Those administrators then reach out to district leaders.

If the officers have a warrant, they would be directed to the district office for next steps. Newby stressed: "We would not allow them to have direct contact with students."

Rob Reetz is the principal of Mounds View High School in nearby Arden Hills. He said it would be helpful to have a state law governing how schools are supposed to interact with federal immigration officers; if nothing else, a law like that could simplify interactions between districts.

Right now, Reetz said, if a sports team from his school travels to play against a team from another district and ICE shows up at the game, it's unclear which district's protocols adults should follow.

Still, if ICE does come knocking at his school, Reetz worries they won't necessarily follow rules set by the state or any other entity.

He said he remembers learning in January that federal agents had been spotted at Roosevelt High School in Minneapolis. At the time, MPR News reported that armed U.S. Border Patrol officers came onto school property during dismissal and "began tackling people, handcuffed two staff members and released chemical weapons on bystanders."

"It contributed to this uncertainty about how would our interactions go with immigration enforcement if they were to be on our campus," Reetz said.

Newby echoed these concerns. He said he and his staff would feel more supported if there were "clear expectations on keeping schools a safe place."

Edited by: Nicole CohenAudio produced by: Lauren MigakiResearch by: Natalie Pate, Nicole Cohen, Sarah Knight and Barclay Walsh

#5933. Senators don't always share bill text online before votes. They should, advocates say

Paper copies of amendment votes are set on display near the Senate Chamber, on July 1, 2025 on Capitol Hill. Tom Brenner For The Washington Post via Getty Images hide caption

If you want to know what bills are under consideration in the Senate, you could follow floor proceedings live on C-SPAN. But, even for parliamentary procedure experts, it can be difficult to understand exactly what's happening.

Among the issues? The text of what the lawmakers are considering isn't always made available for public review before it is voted on. Now, a crosspartisan group of open governance advocates has sent a letter to Senate leadership calling for change. In the letter, first obtained by NPR, they call on the Senate to post bill and amendment text online as soon as it is made available to members.

"The public and the press and the whole apparatus that supports the Senate to help understand what legislation is, they don't have access," said Daniel Schuman, an author on the letter and the executive director of the American Governance Institute, a nonpartisan nonprofit dedicated to strengthening the institutions of American democracy.

That lack of transparency means "that problems can often sneak through," said Schuman. "At times, this has been a mechanism for the Senate to move legislation to avoid the proper public scrutiny." In 2013, Schuman recalled, Congress quietly repealed provisions of a popular congressional financial disclosure and insider trading law — skirting public scrutiny.

While the Congressional Record — the official record of legislative branch proceedings and debates — does provide information about bill text and proposed amendments, it is not published until after the legislative day concludes. That means when bills or amendments are offered on the same day they are considered, they may not be publicly available until after they are voted on.

"We've been working first to get the Senate to update their own systems internally," said Luke Hogg, a senior fellow at the Foundation for American Innovation, which works to help policymakers become more effective stewards of innovation.

The organization joined AGI on the letter, and as Hogg explained, now that staffers themselves have reliable access to what is being considered, "it should also be given to the American public."

In contrast to the Senate, the House of Representatives publishes draft legislative text and proposed amendments online ahead of their consideration, with rare exceptions.

The letter argues that the current Senate process creates unequal access to information, where staff and interest groups with connections to Senate offices understand what's being considered, but legislative support agencies like the Congressional Research Service and the Government Accountability Office, as well as the press and public at large are often left in the dark.

"Privileged access to public business that currently is the subject of floor debate should be minimized to the extent possible," the letter says.

The advocates hope to see the Senate enact the public transparency effort in the new Congress in January.

In a statement to NPR, an aide to Sen. Alex Padilla, the California Democrat who is expected to become chairman of the Senate Rules committee, said that the senator is open to input on the transparency measure as well as other ideas to improve the functioning of Congress.

Sen. John Thune, Senate Republican leader, did not respond to a request for comment.

#5925. A blue wave helped offset for now a falling tally of Black members in the U.S. House

Democratic Reps. Terri Sewell (left) and Shomari Figures of Alabama appear at a voting rights rally in May on the steps of the state's Capitol in Montgomery, Ala. Jake Crandall/USA TODAY Network via Reuters Connect hide caption

A U.S. Supreme Court ruling this year that further weakened the Voting Rights Act raised the possibility of a record drop in Black representation in Congress.

But a Democratic blue wave in the midterm elections helped forestall such a decline for now.

Most notably, Rep. Shomari Figures, a Black Democrat, won reelection despite Alabama's Republican state leaders making his district far more GOP-leaning after the court's decision.

Black representation in the U.S. House is still slated to decline next year, according to an NPR analysis. The incoming Congress is set to include 60 Black House members, including nonvoting members, down from 63 in the current House. That's just one away from the record loss of four Black representatives after the 1876 election, around the end of the Reconstruction era.

In addition to the unprecedented mid-decade redistricting fight, the usual churn of politics, including members retiring or running for higher office, contributed to next year's falling tally.

But high Black voter turnout, especially in the South, fueled Democratic wins that have offset the upcoming decline in the number of Black members of the House.

Voting rights advocates and redistricting experts warn, however, that the redrawing of district lines in the coming years, including after the 2030 census, continues to pose a threat to Black representation in Congress.

Heading into this year's midterms, Figures faced tough odds of keeping his House seat in Alabama, where voting has been racially polarized between a Republican-supporting white majority and a Democratic-supporting Black minority.

Figures joined Congress after the 2024 election as the first Black representative of the state's 2nd Congressional District. As part of a long redistricting battle, a lower federal court had ordered the district to be drawn to include a Black population large enough to have a realistic opportunity of electing their preferred candidate.

For the midterms, though, the Supreme Court's conservative majority allowed Alabama's Republican-controlled legislature to redraw the district. That ruling came weeks after the high court released its landmark decision in Louisiana v. Callais, which weakened the Voting Rights Act's protections against racial discrimination in political mapmaking.

The new lines of Alabama's 2nd District, the lower court had previously found, were "tainted by intentional race-based discrimination" and would "afford Black-preferred candidates zero chance of winning there." Based on the redrawn map, Figures appeared to be on track to leave Congress like Rep. Cleo Fields, the Louisiana Democrat who decided not to seek reelection after the Supreme Court's Callais ruling struck down the Black opportunity district Fields represents.

"It took a remarkable level of turnout in order for [Figures] to be able to win," says Cliff Albright, co-founder and executive director of Black Voters Matter. "Some are calling it a blue wave. For us, particularly here in the South, we're calling it a Black wave because what we saw was the Black electorate's been motivated by the attacks on our voting rights, by the gerrymandering that was taking place."

New voting maps in Texas and Florida led to the undoing of some Black-represented districts, but Democrats' sweeping victories in other states are helping to counteract those losses.

North Carolina's GOP-controlled legislature redrew the district of Rep. Don Davis, one of the state's three Black representatives, but Davis defended his seat. And two incoming Black House Democrats — Cyril Jefferson and LaKesha Womack — flipped districts long represented by white Republicans.

Democratic socialist Reps.-elect Melat Kiros in Colorado and Donavan McKinney in Michigan are also adding to the tally.

And Democrats are set to take control of statewide offices and legislative chambers in Texas and other places that were long-standing Republican strongholds.

By breaking the GOP trifecta in Georgia, for instance, the blue wave lowered the redistricting threat facing the state's congressional districts represented by Black Democrats. Last week, Georgia's outgoing Republican Gov. Brian Kemp announced he will not call a special session on redistricting before handing the office in January to Gov.-elect Keisha Lance Bottoms, who will be the country's first Black woman elected governor.

Rep. Don Davis, a Democrat from North Carolina, speaks at a 2024 campaign event for then-Vice President Kamala Harris in Greenville, N.C. David Yeazell/AP hide caption

But Albright, of Black Voters Matter, sees this year's midterms as unique.

"What happens two years from now or four years from now, where it's not the same wave year, where there's not the same economic realities across the country, where you don't necessarily get the same number of white voters that decide, 'Oh, I guess I can vote for this Democrat'?" Albright says. "It's simply not sustainable for Black voters to be carved up and have our voting power that diluted and for it to not have an impact over a more sustained period of time."

And while midterm successes are leading some Democratic politicians to declare a generational shift in U.S. politics, Kareem Crayton, a redistricting expert and vice president of the Brennan Center for Justice's Washington, D.C., office, is taking a more wait-and-see approach.

"This sounds a lot like what people said in 2009 after a lot of massive change happened, including in the South, with the election of Barack Obama. And it wasn't two years later that people sat in a haze, looking at returns that pretty much wiped out many of those changes that favored Democrats, including Black Democrats," Crayton says. "It could be that this is a start of a generational shift, but it might not be. And if you're thinking about how rules ought to be crafted, I think the cautious approach is always the preferable approach in making certain that rights under the worst conditions are protected as opposed to the most rosy scenarios."

Crayton says current redistricting rules, shaped by the Supreme Court's decisions in Callais and other cases, set up the potential dismantling of more opportunity districts for Black and other racial minority voters in future years. This past year's redistricting rush was limited partly because the Callais ruling came too late for many states to redraw their maps in time for the midterms.

"I think people who are looking at the recent Supreme Court decision around Callais and how it would be implemented always understood that the greatest reaction to that would not be in 2026, but would be 2028, 2030 and onward," says Dedrick Asante-Muhammad, president of the Joint Center for Political and Economic Studies, which tracks Black representation in Congress.

And that reaction is likely to reach beyond Congress, adds Janai Nelson, president and director-counsel of the Legal Defense Fund, which represents a group of Black Louisiana voters in the Callais case.

"When we look at state legislatures, when we look at city councils, when we look at school board maps and we look at any other elected office that relies on districts, those have largely been untouched by Callais up until now," Nelson says. "But as we enter a new state legislative process where these maps may be redrawn, then we might see devastating impact in electability by Black voters across the country and especially in the South."

There's no redistricting, however, for U.S. senators. And on that side of Capitol Hill, Black representation is on the rise. The blue wave of the midterms has lifted the number of Black senators in the new Congress to a historic high of seven. They include Illinois Lt. Gov. Juliana Stratton and fellow Democrat Scott Colom, who is set to become the first Black senator from Mississippi since Reconstruction.

#5920. How Scott Colom defied the odds to go from district attorney to Mississippi's Democratic US Senator

Scott Colom speaks as he gives an update on state of Senate race against Cindy Hyde-Smith November 4, 2026 in Jackson, Mississippi.  Rogelio V. Solis/Getty Images North America hide caption

JACKSON, Miss. — When Scott Colom, a local district attorney, announced his candidacy late last year, it seemed like a long shot in a ruby red state.

But by October of this year, a month before votes came pouring in for him, he was confident.

"We didn't have hundreds of million dollars to come in to spend – we did it going door to door, festival to festival, earning support," Colom said at a canvassing event on the Mississippi coast. "We're gonna shock a lot of people, but I'm not gonna be shocked. The people of Mississippi are behind this campaign: they understand Mississippi matters."

Colom narrowly defeated Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith, according to a race call by The Associated Press on Wednesday evening, marking one of the most stunning upsets of the midterm elections this year.

Colom won by less than five thousand votes, with a small fraction of the vote still left to be counted.

While he swept areas of Mississippi that have traditionally leaned left, like Hinds County, home to Jackson, the state capital, he also picked up places that have traditionally voted Republican, like Forrest County, which President Trump won in 2024 with about 58 percent of the vote.

Colom has been the district attorney for the state's 16th judicial district since 2016. That includes his hometown of Columbus, Mississippi, in Lowndes County – an area that's also got a right-leaning voter base.

Colom ran on accessible healthcare, saving rural hospitals and affordability. He said that working in a Republican-majority area informed the need for bipartisanship in tackling those issues.

"I believed that the majority of the Mississippians love our state more than they hate the Democratic party or love the Republican party," Colom said. "They want their kids to have good schools. ... they want to be able to make sure that young people, if they want to stay in Mississippi, can have a job. They want the hospitals to stay open."

While Colom isn't surprised that his message resonated this year with voters, he faced obstacles when a federal judicial nomination stalled in the U.S. Senate in 2023, when then-President Joe Biden nominated him for a federal judgeship.

Mississippi's Sen. Roger Wicker, a Republican, indicated he would support his nomination.

The state's Junior Senator Cindy Hyde-Smith blocked him. She cited donations from billionaire philanthropist George Soros around Colom's run for DA, which in 2023, Colom said were to an independent PAC – not his campaign.

She also said he opposed legislation to protect female athletes in a statement provided to the Mississippi Free Press at the time.

In a letter to Hyde-Smith, Colom said he didn't know what she was referring to and noted that he'd signed a statement saying he wouldn't use law enforcement resources to criminalize doctors who offer gender affirming care to transgender youth.

He also stated in the letter that if he was confirmed as a judge, any of his personal views would be irrelevant to his "daily work." And he asked the senator  to reconsider – she didn't.

Three years later, she was defending her seat from him. She was confident, on election night, that she'd beat him, telling her supporters, "I blocked Scott Colom from being a federal judge, and you blocked him from being U.S. senator."

She instead lost and on Wednesday night, she issued a statement conceding the race, saying she respects the decision of Mississippi voters.

Colom recognized that attention from national Democratic leaders, and his party's campaign funds, came late this year, but he noted that it was the attention his campaign received from voters within the state that made the difference.

"They understand the power of their vote in ways that we really haven't seen in a long period of time, because if you look at these margins, certainly everything that everybody did made a difference," Colom told reporters in Jackson just days before the election was called.

Being elected as a Democrat is no small feat either. The last time Mississippi sent a Democrat to the Senate was 1982 and an incumbent hasn't been defeated since 1942.

Colom now also holds the distinction of being the state's first Black senator since Reconstruction.

It's something he's proud of – but not the laurels he's resting on.

"My job is to be a senator that makes it easier for the next African American that has the work ethic, the vision, the values, and they want to be United States senator, make it easier for them," Colom said. "I am a person that is continuing to show up in the community and take the tough questions and explain my, you know, live my values. And if I do that, it makes it easier for the next person to be judged by their character and not their skin color."

While his victory is historic, his campaign has also made waves – especially on social media. In an interview with WLOX, Hyde-Smith responded to questions about beef affordability by suggesting folks pick a different protein.

Colom hit back, with a campaign ad, suggesting folks pick another senator instead.

Eventually, the rest of the country started listening. About two weeks before the election, the PAC associated with Senate Minority Leader Chuck Schumer donated $1.2 million for advertising.

And, several big name Democrats also ended up joining Colom on the campaign trail: former Vice President Kamala Harris, former Transportation Secretary Pete Buttigieg, former President Bill Clinton and U.S. Senator Raphael Warnock of Georgia, to name a few.

Colom traveled the whole state – from the coast to the capital, up to the Tennessee border. While in the Mississippi Delta, he said what he wants to do while in the Senate all goes back to his home of Columbus.

"I've always been committed to trying to make my community better, trying to make my city better, trying to make my state better, and that's what running for United States Senate is all about for me," Colom said. "It's a continuation of a service that runs in my family and I was raised [by] my community and my church to give back and that's why I'm doing it in this moment."

Tiara Jackson of Mississippi Public Broadcasting contributed to this report.

#5913. Colom wins Mississippi Senate race. And, White House warns Dems over future probes

Democrat Scott Colom has won the U.S. Senate seat from Mississippi, defeating Republican Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith by just a few thousand votes. Colom will be the first Black Senator from the state in nearly 150 years. His win expands the Democratic majority in the new Congress to 55 seats. The Alaska race is still undecided.

Scott Colom speaks with attendees at an event held at the Two Mississippi Museums in Jackson, Miss. on Aug. 1, 2026. Sarah Warnock/AP hide caption

The White House is warning Democrats that their planned investigations into administration officials could cross the line into "blatant criminality." White House Counsel Will Scharf said in a letter to Rep. Robert Garcia, who is expected to lead the House Oversight Committee next year, that the administration is monitoring "abusive and capricious threats" from Democrats. The letter concludes by saying that "the administration will not be extorted and will not be cowed by unlawful process, motivated by animus, pettiness, hatred or any other unlawful motive to intimidate, penalize or interfere with the lawful functioning of government." Garcia said Democrats will not be deterred by threats from the Department of Justice or Trump's lawyers.

Russia and Ukraine have agreed to a significant prisoner swap after two days of U.S.-mediated talks in Abu Dhabi. Trump's envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff are now in Moscow today to meet with Russian President Vladimir Putin and attempt to make more headway to end the war in Ukraine.

Abdul El-Sayed sits down for an NPR Newsmakers interview. Bronson Arcuri/NPR hide caption

Abdul El-Sayed has made history as the first Muslim elected to the U.S. Senate. The 42-year-old progressive from Michigan is already calling for a new direction for his party and urging President Trump to collaborate on actionable solutions. El-Sayed said that voters have temporarily entrusted Democrats with power, but warns that they risk losing that trust if they don't act boldly. As the senator-elect comes into the new role, he is advocating for new Democratic leadership in Congress, an end to the war in Iran, the passage of "Medicare for All" and the abolishment of ICE. While he acknowledges the challenge Senate Democrats face with a majority that's a few seats shy of being filibuster-proof, he remains open to working with his new Republican colleagues. In conversation with Morning Edition's Leila Fadel, El-Sayed reflects on the recent elections, the future of the Democratic Party and the need for impactful legislation in the upcoming years.

Watch or listen to the interview or read the article about their discussion.

Ford Motor vehicles are displayed for sale at the Leif Johnson Ford dealership on June 30 in Austin, Texas. Brandon Bell/Getty Images hide caption

The average price for a new vehicle in the U.S. has hit a record high of $51,109, according to Kelley Blue Book. This figure is about $500 above the previous record set in December 2025. For years, car prices have remained high, primarily due to supply chain issues stemming from the pandemic. But there's some good news: While that new high sets a record, it only represents a 2.1% increase over the past year. This means the price is rising more slowly. #5897. New York program funds $13 million in energy storage advancement projects

New York State’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program has awarded nearly $13 million to 15 projects to support product development and demonstration of scalable long duration and other advanced battery energy storage solutions.

“Investing in next-generation energy storage technologies is critical to building a flexible and resilient grid that can make the most of our diverse energy resources,” said Doreen M. Harris, NYSERDA president and CEO. “These projects will advance promising solutions that can deliver power when it is needed most, strengthen grid performance and help drive down costs as we build a more affordable and reliable energy system for New York consumers.”

NYSERDA’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program awards projects that develop and demonstrate innovative long duration energy storage (LDES) and other under-utilized, alternatives to traditional lithium-ion technologies. The awarded technologies include new designs for scalable LDES solutions that are over 10 hours in duration at rated power and will address cost, performance, and integration challenges such as grid congestion, hosting capacity constraints and lithium-ion siting limitations.

Public Service Commission Chair Rory Christian said, “Storage is poised to play an increasingly critical role in supporting a reliable, cost-effective, flexible, and lower emissions system as more clean energy resources are integrated into the grid, as well as enhance the efficiency of the state’s existing resources. NYSERDA’s dedication of these funds originating from innovation and research funding overseen by the Commission positions New York’s consumers to yield the promising long-term benefits expected by more complex and scalable applications of these technologies.”

All contracts are awarded to companies located in New York, partnered with New York companies or for development and demonstrations in the state. Today’s announcement supports NYSERDA’s Innovation and Research (I&R) program, which invests over $96 million per year to attract world-class energy innovators, reduce risk for private investors and remove barriers to clean energy adoption in New York State, leveraging $15 in additional investment for every $1 spent. NYSERDA has partnered with over 900 companies that have helped make more than 300 products commercially available for consumers, businesses and utilities.

Power Generation and Storage Innovation funding for comes from the state’s Clean Energy Fund.

#5882. ECA Power completes community solar project in town seeking revitalization

Employee-owned solar and energy storage developer ECA Power, Luminace, ComEd, construction partner Miller Bros., and local community leaders celebrated the energization of the 7.4-MWDC Rockford Community Energy Initiative (RCEI), a community solar project in Cherry Valley, Illinois.

“The Illinois General Assembly has spent the past several years passing legislation to encourage and incentivize renewable energy projects like this one,” said State Sen. Steve Stadelman. “It’s always exciting to work on legislation and then see that work translate into real development on the ground.”

“Northern Illinois is becoming a national leader in community solar, and projects like this are helping bring the benefits of clean energy to more families and businesses,” said Gil Quiniones, president and CEO of ComEd. “We’re proud to work with ECA Power and Luminace to connect projects like this to the grid, and build a power system that can support continued clean energy growth across our region.”

RCEI was built in a state-designated R3 area, bringing new investment to a community targeted for revitalization. As part of its community benefit agreement with the Village of Cherry Valley, ECA Power contributed $150,000 to support the buildout, operation and maintenance of an EV charging station.

“At ECA Power, we believe the success of a project should be measured not only by the energy it produces, but by the lasting value it creates for the surrounding community,” said Todd Fryatt, founder and CEO of ECA Power. “The Rockford Community Energy Initiative reflects that commitment by expanding access to affordable, locally generated energy while bringing meaningful investment to the region. From supporting local revitalization to our partnership with the Village of Cherry Valley, we’re proud to deliver a project that will benefit this community for years to come.”

#5879. REDD — Picking Up Our New Tesla Model Y

REDD, the name for our new Tesla Model Y (Standard Range ’26 build, ’27 model), was inspired by the movie of the same name – Retired, Extremely Dangerous Drivers (the wife and I, of course). As mentioned previously, there is nothing wrong with our beloved 2019 Model 3. It is our bodies that are aging and less able to get in and out of our beloved little sporty Tess. So it was with mixed feelings that we arrived at the Tesla delivery centre to pick up the new car.

As always, it was a hive of activity, with deliveries taking place all around us. Quite a contrast to 7 years ago when Tesla was operating out of one cramped delivery spot near the centre of Brisbane, and the handover took less than 3 minutes. This time there were plenty of sales representatives spending their time and making sure we knew what we were doing.

On arrival, we walked past a line consisting of ICE cars being surrendered as trade-ins for an electric. As we left, a car carrier was unloading half a dozen new Model Ys. All in all, a smooth operation. While Majella was getting her phone synced and lots of information from the sales rep, I had a wander around.

The delivery centre is divided in two by a screen. On our side of the screen, I could see at least 60 new cars anxiously awaiting their new owners — like puppies at the pound, I thought. I wasn’t allowed behind the curtain where cars were being detailed and prepped. I commented to our sales rep that there didn’t seem to be many Model 3s. He told me that they had just received a Model Y shipment; the 3s were on their way. Asked about what lay behind the curtain in Tesla wonderland, he said that there were hundreds of cars “back there.”

Majella drove REDD home and I drove Tess. I am having mixed feelings. I get attached to my cars; it was difficult to sell George (my ‘64 Wolseley) and Ruby (my Hyundai Accent). But they were old and tired and their time had come. Tess, at 180,000+ kilometres still feels young. It’s me that’s getting old. The letting go is being made easier because we are selling Tess to our youngest daughter. So, we will still be in contact with the car and watch the milestones as she grows.

As we sipped our celebratory champagne, I mused at the difference between buying the Model Y in 2026 and the Model 3 in 2019. Going from the Hyundai to the Tesla was a massive leap in both design and technology — the screen, the regen braking. Our Tesla was the only one in our suburb, and there was no high-speed charging. Electric vehicles were seen as a “soon to pass” fad on the way to hydrogen cars, and a stupid decision in a country as big as Australia. In 2019, less than one percent of new cars sold had a plug, Tesla was still on a deathwatch. We had taken a risk — would it work out?

It certainly has! Owning Tess and dealing with FUD gave me a cause to champion. I started exploring this new world and writing about it — driving long trips and proving that an EV can do what it has to. CleanTechnica published my ramblings — which have now reached over 1,000 articles. People contacted me and shared their stories of private ownership. CEOs of new startups (like recently introduced Thermal Dawn) gave me interviews and insights. This might sound weird, but moving from the Model 3 to the Model Y is nowhere near as exciting. Yes, I will continue to write, but the motoring world has changed. In Australia, the Model Y has been the highest selling passenger car of all drivetrains for the past few months. In October, it even beat the refreshed Toyota RAV4. My northern Brisbane suburb is full of Tesla Model Ys. The crossover has become common.

Motoring journalists are learning slowly to accept the new kids on the block, and most of their reviews seem to indicate a modicum of tolerance and truth. Of course, we still have vested interests proclaiming that Chinese cars are destroying the Australian car industry. Read: dealerships can’t sell ICE cars and aren’t making enough money. I wonder sometimes what there will be left write about as plugins take more and more of the market, and “real” motoring journalists write the articles I normally write. Relax, I am sure there will be room for all of us for some time. Especially in the USA!

Calls continue for more charging infrastructure, which is fair enough for highway and tourist travel, but seems to totally ignore home charging. “I live in a remote town and there is nowhere to charge my car!” wails one. I could scream if he would hear me: plug it into your house power socket, you moron!

Tess sits in the carport plugged into our home battery. Majella has taken REDD to her gem club. I am sure that instead of their normal lapidary, faceting, and jewellery making, they will be exploring REDD. Four other members of the club have now got Teslas or BYDs. One of them commented that they want a Model Y for Christmas — not sure if his wife will agree.

REDD will also be the guest of honour at our upcoming Coffee, Cake, and EV morning tea in a few days’ time.

Once we have done the roadworthy and registration transfer, Tess will be off to her new home. We will get to see her at family gatherings and when we pick up the grandkidlets from school. Majella is busy buying the accessories — mats, ceiling screen (no, the new heavy tint doesn’t do much in the Australian sun), rim protectors, etc. REDD came with front and back floor mats. She is buying all-weather mats for the frunk and trunk. The dog may even get to sit in the car! Shock! Horror! And we are slowly noticing subtle differences, like the reduction in noise driving on the highway — REDD has better seals and double-glazed windows.

We may have lost some of the apps that came free with Tess (Spotify, Karaoke, connectivity), but we have gained cooling seats and a heated steering wheel. And, of course, Hardware 4, which will give us access to FSD (Supervised) for our long trips. Speaking of longer trips, the range of our Model 3 (about 300 km with some degradation) pales beside the 460 km range of the LFP batteries in REDD. LFP batteries also mean less anxiety about charging to 100% when needed. REDD is longer, wider, and taller, but, thank goodness, still fits in our garage.

Majella is missing Enhanced Autopilot, for which we paid AU$5,000 just a few years ago. The Model Y’s cruise control is not as convenient. Stay tuned for updates as we enter the next leg of our electric journey.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5875. BMW Pitches Free EV Charging And You Ain’t Seen Nothing Yet

EV sales in the US are beginning to tick up again, and BMW is determined to grab a slice of the action. The German automaker has begun offering a free EV charging package to anyone who purchases a 2027 BMW i5, BMW i7, or BMW iX3. The package includes $500 in charging credits, which seems like small potatoes compared to the cost of a BMW. Still, it’s better than nothing, and it positions the company to compete in a rapidly evolving landscape that includes free charging hours.

The “free hours” trend has already emerged in the US, where Texas (of course!) is an epicenter of activity. The idea is to smooth out peak weekday demand spikes and take advantage of spare capacity on the grid by offering ratepayers an incentive to use more electricity during nighttime hours, with the leading utility Constellation being a representative example.

“When you sign up for a free night electricity plan, a smart meter monitors your energy usage by time of day,” Constellation explains. The company offers a “Free Power Hours” plan between midnight and 4 a.m. every night.

“Constellation also offers Discounted Nights & Fair Days, with discounted electricity supply rates from 10 p.m. to 6 a.m.,” the company adds.

Constellation notes that standard service charges and other fees apply. They also advise that the free plan is not a good fit for households with heavy electricity use during the day, when higher rates apply.

Still, access to EV charging at a minimal cost is a tantalizing incentive for some households. Constellation currently offers its “FreeCharge” plan between midnight and 4 a.m. to ratepayers in Pennsylvania as well as Texas.

Against this backdrop, a mere $500 EV charging credit for a BMW is not much of an incentive, at least not in states where free hours are available. The monthly lease for the 2027 BMW i5, for example, clocks in at almost $750. Purchasing the car outright will set you back almost $70,000, including the destination fee.

Still, any incentive is better than no incentive, and the new offer provides BMW an opportunity to hitch its star to the free hours trend. “The new offer, available beginning November 5, 2026, is designed to make public charging simpler, more convenient and more affordable for BMW customers,” explains BMW.

“The package includes a $300 BMW Charging Network credit that can be used at participating charging providers1, as well as a separate $200 credit for charging at IONNA locations,” the company elaborates, with IONNA referring to a consortium of EV makers (including BMW) focused on improving the driver experience at charging stations.

In addition to the $200 IONNA credit, BMW is also offering a one-year IONNA membership plan that provides for a 25% charging discount.

For the record, the BMW Charging Network includes more than 180,000 charging stations compatible with the company’s My BMW app.

Over and above the EV charging package, BMW is also among the electrification stakeholders that did not throw in the towel after last year’s EV sales crash. Despite the premature demise of the federal EV tax credit on September 30 of 2025, the number of public EV charging stations in the US has continued to increase, and charging networks have become more sophisticated and convenient.

In addition to the hookup with IONNA, for example, the BMW Charging Network includes Shell Recharge, Electrify America, ChargePoint, and EVgo among others.

“The company also has invested in Plug & Charge, which simplifies the charging experience; and ChargeScape, a collaborative effort to help manage the flow of energy between EVs and the power grid,” BMW added in a press statement last week.

ChargeScape represents another indication that Trump’s war against EVs was over before it started, tax credits or not.

ChargeScape launched three years ago as a joint venture between BMW, Ford, and the US branch of Honda. “Benefiting both EV customers and the electric utility industry in the U.S. and Canada, ChargeScape will unlock entirely new value that EVs can provide to the electric grid, while enabling EV customers to earn financial benefits through a variety of managed charging and energy-sharing services never before possible with traditional gasoline-powered vehicles,” the three automakers enthused in a press statement dated December 29, 2023.

“ChargeScape’s platform will give electric utilities access to EV battery energy across a wide pool of EVs. Participating EV customers will have the potential to earn financial benefits by charging at ‘grid-friendly’ times through flexible and managed schedules,” the partners elaborated.

“The development of this project comes at a time when electric vehicle sales and infrastructure growth are ramping up quickly,” the partners added.

That was certainly an optimistic take on the future of EV sales in the US, but that was December of 2023. Just about one year later, then-candidate Donald Trump sailed to victory on Election Day 2024, winning a second turn around the Oval Office with a plan to stop the vehicle electrification trend in its tracks.

Still, ChargeScape soldiered on. By September of 2024 the alliance was ready for launch, to be joined by Nissan just one month later. ChargeScape also added the startup Rivian to its rolls in June of this year, with an option to add bidirectional EV charging. Other automakers, including Tesla, also use the platform.

The bidirectional charging element is where things get particularly interesting. The technology emerged in the early 2000’s, enabling EV drivers to power their home from their car battery, or send electricity to the grid. Unlocking the mass market potential of bidirectional charging is a next-level challenge, and the free hours trend could help accelerate uptake. With free hours EV charging, drivers can store electricity at minimal cost during those periods, and discharge it to trim the cost of electricity for their homes during periods when higher electricity rates apply.

Ford is particularly excited about the potential for bidirectional charging to scale up. The company’s forthcoming Ford Fathom electric pickup truck comes with an internal inverter that converts the DC charge from the battery into an AC current for home use. The built-in inverter eliminates the cost of the external, wall mounted inverter otherwise needed for bidirectional charging at home.

Photo: BMW is sweetening the EV charging pot with a $500 charging credit on its 2027 i5, i7, and iX3 electric cars in the US (courtesy of BMW).

#5873. Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5872. A New $1.5 Billion Wind Farm Will Play In Peoria

It ain’t over ’til it’s over, but the proposed $1.5 billion, 500-megawatt Four Creeks wind farm in Peoria County, Illinois has just passed a key step in the approval process. The new milestone is yet another indication that the domestic wind industry will continue to play a key role in the US energy transition, regardless of the topsy-turvy energy policy articulated by US President Donald Trump.

If you’re somewhat shocked to hear word of a new wind farm proposal in the US, join the club. The President has spent considerable time, energy, and taxpayer dollars in a Herculean effort to keep new wind turbines from cropping up around the US under his watch. However, his efforts have earned only mixed success so far.

Offshore wind turbines keep slipping through the cracks, even though virtually every offshore wind farm in the US is subject to federal authority. Opportunities for wind power also persist on land, where the President’s direct authority is limited to projects on federal property.

Trump attempted to work around that obstacle in August of 2025, when he weaponized an otherwise routine wind project review process that applies to projects on non-federal property. The change in policy threw at least 155 onshore wind projects totaling 45 gigawatts into limbo, prompting a lawsuit from a coalition of clean energy organizations and wind energy developers.

In July of this year, Attorneys General in 19 states intervened in support of the lawsuit. The participants included Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, the District of Columbia, Illinois, Maine, Massachusetts, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Nevada, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, Oregon, Rhode Island, and Washington State.

Illinois Attorney General Kwame Raoul, who co-led the intervention, notes that federal law does require wind developers to submit proposals for wind turbines more than 200 feet tall to the Federal Aviation Administration, which then refers the project to DoD to check for any conflicts with military operations and related activities. Raoul further notes that the normal process involves collaborating on mitigation measures to resolve any conflicts.

That all ground to a halt in August of 2025, when DoD dropped the ball on its part of the process. “Officials ceased countersigning mitigation agreements, stopped sending completed agreements to developers for signature, and delayed or halted communications with developers about mitigation,” Raoul explained in a press statement in July.

The abrupt change in policy impacted at least nine wind projects in Illinois alone. According to Raoul’s office the nine projects represent $4.3 billion in private investment, or more.

Separately, on June 16 of this year the same coalition of state Attorneys General won the final resolution of a series of legal challenges to an Executive Order issued in January of 2025, in which Trump ordered a halt to all federal approvals for wind projects.

The DoD lawsuit is heading down the same path, at least so far. On August 6, a federal judge with the US District Court for the District of Oregon ordered DoD to resume its normal wind turbine review process while the case continues to wind its way through the courts.

DoD has been slow-walking the process since then, reportedly approving just 11 projects since August. However, the US branch of the global clean energy developer Repsol already saw the writing on the wall. On August 14, just one week after the Oregon decision came down, Repsol Renewables began submitting paperwork for the proposed Four Creeks Wind project.

“Repsol Renewables has identified northwest Peoria County as suitable for wind development because of a robust wind resource, its proximity to the existing electric transmission system with available capacity, compatibility with existing land use, and minimal anticipated environmental impacts,” Repsol summarized, with existing land use referring to provisions in the Peoria County Code that permit commercial wind farms in certain agricultural districts.

Although the wind farm involves a total of 27,000 acres on private land, each of the project’s 113 turbines will only account for less than one acre. The bulk of the land is dedicated to required setbacks as well as adequate spacing between the turbines.

Things are moving along at a relatively fast clip so far. On November 6, Peoria public radio station WCBU reported that the land use committee of the Peoria County Board recommended the Four Creeks project by a 6-3 vote. The project almost appeared to be dead in September when the Zoning Board of Appeals issued a 4-1 denial, but the Zoning Board also voted to include conditions for consideration by the full Peoria County Board at its meeting on November 5. The County Board approved the Four Creeks proposal on a split vote of 12-5 alongside a list of 36 restrictions mainly governing construction and operations.

Wind fans, don’t break out the champagne just yet. Repsol still has to apply for construction permits, and the entire process could take up to five years or so before shovels meet ground.

If and when Four Creeks materializes, Repsol is prepared to pour more than $193 million into the coffers of the Princeville, Brimfield, Williamsfield, and Stark County School Districts, with an additional $44.7 million in total property tax revenues for Peoria County and associated jurisdictions.

Repsol also draws attention to more than $69.4 million in total property tax revenues earmarked for libraries, fire districts, Illinois Central College, local road authorities, and the Greater Metro Airport Authority among other community resources.

Local property owners will also rake in millions of dollars in escalating lease payments.

As for objections to the new wind farm, WCBU reporter Joe Deacon highlighted remarks by Peoria County Board member Terry Ruhland, who acknowledged those who spoke against the  project at a public meeting.

“I don’t know how many people are in this room; I won’t even guess. But there is at least 179,000 people in Peoria County who are not in this room,” Rhuland said.

“Of that 179 [thousand], for every school kid who deserves the best education we can give them, and for every family that’s struggling to pay their utility bills, I’m going to vote yes,” Ruhland added.

Perhaps Ruhland was also thinking of Laborers Local 165, which has been working with Repsol for more than a year on developing a local workforce for the project. In a hearing before the Zoning Board in September, union representative Dane Simpson asserted that each wind turbine would account for approximately 3,000 hours of work.

Image: The global energy developer Repsol is on track to construct a 500-megawatt wind farm in Peoria County, Illinois, regardless of last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy (screenshot, courtesy of Repsol).

#5871. Tesla Hasn’t Had A Successful New Product Launch Since JB Straubel Stepped Down As CTO

As has been widely reported, Tesla is about to end Cybertruck production. The Cybertruck has been a shocking failure. I’m sure there are more detailed analyses out there somewhere, but this short response on X from Ben McCombe that Tina Casey included in her article on the matter sums it up very well:

“Installed tooling to build 125k Cybertrucks a year.

“Made just $100m in gross profits from sales.

That’s pretty wild. A net project loss of around $2 billion. Now, I have to admit that I haven’t fact checked the numbers here, but what we know for certain is that Tesla planned to produce a lot of Cybertrucks per year and ended up producing a small fraction of that target. In fact, the annual sales target for the Cybertruck was higher than 125,000 — it was 250,000, with a goal of getting up to 500,000!

The Cybertruck was hyped up as having more reservations than any vehicle in history and was supposedly going to shatter records and transform the market. I warned people on many occasions that the reservation total was not a real reservation total and was definitely not a reliable indicator of demand because a) a reservation only required a $100 refundable deposit, and b) a ton of people made reservations after Elon Musk said that you could lock in the price of lifetime Full Self Driving when making a reservation (so, you’d only have to pay $6,000 for FSD no matter how much the cost of it went up in the following years). Anyway, the Cybertruck had other issues as well when it finally arrived. It cost about twice as much as it was supposed to cost and it had about half as much range as it was supposed to have. In other words, it was not even close to the vehicle it was supposed to be — its range per dollar cost was about ¼ what it was supposed to be.

The Cybertruck was supposed to be the next big thing after the Tesla Model Y. It was supposed to disrupt the gigantic US pickup truck market. The Model S was a superb and widely acclaimed car, if a bit more expensive than the mass market could afford. The Model S did in fact compete superbly well with the leaders in its segment, the BMW 7 Series and Mercedes S-Class. The Tesla Model X brought a similar offering in a larger package with some fun extra elements (falcon-wing doors, self-presenting doors, a third row). The Tesla Model 3 then completely shook the automotive world as a tremendous market phenomenon and perhaps the biggest product launch in history (in terms of total revenue). The Tesla Model Y was basically the Model 3 in a more popular crossover package and eventually became a much higher selling vehicle. In fact, it became the highest selling vehicle model of any kind in multiple years. Then JB Straubel stepped down as CTO.

Due to their EV passions, JB Straubel and Elon Musk were introduced to each other and had lunch together to talk about electric airplanes (Elon’s dream) and then electric cars (because JB talked more sense into Elon). Then the two of them were introduced to Marc Tarpenning and Martin Eberhard, who had founded Tesla Motors and just barely got started on the idea. After a while, Tarpenning and Eberhard left the company, and Musk and Straubel truly led its development and growth into an automotive superpower (as briefly described above). Since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, however, if one thinks about it, it’s hard to identify any successful product launch from the company or much significant improvement at all.

Straubel left his CTO position in July 2019. The Model Y was unveiled in March 2019 and was about ⅔ the same as the Model 3. It would seem that getting the Model Y designed and prepared for launch was the last big project Straubel worked on. (Note that the Semi was unveiled in November 2017 but was long delayed due to battery supply, according to Elon Musk.) Since the Model Y, what significant product launch have we seen from Tesla? There was the Cybertruck, as we’ve already discussed. That was unveiled in November 2019. When you think about the timing now, did Straubel disagree strongly with Musk on the Cybertruck and decide it was time to move on when Musk pushed forward with his idea for an electric truck? It took another 4+ years for Tesla to start delivering the Cybertruck, in contrast to Model Y deliveries starting in January 2020, less than a year after the model was unveiled. More recently, the Cybercab has been developed, unveiled, and is now in production. A consumer car smaller and cheaper than the Model 3 was never developed and unveiled — just a stripped down, decontented Model 3 — and there basically haven’t been any other product unveilings since Straubel left his role as CTO. Elon Musk has published a couple of somewhat scattered, rambling “master plans,” but we haven’t gotten any significant new products aside from the Cybertruck and the just arriving Cybercab.

Even looking over on the energy storage side of things, the Tesla Megapack was launched in July 2019, the month Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO. Did Straubel just decide he had done enough once he had finished the Megapack launch and gotten the Model Y essentially ready for market? Did he have conflicts with Musk on where to take the company from there?

Yes, there’s been incremental product development and refreshed versions of Tesla cars and energy storage systems. There are still plenty of engineers working on improving things. However, there doesn’t seem to be the same leadership in new product development, and there also isn’t the same practical, moderating element that Straubel seemingly provided to Musk’s more wild, grandiose, sci-fi inspired dreams and ideas.

Perhaps this is all misunderstanding the roles of the two men to some extent. Perhaps things would not be much different if Straubel had stayed in the CTO role and had the same influence he had before leaving. He is still on the board of directors. (What that actually means is unclear, though.) Perhaps Tesla’s success and business would still be primarily based on the Model 3 and Model Y, without new transformative products. Or perhaps we would have had a more normal pickup truck from Tesla, an electric van, better batteries, a better market rollout of the Semi, and other products. Perhaps the Cybertruck, an epic product failure, never would have been shown to the public and hyped up unrealistically with specs and figures disconnected from what was going to be possible. Perhaps we’d have a Model C (affordable, compact Tesla) rather than a Cybercab. Perhaps Tesla would be a very different company.

Who knows? We don’t know the ins and outs of JB Straubel and Elon Musk’s relationship, or how much Straubel actually led Tesla in his CTO role. We don’t know how much Straubel tamped down Musk’s worst instincts or guided product development. We still really don’t even know why Straubel left. According to Tesla, it was so that he could focus more time on Redwood Materials, but why wasn’t that business within the umbrella of Tesla in the first place? Vertical integration has long been a key element of the company. Who knows? There’s a lot we don’t know and never will. However, what we do know is that since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, we haven’t seen a significant, successful new product launch at the company. We’ll have to wait a bit longer to see if the Cybercab is one, and whether that sets a new, positive course for the future of the company or not.

#5870. A review of ‘Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV’

By Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza | November 10, 2026

Was today’s modern solar industry inspired by off-grid California-living, marijuana-growing, Grateful Dead-loving hippies? That’s the main question explored in “Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV,” a new documentary that recently premiered in Grass Valley, California.

The entertaining documentary traces the history, colorful characters, off-grid homeowners, solar educators and the entrepreneurs who embraced solar photovoltaic technology for vastly different reasons.

Shot on a shoestring budget with volunteers, “Solar Roots” may not have the polished production of a Hollywood documentary, but it is an untold story about the people and early solar and technology that led to the modern solar industry.

Since 2009, when I first started in the solar industry, I had heard jokes and anecdotes about the marijuana-growing hippies who sparked the residential solar industry. Until “Solar Roots,” no one had ever set up a camera to talk to those early solar pioneers and document why rural America became a hub of off-grid solar innovation.

Enter Jeff Spies, a longtime solar veteran and the documentary’s executive producer. In 2015, Spies organized a weekend party for the off-grid solar pioneers of Northern California, where he started his career in the mid-2000s. After hearing so many colorful stories about why these rural homesteaders bought $14/watt solar panels and started new solar installation and distribution businesses, Spies enlisted another solar industry veteran, Jason Vetterli, to travel to Southern California, Arizona, Oregon, Colorado and Wisconsin to capture these unique and personal stories on video.

This isn’t the first documentary about the solar industry or its challenges, and to be clear, Vetterli and Spies’ narrative is not about creating “the solar biz.” Truly, it’s about a particular tribe of solar early adopters. Through interviews, we learn about the 1970s, ’80s and ’90s counterculture movement and the people who stumbled into solar businesses out of necessity and good timing.

Listening to these stories, you quickly understand that none of these solar pioneers intended to get into the solar business to make millions or solve climate change. Their real motivations were tied to maintaining their independence from cities while having enough electricity to keep the lights on and the music playing while raising a family. Beyond the home applications, solar became a new water-pumping solution that supported the pioneers’ cash-only “agricultural” businesses.

Vetterli sets the film’s tone and theme with the first scene. Instead of fading in to a utility solar farm or suburban residential roofs, the film opens with a young hippie using a wire to connect a single 35-watt ARCO solar panel to a car battery that powers an old cassette boombox. He then inserts a Grateful Dead tape and presses play.

The Grateful Dead-inspired musical score, composed by Midnight North, a band whose members include another solar veteran, TJ Kanczuzewski, appropriately plays under the rest of the film’s narrative and interviews.

After a brief solar technology history lesson, Spies interviews the solar pioneers about the roots of their solar PV fascination, how they came to realize that solar could grow and impact their lives and their off-grid movement.

This realization wouldn’t have happened without the back-to-the-land movement. Buying land was inexpensive but getting a utility to build a power line to DIY houses in rural California cost millions. While living without electricity, we learn about their lives before and after PV.

Before PV, the pioneers had to jerry-rig their pickup trucks’ batteries to listen to music and use dangerous kerosene lanterns for light. Gasoline generators were loud, costly, required maintenance and were contrary to the quiet, remote lifestyle they wanted. After PV (and lead-acid batteries), the solar pioneers could enjoy light, music, small DC refrigerators for cold beer and fresh food, 12-volt TVs and other DC devices that were designed for camping.

After implementing solar PV into their own homesteads, the pioneers wanted to share their find with their neighbors. It was that desire to share the off-grid solar PV love that led to the early solar distributor businesses, publications and training centers — not a desire to make millions.

As businesses grew, cultures clashed. We hear the story of when corporate solar salespeople traveled to Humboldt County in three-piece suits to meet their best customers who are wearing T-shirts and jeans and carry the scent and cash of a “weedpreneur.”

While the majority of the film is about the people who wanted an off-grid lifestyle, Vetterli and Spies also interview the late 1990s PV code and policy pioneers. These solar people also wore suit, but they also saw grid-tied potential to make solar mainstream.

“Solar Roots” does a great job covering this off-grid to grid-tied transition, but the film only covers a fraction of the stories and people who inspired the multi-gigawatt and global solar industry that exists today, and that’s O.K. A single two-hour documentary could never cover all of the world’s solar pioneers, but we’re fortunate to have met these particular solar people. They may not have started the residential solar industry, but they certainly contributed to it.

Spies and Vetterli plan to release the entire documentary online in the coming months and have set up a mailing list at www.solar-roots.com for people to receive updates.

Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza is a former film and TV writer (Stargate SG-1, Dharma & Greg, The Dead Zone) and current chief solar public relations geek at UnThink Solar, a solar PR and communications firm.

Tor, Thank you for participating in the debut of Solar Roots – The Pioneers of PV. I was honored to have you at this historical event with many of the main characters of our story in attendance.

Tor, thank you for this review. As you know, I was at the Grass Valley premiere too, and the film is a delight. Its account of how solar cells, battery charging, and inverters developed is the best I have seen: clear, even-handed, and grounded in the people who made it happen, with phenomenal historical footage.

Having spent those years at ARCO Solar, I smiled at the opening scene with the 35-watt ARCO module, the car battery, and the Grateful Dead tape. The homesteaders in this film put those early modules to work, solved practical field problems, and showed what solar could make possible.

The path runs from the 12-volt appliances of those early days to today’s 800-volt DC data centers, and it is far from finished. I look forward to sharing the film with students when it is released online, so the next generation can see whose work they are building on.

Congratulations to Jeff Spies, Jason Vetterli, TJ Kanczuzewski, and everyone who helped preserve this history.

Charlie, It was wonderful seeing you at the debut of “Solar Roots – The Pioneers of PV”. I was deeply honored to hear you say our story was the most balanced accounting of the development of the PV industry. Thank you for supporting our project and for all your work in bringing this magical technology down from space and introducing it to regular folks all around the world.
