#5824. New BYD Seal 07 Illustrates Chinese EV Evolution

Last week, BYD introduced the next generation Seal 07 EV. This model replaces the original BYD Seal, which entered the market in late 2022 and put the brand on the map in the minds of many people around the world. While the new vehicle is important to BYD, it also illustrates many of the changes that have happened to the EVs and the EV market in China over the past few years.

The new Seal 07 grows to 5080 mm long by 1960 mm wide by 1495 mm tall. That compares to 4,800 mm long, 1,875 mm wide and 1,460 mm high for the previous Seal. That also makes it slightly larger than the late Tesla Model S.

Like many new BYD cars, the interior has become more premium and technologically advanced.  Interiors offer front zero gravity seats with heating, ventilation and massage. An onboard refrigerator is offered in the center console and a power sunshade in the roof. Speakers have proliferated. The screens have improved,  programmable buttons can be added, and voice interaction has improved. Basic versions come with BYD’s God’s Eye C with BYD covering autonomous parking, while higher trims offer LiDAR-based God’s Eye B with BYD adding coverage for city navigation.

Seal 07 models now come with DiSus-C adaptive damping on lower trim models and DiSus-M magnetorheological active dampers on higher trims. Suspension is double wishbones up front, multilink at the rear.

Of course, the new car offers BYD’s flash charging, capable of charging from 10-70% in 5 minutes and 10-97% in 9 minutes, while taking 12 minutes to go from 10-97% at -30 C with no preconditioning. 20,000 BYD flash charging stations are planned to be in operation by the end of the year, which will be more than all DC charging stations in the US combined. Meanwhile, the previous Seal took 26 minutes to go from 30-80%.

Looking at the closest version of the original Seal from 2022, battery capacity has dropped from 82.5 kW to 76.4 kW for the new model. Meanwhile, range increased to 775 km CLTC from 700 km in the old model due to greater efficiency. While efficiency may have once been a criticism of the Seal, it is now a highlight. Power is up from 230 kW to 245 kW (329 hp). Meanwhile, a curb weight of 1975 kg is lighter than the previous 700 km model (2055 kg), with increased use of UHSS in the structure.

Overall, it is a larger and more luxurious car than the vehicle it replaces, while losing a little weight. Performance, charging and efficiency have improved. Equipment levels are higher. Technology is more advanced. And then we get to affordability.

In 2022, the original Seal offered a 550 km range trim for 209,800 RMB ($31,424 USD at current exchange rates), but the 700 km range version, which is closest to the new model, started at 259,800 RMB ($38,913).

A little over 4 years later, the new Seal 07 EV starts at 149,900 RMB ($22,437 USD) with 775 km of range, more power and much faster charging. It costs 10,000 RMB ($1498) more for a version with God’s Eye C intelligent driving. For the top-of-the-line flagship with LiDAR-based God’s Eye B, the price is 184,900 RMB ($27,694 USD). In addition, there are launch discounts and perks. Of note, there is no AWD version of the new Seal 07, with that role filled by other BYD models in China.

For the default comparison, the standard RWD Model 3 is smaller (4720 mm long) slower, comes with less equipment and only 634 km of CLTC range (612 km with optional 19” wheels). Price starts at 225,500 RMB, although paint colors and interior options increase the price (included with the Seal). To get Enhanced Driver Assistance, which is roughly equivalent to God’s Eye C, you need to add on 32,000 RMB, while Tesla Driver Assist (similar to God’s Eye B) adds on 64,000 RMB ($9,586 USD). To get as close to a top spec Seal 07 in a standard Model 3, would take you to 307,500 ($46,057), but you would still be under-equipped with less range in a smaller, less powerful vehicle. The Long Range RWD would take you to a bit above the Seal 07 with 800 km of range on 19” wheels, even though that ternary  battery is recommended not to charge to 100% on a regular basis, unlike the LFP-based second generation Blade Battery in the BYD. The Long Range RWD Model 3 costs 331,500 RMB ($49,652 USD) with paint, 19” wheels and Driver Assist, but still lacks in equipment.

However, the Seal 06 (4870 mm) is a better match for the standard Model 3 but comes in at 119,900 to 159,900 RMB ($17,958 to $23,950 USD). There is also a no-compromise, larger (5150 mm) Seal 08 with more available range, power and amenities from 196,900 to 239,900 RMB ($29,492-$35,932 USD). And if you want something sportier, there is now the Fang Cheng Bao Formula S (5060 mm) at 189,900 to 229,900 RMB ($28,443-$34,434 USD). For something more luxurious, there is the Denza Z9S at 255,800 to 325,800 RMB ($38,313-$48,798 USD). None of those BYD models existed when the original Seal was launched. In addition, there are also Dynasty Han and Qin sedans from BYD for a little different flavor. And those are just the BEV models. There are also PHEV versions.

Looking beyond Tesla and BYD, the Seal 07 fits in the price gap between the Mona M03 and Next P7 at XPENG. It slots below the SU7 from Xiaomi. Below a Zeekr 7X or Nio ET5. At its current price, size and capability, the Seal 07 is in a good competitive niche between entry level sedans and more premium vehicles. If the Seal 07 had kept the original Seal’s higher price point, the competition would be much more challenging.

Compared to 2022, the new Seal 07 does not need to cover as many bases. This lets it better meet the needs of specific customers, while related models can be tailored to different customer needs. Undoubtedly, these different models will divide sales numbers, which may not be good for bragging rights. Analysis will become more challenging and may require combining sales of related models for relevant comparisons. But the increased choice is better for customers.

It may be a while before we have a clear picture of what impact this all has on sales. Chinese regulations that began earlier this year prevent manufacturers or dealers from selling below cost. This essentially prohibits the model-year-end clearance sales that are typical throughout much of the West. In addition, Chinese regulations stopped the practice of registering a vehicle and then exporting it as a zero-mile used car. In combination, it becomes much more difficult to manage inventory. As such, many models that are about to be replaced have their deliveries cut well in advance of the new model launch and have sales essentially evaporate. Then, it can take a while for the new models to ramp up production and deliveries to take hold. We may not have a good picture of BYD’s sedan sales in China until next year.

It also may be a while before we have global distribution plans or pricing for the new models. The three new Seal models are not yet offered outside of China. BYD has taken the approach of waiting to bring their latest models to export markets, often introducing models overseas as new that have already been discontinued in China. When they do offer current models, they tend to be at a significant premium (see Denza Z9GT). Meanwhile, companies like XPENG are introducing their latest models globally.

However, with intensifying competition from Chinese automakers in some export markets, BYD’s approach may change. Some flash charging cars are starting to reach export markets. The Seagull (ATTO 1) has recently been selling in Australia for $19,990 AUD ($13,752 USD), representing a significant reduction in export markup. We might see more advanced models at lower markups in markets where protectionism has not taken hold.

For comparison to the US, the least expensive car of any powertrain is the gasoline Kia K4 at $23,535, which is more than the base Seal 07 ($22,437). The least expensive EV is the soon-to-be-discontinued Chevy Bolt at $28,995, which is more than the most expensive Seal 07 ($27,694). Meanwhile, the least expensive EV sedan in the US is once again the Tesla Model 3 at $38,630 (including destination and fees, which are included in Chinese prices). A number of other EV models were canceled.

In China, EV choices have expanded, cars have become more capable, and prices have come down over the past four years. Meanwhile, in the US, cars remain unaffordable and EV choices have diminished with aging products.

Of note, the original BYD Seal had already attained regulatory approval from Transport Canada for import prior to Biden and Trudeau implementing prohibitive 100%+ tariffs on Chinese cars. Due to regulatory harmonization between the two countries, that meant it would have also been approved to enter the US. The US EV market could have looked dramatically different now, even with the rollback of consumer subsidies. With free trade, we could be looking at an EV growth in market share, expansion of options and improvement in affordability similar to China, even if not to the same extent.

Overall, the new Seal 07 is a strong entrant into a competitive segment in a very competitive market. It might be a challenge to choose it over one of the many other excellent options from BYD and its competitors in China. However, having too many excellent, affordable EV options to choose from is a good challenge for customers to have.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5823. Most generators prepared for extreme cold, but risks remain: NERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Despite improvements to cold-weather operations, the grid “continues to face serious reliability risks during the winter period,” NERC President and CEO Jim Robb said.

Almost all U.S. power generators are capable of operating in extreme cold, with a relative few facing known constraints that could keep them from reinforcing their ability to produce electricity during winter storms, the North American Electric Reliability Corp. said Thursday.

“Industry is rising to the challenge of protecting the grid during periods of extreme cold,” NERC President and CEO Jim Robb said in a statement.

The reliability watchdog last month filed its “Cold Weather Data Annual Report” with the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, tallying the industry’s progress in implementing new winter weatherization requirements. About 94% of the bulk power system’s total capacity could be called on to operate below 32 degrees, thus NERC requires those units to implement protective measures. Relatively few units experienced reliability issues requiring corrective action plans, NERC said.

Units constituting 4.3% of capacity are implementing corrective action plans to address reliability issues, and 60% of those plans will be completed before this upcoming winter, NERC said. “A small percentage of units,” about 7.4% of the total capacity, “declared a constraint that precluded them from implementing corrective actions.”

The findings “highlight measurable improvements in the electric industry’s cold weather preparedness, while underscoring the need for continued vigilance to address winter reliability risks,” NERC said.

The risk from extended corrective action plans and cold weather constraints “is limited,” NERC said. “Based on the data reported this year, 99% of the total net winter capacity in the continental U.S. responded that they were able to operate” at calculated extreme cold weather temperatures.

Generator owners reported a total net winter capacity of 1,154,231 MW in the continental United States. That’s a 7% increase over the winter 2024-2025 reporting period, NERC said. Owners reported cold weather constraints for 633 units comprising 85,691 MW, with known limitations of wind accounting for 71% of units reporting a constraint.

“Coupled with our recent joint report with FERC on winter system performance, it’s clear that improved preparation is making a difference with no significant system disruptions over the past winter,” Robb said. “While that is good news, the bulk power system continues to face serious reliability risks during the winter period.”

Milder temperatures in some regions could provide relief this winter as well. The El Niño weather pattern is likely to result in warmer-than-average temperatures in the northern U.S., according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. Wetter-than-average conditions are likely across the southern U.S., from California to Florida, and along the East Coast, NOAA said.

Cold weather grid operating standards have been a priority for FERC and NERC since Winter Storm Uri in 2021, when about 4.5 million Texans lost power because power plants were unable to run.

PJM Interconnection, the grid operator for 13 states and the District of Columbia, on Thursday said it expects to have 182,426 MW of operational capacity to serve a forecasted peak electricity demand of approximately 147,500 MW, plus a forecast average of approximately 3,200 MW of electricity exports to neighboring systems.

That peak, driven by demand growth in the region, would set a new winter record if reached, the grid operator said. PJM’s ability to reliably serve winter demand “will depend on continued preparation and strong generator performance,” the grid operator said. “This is especially important in areas where transmission constraints limit the ability to deliver electricity from other parts of the system,” particularly including some zones in Maryland, Virginia and the District of Columbia.

ReliabilityFirst, the regional entity which monitors reliability along most of the East Coast, including the PJM grid, has about 13% of its total net winter generating capacity under a cold weather constraint, NERC noted. About 12% of the total net winter capacity in the Midwest Reliability Organization region reported a generator cold weather constraint, according to the report. In the Northeast Power Coordinating Council territory, about 4% of the total net winter capacity reported a generator cold weather constraint.

"Our goal is to coordinate operations with the generation and transmission owners and proactively manage the system to perform at high levels when consumers need energy the most," Michael Bryson, PJM senior vice president of operations, said in a statement.

#5822. 5 Years In, Mustang Mach-E Battery Data Shows What Real-World Electric Vehicle Durability Looks Like

Five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E customers began adding something important to the Ford electric vehicle battery validation work: real-world ownership at scale.

As part of our quality process, design and validation engineering starts long before a vehicle reaches a customer. We model battery performance, run physical tests, expose packs to heat, cold, and charging profiles, and build in margin for demanding real-world conditions.

These rigorous quality tests are designed to help us understand how battery systems perform across a wide range of climates, charging behaviors, and driving conditions before customers get behind the wheel.

Then David Blenkle drove past 400,000 miles on the original battery in his 2022 Mustang Mach-E. He gave us a real-world example of what is possible — and begged the question of whether he’s an outlier or not.

His battery health has remained strong through unusually high-mileage use. His experience gave our team a chance to compare one exceptional ownership case with what we are seeing across the broader fleet.

The bigger question was if David’s experience reflected a broader pattern. So, we decided to look back at the Mustang Mach-E owners who started it all: The Class of 2021.

Over five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E vehicles — model year 2021 — reached customers. Those electric SUVs have been on the road, in customers’ hands, ever since.

We analyzed the battery state-of-health data for the entire fleet.

Here is what the Class of 2021 looks like at the five-year mark:

More than 36,000 vehicles. Two billion all-electric miles, across all 50 states and across Canada.

Our Class of 2021 has almost reached Neptune — not a practical comparison, but a useful way to understand the scale and distance.

The number I look at closely is the spread. Ninety-five percent of those vehicles fall between 87% and 97% health. Averages can hide variation, so that range matters.

In some cases, David has driven 300,000 miles more than many in our 2021 cohort. Then there are also the people who are road-warriors, like David.

More than 2,000 Class of 2021 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery, averaging better than 91% battery health. A smaller group of high-mileage drivers — more than 30 drivers past 200,000 miles — is averaging 88%.1

David’s mileage remains exceptional, but the broader Class of 2021 data shows he is part of a larger pattern of strong battery health across real-world use.

Long-term battery health remains one of the biggest questions for many people considering an electric vehicle, and real-world data like this helps make that conversation more concrete.

Two billion customer-driven miles is meaningful because it reflects how people use their vehicles — commuting, road-tripping, and running their everyday lives.

And it’s not just the 2021 Mustang Mach-E.

Aggregated across the full battery-electric lineup released over the last five years in the U.S. and Canada, the pattern holds: our packs are designed to last at least 10 years, and in the wild they’re maintaining, on average, greater than 90% health at 100,000 miles.2

The Class of 2021 is the cohort old enough, right now, to tell the most detailed version of that story.

While this story focuses on the 2021 Mustang Mach-E, the learning doesn’t stop there.

Ford uses field data from vehicles on the road to inform current and future electric vehicle programs such as the Universal Electric Vehicle platform and Fathom. This includes how we think about battery management, charging guidance, and long-term ownership confidence.

Battery chemistry is complicated, but a few simple everyday habits can help support long-term battery health:

Owners should not have to become battery chemistry experts to take good care of their electric vehicles.

The 2021 Mustang Mach-E vehicles in this data set use NCM battery chemistry, where frequent full charges are generally not needed for daily use. Some newer Ford EV batteries use LFP chemistry, which benefits from an occasional charge to 100% to help the battery management system stay calibrated.

Ford over time has implemented new features, like guidance in the Ford App, to help owners look after their batteries, specific to the vehicle and battery system they drive.

I have spent my career on the battery side of this business — cell chemistry, pack design, thermal management, and the systems that help a battery perform over time.

For an owner, the battery is central to the EV experience. It shapes confidence in every commute, road trip, and charging stop.

This is one of the most valuable feedback loops we have had at this scale: more than 36,000 vehicles, five-plus years, and billions of real-world miles.

The data is encouraging, and the learnings continue to inform how Ford approaches battery systems, software guidance, and future electric vehicle programs.

The Class of 2021 has added a real-world chapter to what our validation work was designed to understand. And we have many more electric miles ahead of us.

By Kevin Vander Laan, the Technical Leader of High-Voltage Battery Cells and Systems at Ford.

1. Battery state-of-health data is as of August 2026 for all 21MY Mustang Mach-E vehicles sold to U.S. and Canada retail and fleet customers, covering approximately 36,000 owners with an average ownership period of 5.2 years. Figures reflect usable battery capacity.

2. Based on a 2026 manufacturer field data study of high-voltage battery state of health on Ford battery electric vehicles.

#5821. Permitting bill advances in Senate, but election results cast doubt on its future

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Senate passage is possible, but enactment remains unlikely without a clear House strategy,” Jefferies analysts said in a research note.

The cloture vote to advance the bill received 73 yesses, 25 nos and two abstentions. Among the nos were Sen. Cory Booker, D-N.J.; Sen. Adam Schiff, D-Calif.; Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vermont; and Sen. Jeff Merkley, D-Ore.

According to Politico, Schiff said he was voting no following President Donald Trump’s comment Monday that he supports the bill but “will not approve wind turbines,” adding, “The bill hasn’t been fully negotiated.”

“He has not changed course in any way when it comes to renewable energy, and any wiggle room left in the bill to prioritize oil and gas over renewables he’ll make full use of,” Schiff told Politico.

In a Monday release, Merkley said he “strongly supports” the parts of the bill that seek to increase transmission and clean energy, and ensure fair cost allocation for data centers, but said the legislation has “features that merit careful analysis” and “robust consideration of the bill should be a priority in the new Congress.”

“At the demand of the fossil fuel titans, this bill also accelerates the extraction and burning of fossil fuels,” Merkley said. “This bill could make it easier to build more data centers powered by ‘behind the meter’ natural gas plants. And it impacts the sovereignty and rights of Tribes.”

Sen. Catherine Cortez Masto, D-Nev., voted to advance the bill but warned in a Monday release that tribes in Nevada still have concerns regarding the legislation’s changes to the National Historic Preservation Act, and if those aren’t struck from the bill, she will not vote for final passage.

“The manager’s amendment to BAAJA does not adequately address the serious concerns raised by Tribal Nations across the country,” a coalition of tribal organizations said in a Sunday release.

In a research note sent Monday following the vote, Jefferies analysts said the manager’s amendment “largely preserves” the bill’s original framework “but is incrementally better for transmission, offshore-wind projects, nuclear, and non-RTO utilities, and worse for large BTM gas configurations, incumbent RTO utilities and projects relying on strong permit certainty.”

“Senate passage is possible, but enactment remains unlikely without a clear House strategy,” the note said. “The irony is that the bipartisan Senate coalition has made meaningful progress just as the opportunity to convert that progress into law is narrowing. House Democratic leaders argue that the Senate bill falls short, while Republican support for accepting the Senate product also appears uncertain.”

The analysts said the bill could see more revisions to reflect House concerns.

Some of those concerns were voiced by Rep. Adelita Grijalva, D-Ariz, who was reelected last week.

“Just one week after voters resoundingly rejected the Trump agenda, Congress should not be rushing to pass a bill that gives him even more unilateral authority to bypass our laws,” she said in a Monday statement. “I am a hard NO on BAAJA.”

On Sunday, the Sierra Club said in a release that the manager’s amendment was released amid a “continued rush to hold a vote during the lame duck session,” adding that while the amendment “partially addressed” the group’s previously outlined concerns, “major substantive issues remain.”

Former FERC Chairman Mark Christie said in a Monday LinkedIn post that the manager’s amendment had not “at all” addressed two of his three major concerns with the legislation.

“The ‘public interest’ standard in the bill remains a blank check for FERC to override the states and order trillions of dollars in new transmission built to fit the policies of a President or more likely, special interest lobbying of RTOs,” Christie said.

He added that the legislation doesn’t give states authority over cost allocation for large transmission projects “that will cost their consumers tons of money.”

“The transmission provisions are a DC lobbyists' dream list,” Christie said. “Massive expansion of federal power that will cost consumers literally trillions of dollars.”

Paul Cicio, CEO of the trade association Industrial Energy Consumers of America and chair of the Electricity Transmission Competition Coalition, criticized the bill in a Monday LinkedIn post, saying it “fails to unleash transmission competition and deliver cost savings to ratepayers.”

“Without competition, a monopoly utility has zero incentive to reduce costs,” he said. “Section 2105 codifies existing exceptions to competition that are used by the monopoly utilities to evade competition.”

Cicio also objected to a change the manager’s amendment made to Section 2104 of the bill, saying it “now gives FERC the ability to dismiss a state's request to investigate actions by a utility to evade competition and actions that would harm consumers.”

Editor’s note: This story has been updated to include additional reporting from Politico about the bill’s ongoing negotiations.

#5820. Connecticut solar projects directly support Veterans Affairs campus

The Connecticut Department of Veterans Affairs (DVA), Connecticut Green Bank and their partners recently marked the completion of solar installations serving DVA’s main campus and skilled nursing facility in Rocky Hill, Connecticut.

The projects will deliver renewable electricity and reduce energy costs at the campus. The Green Bank developed the projects through its Solar Marketplace Assistance Program Plus (Solar MkAP+). Kearsarge Energy has partnered with Connecticut Green Bank as the long-term owners of these projects as well as nine others and will also oversee operations and maintenance. Greenskies Clean Energy was the engineering, procurement and construction contractor for the two DVA installations.

“To Gov. Lamont and your administration, thank you for having the vision for the GreenerGov CT initiative, providing reliable, environmentally friendly and cost-saving energy for residents and businesses in Connecticut, which is one of your top priorities, along with countless others, enabling Connecticut’s 3.6 million residents to thrive,” said Ron Welch, a retired brigadier general with the U.S. Army, and commissioner of the DVA. “Our team at the Connecticut Department of Veterans Affairs thanks you for your full support of this solar field project and for all you do through your significant partnerships to sustain and improve the environment and the quality of life for Connecticut residents and beyond.”

The two systems have a combined capacity of 1.404 MWDC and are projected to generate approximately 1.84 million kWh of electricity annually. The systems are projected to offset 85% of electricity use for the skilled nursing facility and 35% of the electricity use at the main campus building.

“This project brings together two important commitments: advancing sustainability in state government and supporting the people who have served our nation,” said Michelle Gilman, commissioner of the Department of Administrative Services. “DAS is proud to support this project and the broader Greener Gov CT partnership.”

The two arrays built at the Rocky Hill campus are 604.8 kWDC and 799.2 kWDC.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5819. More than 300 Solar & Storage Companies Urge Congress to Pass Bipartisan Permitting Reform to Accelerate Clean Energy Deployment, Lower Costs

WASHINGTON D.C. — More than 300 solar and storage companies are urging Congress to pass the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), S. 5653, warning that outdated federal permitting processes and grid infrastructure bottlenecks are delaying critical energy projects and driving up electricity costs.

In a letter to congressional leaders, the companies call on lawmakers to quickly send the bipartisan legislation to the President’s desk. The signatories note that solar and storage account for 70% of new electricity capacity added to the grid thus far in 2026, while $120 billion in clean energy investment remains caught in permitting delays.

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA).

BAAJA would modernize the federal permitting system by ensuring fair treatment for all technologies, protecting lawfully issued permits from politically motivated delays, establishing predictable timelines for federal reviews and judicial proceedings, modernizing transmission planning, and expanding distributed energy resources’ access to wholesale electricity markets.

New SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.

The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern. Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.

About SEIA®: The Solar Energy Industries Association® (SEIA) is leading the transformation to a clean energy economy. SEIA works with its 1,200 member companies and other strategic partners to fight for policies that create jobs in every community and shape fair market rules that promote competition and the growth of reliable, low-cost solar power. Founded in 1974, SEIA is the national trade association for the solar and solar + storage industries, building a comprehensive vision for the Solar+ Decade through research, education and advocacy. Visit SEIA online at www.seia.org and follow @SEIA on Twitter, LinkedIn, and Instagram.

#5818. Are data centers really paying their share? AES Indiana’s Google deal highlights a thorny question

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While local grid infrastructure projects are fairly easy to trace back to a specific data center, regional system upgrades raise more complex questions about who benefits and cost allocation.

The Indiana Utility Regulatory Commission recently approved AES Indiana’s plan to serve a Google data center in a contested case that highlights the complex challenges regulators and utilities face in allocating system costs for large computational loads.

According to the Oct. 7 order, AES will acquire or build new generation, storage and grid infrastructure for a 390-MW data center planned for Monrovia, a town Southwest of Indianapolis, at an estimated project cost of about $737 million.

The new generation, storage and transmission assets included in the Monrovia Project will go into the utility’s rate base.

AES says its electric service agreement with Google is designed to ensure the revenues will be sufficient to “reimburse at least 100%” of the project’s incremental direct costs. But the contract itself is heavily redacted, and an estimated $74 million in network upgrades has been explicitly allocated to all customers as a reliability need, prompting criticism from consumer advocates that the arrangement is shifting costs onto other ratepayers.

Indiana state law requires large load customers to reimburse at least 80% of project costs “reasonably allocable” to them.

Claire Wayner, a senior associate at the clean energy nonprofit RMI, said in an interview that while local projects are fairly easy to trace back to a specific data center, broader regional system upgrades raise more complex questions about who benefits and how costs are allocated across jurisdictions.

“Maybe they're upgrading a part of the grid that they were going to have to upgrade anyway because it's aging infrastructure,” Wayner said. “If that's the case, they should have to demonstrate that, and then maybe cross socialization makes sense.”

But, she continued, “If that upgrade was not going to be built without the data center, without benefits modeling or just a demonstration of the benefits to the rest of the zone, it's difficult to make the claim that all ratepayers in that zone should pay for it.”

AES Indiana will serve Google’s data center from its portfolio of shared generation and battery assets. The new generation and storage assets built to meet the additional demand will be owned through a subsidiary by the utility and go into its rate base.

In testimony to the commission, a utility representative said the transmission infrastructure included in the Monrovia Project falls into three basic categories: interconnection infrastructure for the generation and storage; “direct connection” infrastructure for connecting the data center to the system; and network upgrades necessary “primarily” to serve Google “but also to meet other customer demand or energy needs.”

Google will pay about $32 million upfront for the direct connection, but $73.8 million in network upgrades will be included in AES Indiana’s transmission rate base and recovered through future rates from all customers, including residential ones.

Commissioners approved the proposal 5-0, saying in their order that “although the Customer may be the impetus for the network upgrades being made, this does not preclude the upgrades from providing system-wide benefits.”

“Network upgrades, as the name implies, are upgrades or additions to the transmission network intended to provide reliability, resiliency, and stability to the system at large,” it said. “Just as the Customer will be allocated a portion of the existing network transmission infrastructure, it is reasonable for existing customers to also be allocated a portion of the network upgrades.”

The Midcontinent Independent System Operator approved some of the network upgrades, which include a new substation, citing reliability and load growth.

Although the order says the portfolio proposed in AES’ filing is for 390 MW of new load with 460 MW of new 4-hour storage and 130 MW of solar generation, the substation planned for the project is designed for 1,200 MW of load, creating opportunity for future expansion at the site.

The generation and storage resources consist of one 200-MW and one 130-MW battery energy storage system, called Foundry Works and Hardy Hills, respectively, as well as a 130-MW solar facility paired with a 130-MW BESS called Crosstrack. Their cost is redacted in the case filings.

Ben Inskeep, program director at the Citizens Action Coalition, a consumer advocacy group that intervened in the case, sees the deal as running counter to President Donald Trump’s Ratepayer Protection Pledge signed earlier this year by tech companies and utilities, including Google and AES Indiana.

“These network upgrades are specifically driven by the Google data center,” Inskeep told Utility Dive in an interview. “Instead of making the cost causer pay, AES Indiana has decided to spread the costs out to other ratepayers, and our commission has unfortunately approved that.”

Ben Gavelek, a spokesperson for the commission, declined to comment on the deal. Google did not respond to requests for comment.

Claire Rice, a spokesperson for AES Indiana, said in an email that although the transmission assets are being allocated through the normal cost-of-service process, Google’s rate includes a “separate incremental-cost test designed to recover the incremental costs required to serve Google, including the network upgrades.”

“Cost allocation and cost recovery are two different concepts,” she added.

Many of the exact terms of the service agreement, including monthly energy and demand charges and riders, are redacted in the public record. Parts of other contracts underpinning the deal are also confidential.

The assets will be included in future base rate case filings. Until those new rates are established, the utility plans to recover those costs through its Environmental Cost Recovery and netted with contract revenues.

The commission’s order says AES Indiana’s accounting and ratemaking proposals enable it to “match” the project costs with revenue collected through Google’s electric service agreement and “flow” those contributions “back to existing and future customers” through riders.

AES says using this arrangement, Google will pay $300 million toward fixed costs through the end of the contract period at the minimum demand level, including 100% of the annual revenue requirement associated with the network upgrades. The utility expects Google to pay approximately $770 million at the expected level of demand.

Company representatives told regulators that its cost allocation framework is not based on “direct cost method” but instead uses a modified “slice of system” approach.

Another point of contention is that the tech giant’s rates will be based on a 90% load factor, which AES says is high compared to all other customers, but critics said amounts to a 10% discount.

The commission’s order said using the 90% estimated load factor “recognizes the Customer’s substantial and continuous utilization of the system capacity without assuming that the Customer’s maximum demand occurs exactly when the system peaks each month.”

Even though the network upgrade costs are not allocated to Google alone, AES Indiana Vice President of Commercial Patrick Maguire said in an interview that revenue from the company will “more than offset” residential customers’ portion.

“Customers’ rates are not impacted by those transmission investments at all,” Maguire said.

State regulators approved a $71 million rate hike for AES customers in June, attracting the ire of Gov. Mike Braun and setting off a chain reaction of turnover at the commission, which voted in September to rehear the case, the Indiana Capital Chronicle reported. The first of two increases has already taken effect, with another scheduled for January, despite the rehearing.

The utility’s next rate case is scheduled for 2029. That is also when AES plans to share a full cost-of-service study, cost allocation and rate design principles associated with the Monrovia Project.

Inskeep said it’s not clear if there will be a net savings for Indiana utility customers from Google’s contract with AES when factoring in other costs, such as congestion pricing, rising labor and equipment costs, new generation and potentially more network upgrades.

“We really do need some legislative reforms to increase transparency and establish better protections for ratepayers,” Inskeep said.

In the meantime, the grid operator is undergoing reforms to address the rapid scale-up of large load data center additions and associated transmission upgrades.

Jonathan Neal, a spokesperson for MISO, said in an email that the grid operator has undertaken an Expedited Project Review and Expedited Resource Addition Study to try and bring generation and transmission online more quickly.

MISO is also “working with our stakeholders” to make improvements around cost transparency, speed to partial power, a non-firm service option and market operations and processes.

Many of these reforms respond directly to concerns noted by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission when it issued Show Cause Orders to six grid operators, including MISO, directing them to overhaul their processes for interconnecting large loads.

“MISO is committed to addressing these challenges efficiently, reliably, and fairly,” Neal said.

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

The donation, which advocates called “problematic” and “convenient” for the company, comes to light as federal regulators chosen by President Donald Trump consider whether to allow NextEra to merge with Dominion Energy.

In North America, data centers are boosting the long-term investment case for energy storage and renewables as gas turbine backlogs and rising fuel volatility drive up peaking costs, according to a note from Wood Mackenzie.

#5817. Australian Plugin Vehicle Market Steady in October — Update

… with a spotlight on the Great Wall Motor ORA 5 rising star.

CleanTechnica readers were probably hoping for the surge to keep surging Down Under. I was. However, it appears to have remained steady. Not the flash in the pan petrolheads were hoping for at least. In September 2026, 108,778 passenger vehicles were sold, including all drivetrains. See the charts here.

Battery electric vehicles (BEVs) achieved a market share of 24.2% (26,287 units), outselling petrol-only and diesel-only vehicles. Add in 10,072 plugin hybrid (PHEV) sales and you get a plugin penetration rate of approximately 34%. PHEV sales are the fastest growing segment in Australia — increasing 122% year on year. For those who care, 17,843 HEVs sold in October (a YoY increase of 20%). I am looking forward to peak HEV, followed by peak PHEV, as we have seen in more mature Scandinavian markets.

Australia’s EV penetration rate (including BEV and PHEV) has hovered in the low to mid 30% for the last few months. Not sure what the future holds, but even just maintaining this rate for a few months will make a difference. Nothing beats lived experience. My neighbours are now asking about how much it costs to fill up. One came for coffee yesterday and stayed to go for a drive in our new Model Y. He was very impressed with the tech, the finish, and above all the running costs. He confided that he had just filled up his wife’s Honda Civic — AU$82 — and his Isuzu D Max — AU$121.

On a recent shopping expedition, we counted 10 electric cars in the car park, out of 50 vehicles. Not just Tesla and BYD, but also Zeekr, Geely, the GWM ORA 5, and a Deepal. Battery electric vehicles are making their presence felt. But not all my friends can see them. They can spot Tesla and BYD vehicles, but they tell me they don’t see any other EVs on the road. Yes, we have tried to educate them, but it is not working just yet. “See if it has an exhaust pipe. Does it have a sealed front?”

One car that I am seeing in ever increasing numbers is the refreshed Great Wall Motors ORA — now in an SUV body and a much cheaper price. When it launched back in 2023, I expected great things (see our review here). Sadly, the Funky Cat (as it is known in China) did not live up to expectations. It was the cheapest EV in Australia at the time (AU$46,000) but couldn’t compete with the BYD Dolphin and the MG 4. Now, relaunched as an SUV and with a more appropriate price (AU$34,000 — scratch that, just got an email that knocks another grand off the price), I am seeing them everywhere — 6 on the highway just yesterday.

So, on to the best-selling BEVs on the Australian market in October 2026:

Pretty much the same as last month. Obviously, BYD was the best-selling brand, closely followed by Tesla. BYD has six cars among the top twenty BEVs. There are no cars from Korean, Japanese, or European carmakers in the top ten BEV numbers. When all powertrains are considered, BYD is the second highest selling brand behind Toyota in the Australian market. There seems to be some talk in the auto news about Tesla and BYD being considered “legacy” brands — who would have thought?

Expect big things from Geely as it aggressively expands throughout Australia. Geely recently announced that it has opened 100 dealerships over the past 18 months. Its EX5 sales figures are putting pressure on the competition in the medium-SUV-segment Mazda CX-5, Hyundai Tucson, Mitsubishi Outlander, and Nissan X-Trail.

Utes still dominate the Australian market. When a compelling BEV offering becomes available, the top ten may change again. I was excited to see the KGM Musso BEV ute, but it has failed to gain traction. Only 269 have sold in the past few months. The Toyota BEV Hilux only has 14 units in the country — likely to be demonstrators. PHEV utes seem to be doing much better, led by the BYD Shark. Over 15,000 have sold so far this year.

Now for some newsy titbits: Dealers for established brands are calling for a temporary ban on new car brands (read Chinese made cars) entering Australia, as they cannot compete. The boss of the Australian Automotive Dealer Association says the industry has reached a crisis point, with the last quarter delivering the first loss since the global financial crisis. Chinese brands now own 36% of new car sales.

“This is a clear warning that the commercial settings underpinning our industry are becoming unsustainable,” Mr Voortman said. He speculates on the possible risks to consumers: “… the influx of new brands also puts consumers at risk, as it can lead to rapid depreciation of vehicles, a lag in parts supply, longer repair times, and orphaned vehicles should those companies choose to leave the country.”

Though, what we are seeing in Australia is that legacy brands, mainly European, are the ones leaving the country.

The FUD continues as misconceptions and outdated information are rolled out as facts. If you want to revisit these arguments, check this out. Trigger warning: some of you may wish to yell at the screen. The author twists himself in knots trying to make the argument that the wrong Australians are buying electric vehicles. I would contend that the right people are buying EVs — those commuting from the outer suburbs of our sprawling cities. Data shows that it is these people who are behind the EV sales boom we are currently experiencing, and these are the ones benefitting most from not paying inflated petrol costs caused by Iran’s unwillingness to admit defeat and grovel at the feet of the orange one. Please excuse my sarcasm. All of this is documented in the 10th annual Electric Vehicle Council Report. We reviewed this report here.

Despite the desperate attempt to promulgate fear of “these here new-fangled doodads” — the creation of doubt and uncertainly — every month shows how intelligent the average consumer is as they seek a better, electrified, and brighter future.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5816. Community engagement is key to on-site battery storage, experts say

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Building operators are turning to batteries to combat rising energy prices and add resiliency, but safety concerns mean these projects require thoughtful planning, Greenbuild panelists say.

Building owners are turning to battery energy storage systems to lower energy costs, add resilience against grid disruptions and support decarbonization. Despite momentum, their adoption faces a number of persistent obstacles, said panelists at the Greenbuild conference last month.

Nationwide, nearly 58 gigawatt-hours of on-site battery storage capacity was installed in 2025, the largest single year of new battery capacity on record, according to a report by the Urban Land Institute and UL Standards & Engagement, drawing on data from the American Clean Power Association. Looking ahead, the sector is expected to install 500 GWh of battery storage between 2026 and 2032, the report says.

Notwithstanding the success of these models, several barriers are keeping the market from growing more quickly, according Jill Maltby-Abbott, senior environmental and sustainability standards engineer at UL Standards & Engagement, a panelist who contributed to the ULI and UL report.

Location-specific challenges. Even when a project makes economic sense, it can be stopped by interconnection and permitting woes, unclear approvals, shifting local requirements and unfamiliarity with the technology, according to Maltby-Abbott. Even where interconnection and permitting processes exist, commercial-scale storage projects can take years to deliver, the report said.

Long and unpredictable timelines can cause projects to accumulate soft costs — like time and money spent on community engagement or engineering fees — and be exposed to changing market conditions, incentive structures and regulatory requirements, adding to the financial risk of implementation, per the report.

Risk thresholds. Another key consideration is how municipalities view the risk and governance of these assets, Maltby-Abbott said. “How are [municipalities] defining the [risk] thresholds that they’ll accept? It’s very much on a per-project, per-owner basis, and this is a challenge that a lot of people experience with multiple emerging technologies.”

Concern over risk can lead to delays as local officials put up hurdles, Dean Apostoleris, practice leader of renewable energy development at Kimley-Horn, said at the conference. “What we see as a really big barrier in terms of penetration into new markets and, even in some situations, mature markets is resistance from the regulatory, zoning, land use and siting perspective of these batteries,” he said.

Even in really population-dense markets, where storage development has been a priority for quite some time, there has only been increased adoption of on-site battery energy storage in the past five or so years, according to Apostoleris. “That issue stems from a lack of education and lack of knowledge about what battery energy storage really is,” he said.

For example, New York state has established a mandate to procure 6 GW of battery energy storage by 2030, with Illinois and Massachusetts setting targets of 3 GW and 5,000 MW as well, respectively. But with those targets close to just three years away, New York only has 415 MW of battery storage installed, or 7% of its target, as of the presentation, Aposoleris said.

State mandates aim to spur battery adoption but development "comes down to the local and community level,” Apostoleris said. “And a lot of these communities have not yet had the bandwidth, the knowledge or the education to implement some of these regulations.”

“As a result, when you actually go to permit and construct BESS, ultimately the reviews and approvals are … preventing a lot of that mandate and that goal from being achieved,” he said.

Fire risk. Of all the risks, fire is one of the first localities worry about, said Kara Kokernak, senior director of the ULI Lewis Center for Sustainability in Real Estate. “That is something that became very apparent” when conducting research for the report, she said. “That was tagged in most of our conversations, but having fire safety teams part of that education can help ease those concerns.”

Because of this pushback, Apostoleris said, it can be much easier to implement these projects in jurisdictions that have already established BESS safety requirements and review processes, which provide more prescriptive guidance on how projects will be evaluated.

He points to the Massachusetts Energy Facilities Siting Board as a good example. It “allows a lot of developers to push through their projects in a very prescriptive manner that reduces some of the risk that often comes with uncertainty of reviews and approvals they might have at the state level,” Apostoleris said.

Even when requirements are stringent, this predictability can help project teams incorporate safety considerations earlier while supporting fire department preparedness and confidence in how potential incidents will be avoided or managed, the report says.

Community engagement, or lack thereof, can often delay, constrain or stop otherwise viable projects, the report says. For that reason, early engagement, before formal filings, is a “consistent recommendation from those who have successfully navigated community opposition,” the report says. “This gives developers the opportunity to understand specific concerns, explain safety and emergency response approaches, and adjust the project so that community input is reflected in project planning from the start.”

Get the free daily newsletter read by industry experts

The PJM Interconnection’s plan for curtailing large loads that don’t have matching new generation intrudes on state jurisdiction and appears to be discriminatory, the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission said.

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

#5815. A Tribute to Bunker Roy: The Man Who Turned Village Grandmothers into Solar Engineers

Sanjit “Bunker” Roy, who taught illiterate grandmothers to become solar engineers, passed away peacefully this morning at his home in Tilonia, Rajasthan, aged 81.[1] I first encountered his work not in India, but in the rain forests of Borneo.

It was October 2016, and as night fell, the surrounding Borneo tropical rain forest had come alive with noise, dominated by the trill of frogs and the rhythmic pulse of cicadas. I was about to embark on the Sabah study tour of the Emerging Leaders Dialogue Asia (ELDA). Over the next two weeks, we would visit timber plantations, mountain communities recovering from a deadly earthquake, and marine parks.

But undoubtedly the highlight for me was discovering the inspiring story of Barefoot College, which trains village grandmothers to be solar engineers. On our first evening, while enjoying a mini feast of banana fritters, pomelo, and langsat (a bit like lychees but more delicious), we were told about the college’s remarkable partnership with the Sabah Women Entrepreneurs and Professionals Association (SWEPA) in Borneo.

Barefoot College was the brainchild of Bunker Roy, and he was an unlikely revolutionary. Born into privilege in Bengal in 1945, he was educated at the Doon School and St Stephen’s College, Delhi—two of India’s most elite institutions—and was a national squash champion. A conventional career beckoned. Then, during the Bihar famine of the mid-1960s, he saw rural poverty firsthand, and his life changed course.[2] When he told his mother he wanted to live and work in a village, he later joked, she “went into a coma” and didn’t speak to him for a long time.[3]

In 1972, Roy settled in Tilonia, a dusty village in Rajasthan, and founded the Social Work and Research Centre (SWRC), which later became known as Barefoot College. Its rules turned conventional education on its head. Anyone with a university degree was disqualified from studying there, and no degrees were awarded. In 2011, Roy said that nobody at the college earned more than $100 a month. The campus itself was built in 1986 by a dozen local “barefoot architects” who could not read or write, and it went on to win an Aga Khan Award for Architecture. At the college’s night schools, children aged 6 to 14 elect their own prime minister and cabinet.[4]

Roy’s guiding conviction was simple: “There is no urban solution to a rural problem.”[5] He was awarded the Padma Shri, one of India’s highest civilian honors, in 1986, and named one of Time magazine’s 100 most influential people in 2010.[6] He is survived by his wife, Aruna Roy, the former civil servant who led the grassroots movement behind India’s Right to Information Act.[7]

The positive impact of his work has been immense. By the college’s own count at its 50th anniversary in 2022, it had trained 1,708 illiterate and semi-literate rural women from 96 countries as solar engineers, bringing light to more than a million people in over 75,000 households and saving about 45 million liters of kerosene. Its rainwater harvesting had captured some 70 billion liters and brought safe drinking water to more than 1,650 schools. More than 90,000 working children had studied in its night schools, and its barefoot health workers had treated over 300,000 patients.[8]

During our study tour, SWEPA arranged for us to meet one of Barefoot College’s beneficiaries, a so-called solar mama. She was part of the program that takes illiterate grandmothers from poor rural villages and, within six months, trains them at the college in India for their new job. They return to their villages to install and maintain full solar lighting systems. Roy deliberately chose grandmothers because they are rooted in their communities; men and younger people, he found, were more likely to take their new skills and leave for the city.[9] Because the women shared no common language, much of the training was done through sign language and hands-on practice.[10]

Malaysia’s first solar mama—very likely the woman I met that evening—was Tarihing Masanim, from Kampung Sonsogon Magandai, near Kota Marudu in Sabah. She completed her training in India in 2015 and returned to bring electricity to around 100 homes in her village.[11] Roy loved to tell stories like hers. One of his favorites was about a 55-year-old Afghan grandmother who electrified 200 houses and set an Afghan engineering department head straight on the difference between AC and DC.[12]

The story of the solar mamas captures many elements of what it means to harness the power of responsibility to create a more inclusive society and economy. So many barriers are being broken through, as these amazing women challenge stereotypes about age, gender, and rural capacity. Barefoot College shows that sustainable development—including adopting sustainable technologies—is an option for people living with low incomes in remote villages. The Barefoot model empowers people to be agents of their own destiny.

Bunker Roy stands out as an inspiring example of inclusive leadership. His impact, which is so strongly evidenced by the solar engineers program, is as much about restoring dignity to those who are traditionally excluded as it is about developing or transferring practical skills. Roy, who said he had “a very elitist, snobbish, expensive education in India” that “almost destroyed” him, explained:

“So we started the Barefoot College, and we redefined professionalism. Who is a professional? A professional is someone who has a combination of competence, confidence and belief. A water diviner is a professional. A traditional midwife is a professional. A traditional bone setter is a professional. These are professionals all over the world. You find them in any inaccessible village around the world. And we thought that these people should come into the mainstream and show that the knowledge and skills that they have is universal.”[13]

Roy ended that talk with a line often attributed to Mahatma Gandhi, whose principles guided his life’s work: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, and then you win.”[14] For the solar mamas of Barefoot College, and for the more than a million people whose homes they have lit, Bunker Roy won.

This article draws from content in Wayne Visser’s book Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

#5814. Roll-A-Rack debuting low-profile, manufactured-on-site solar racking

Roll-A-Rack, a developer of patented, on-site-manufactured solar racking systems, will introduce SunChord, its new multi-purpose ground-mounted system, at RE+ 2026 in Las Vegas.

SunChord is a low-profile solar racking designed to use less land than conventional fixed-tilt racking or trackers. It is composed of two components: gutter channels and clamp brackets that attach to solar panels. SunChord assembles using what Roll-A-Rack has dubbed its “snap-slide-secure” system.

“The next generation of solar technology needs to do more than produce clean electricity. It needs to make better use of land, minimize environmental impacts and create value beyond the energy generated,” said Don Scipione, founder of Roll-A-Rack. “SunChord reflects our vision of harmonizing sun and earth, combining solar energy production with rainwater harvesting and soil conservation in a system that can unlock new opportunities for solar development on land where conventional approaches fall short.”

Roll-A-Rack claims an array using SunChord can capture 95% of rain that falls it using its racking channels to redirect the water. The company sees potential for the racking in agrivoltaic applications, sites with land constraints, on brownfields and where ground disturbance should be limited.

SunChord is assembled on-site using a roll-forming device similar to producing roof-edge gutters. SunChord was developed with support from the U.S. Department of Energy’s Integrated Energy Systems Office and the USDA National Institute of Food and Agriculture. The company has two issued U.S. patents, a patent application pending, wind-tunnel ratings and UL 2703 certification.

Roll-A-Rack is planning to supply SunChord for up to five pilot projects. Interested EPCs and developers can contact the company at [email protected].

#5812. Trump admin ‘pauses’ leases for five offshore wind projects already under construction

The U.S. Dept. of the Interior has immediately paused the leases for all large-scale offshore wind projects under construction in the United States due to “national security risks identified by the Dept. of War in recently completed classified reports.”

“The prime duty of the United States government is to protect the American people,” said Secretary of the Interior Doug Burgum. “Today’s action addresses emerging national security risks, including the rapid evolution of the relevant adversary technologies, and the vulnerabilities created by large-scale offshore wind projects with proximity near our East Coast population centers. The Trump administration will always prioritize the security of the American people.”

The Dept. of the Interior claims that the blade movement and reflective towers create “radar interference called ‘clutter.'” Allegedly, this is a national security risk.

Dominion Energy released the following statement in response to the pause issued for the Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind project:

“The Coastal Virginia Offshore Wind Project (CVOW) is essential for American national security and meeting Virginia’s dramatically growing energy needs, the fastest growth in America. This growth is driven by the need to provide reliable power to many of America’s most important war fighting installations, the world’s largest warship manufacturer, and the largest concentration of data centers on the planet as well as the leading edge of the AI revolution.

Stopping CVOW for any length of time will threaten grid reliability for some of the nation’s most important war fighting, AI and civilian assets. It will also lead to energy inflation and threaten thousands of jobs.

CVOW is American-owned and benefits all of our Virginia customers. Our customers are paying for the project after a careful review of project costs and benefits by Virginia state regulators in 2022. These same state regulators, along with numerous federal agencies, oversee our cyber and physical security program, which is among the strongest in the energy industry.

The project has been more than 10 years in the works, involved close coordination with the military, and is located 27 to 44 miles offshore, so far offshore it does not raise visual impact concerns. The project’s two pilot turbines have been operating for five years without causing any impacts to national security.

CVOW enjoys bipartisan support and is within months of generating a massive 2,600 MW to support the fastest growing part of America’s energy grid. This growth serves the largest concentration of critical infrastructure in the world.

Virginia’s All-American, All-Of-The-Above-Energy Plan requires a range of power generation assets, including natural gas, advanced nuclear, and renewables. Virginia needs every electron we can get as our demand for electricity doubles. These electrons will power the data centers that will win the AI race, support our war fighters, and build the nuclear warships needed to maintain our maritime supremacy. Virginia’s grid needs addition of electrons, not subtraction.

We stand ready to do what is necessary to get these vital electrons flowing as quickly as possible.”

#5811. The AI boom could shift diesel generators from backup to everyday use

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Last week’s election results demand community-friendly data centers. Overlooking the role of diesel backup could create more pollution and noise for residents, write experts from Inlyte Energy.

Antonio Baclig is CEO of Inlyte Energy, which develops iron-sodium battery technology. Ben Kaun is Inlyte chief commercial officer and Jon Wellinghoff is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and a member of Inlyte’s advisory board.

In October, during what is supposed to be a slack season for the grid, when generators are taken offline for maintenance, a spell of unseasonable fall warmth prompted the U.S. Department of Energy, at PJM Interconnection’s request, to issue an emergency order to avoid blackouts. The event is yet another indication of the strain on grids from demand growth from data centers.

But the Oct. 13-16 event also reveals a crutch that is increasingly being leaned on during grid stress, which may be an unwelcome surprise for communities living next to data centers: the diesel generators that are installed for emergency backup. The DOE’s 202(c) order, consistent with similar orders begun this year around Winter Storm Fern, authorized PJM to direct backup generation at large loads such as data centers to run. Earlier this year, on July 2, as an intense summer heat dome settled over the Mid-Atlantic, PJM recorded its highest-ever unrestricted demand, reaching 168 GW. In Northern Virginia’s Data Center Alley, utility executives said they came within minutes of needing to call on the vast fleets of backup generators installed at data centers to keep the system supplied.

This is a remarkable development. For decades, those backup generators had a simple job: If the grid fails, keep the data center running. Now, as electricity demand grows faster than it has in decades and extreme weather increasingly tests the grid, the generators themselves are beginning to look like a potential source of grid capacity.

That may seem expedient. But this development is happening at the same time as the public backlash against data centers, seen in last week’s mid-term election results, is prompting states to impose requirements for data centers — in September alone, new rules on data centers were signed in Virginia, Massachusetts, Maryland and California. If policymakers and regulators do not pay attention to what is likely to happen during periods of grid strain, increased reliance on diesel may end up being the default for the U.S. grid.

The existence of huge fleets of behind-the-meter diesel generators is a new development brought on by the AI boom. A modern hyperscale data center is no longer simply another large electricity customer. Its electrical infrastructure can resemble that of a small city, with substations, private distribution networks, sophisticated power electronics and enormous amounts of backup generation. Because even a momentary interruption can be extraordinarily costly, data centers install enough backup capacity to keep operating through a major grid failure.

Diesel generators have been the default because they are proven, reliable and relatively compact. Diesel fuel is also extraordinarily energy-dense and can be stored on site. But the generators were designed to sit idle most of the time and operate only during emergencies.

Rapid load growth is changing that equation. New generation and transmission take years to permit and build, while enormous new data center loads can arrive much faster. During periods when the grid is short of capacity, thousands of idle diesel generators can therefore look like an irresistible resource.

Federal emergency authority has already been used to make such backup generation available during periods of severe grid stress. In January, DOE authorized grid operators to tap as much as 35 GW of idle backup generation at large facilities. Similar authority has been invoked 13 times this year, seven of those in PJM.

The danger is that an emergency exception becomes a routine operating model. Once policymakers and utilities begin counting on data-center diesel generators as a source of capacity, there will be less incentive to develop cleaner resources that can provide the same reliability services.

This extracts a local price. Diesel generators produce nitrous oxides, particulate matter and other pollutants. A study of Northern Virginia data centers found that operating their diesel generators up to permitted limits would increase the region’s total nitrous oxide emissions by 45%. The study also found that the census blocks most exposed to those emissions tend to have lower household incomes than other census blocks.

The noise is also disruptive. During a previous electrical emergency in Loudoun County, residents described the sound of data-center generators operating continuously as being like airplanes landing for more than 24 hours.

That may be tolerable as an emergency measure. It is a poor model for the everyday infrastructure of the electric grid.

Virginia is starting to ask the question: its order directs environmental regulators to review the cumulative impact of data center backup generation and to evaluate alternatives including on and offsite battery storage, load flexibility, fuel cells, and other non-diesel options. But will state and federal regulators, and data center companies themselves, take the next steps to prevent diesel generators from being the default solution?

This question is particularly important because data centers are being asked to become more flexible participants in the grid. Recent regulatory initiatives are creating pathways for large new loads to interconnect more quickly if they agree to reduce their demand when the system is short of capacity.

A data center cannot simply turn itself off. However, that does not mean it cannot be flexible. Many strategies are being developed to make data centers appear flexible to the grid, including throttling low-priority compute, shifting compute to other regions, and putting flexible resources such as batteries at the data center site, or nearby, connected front-of-the-meter or behind-the-meter at businesses and homes.

Batteries can reduce the data center’s demand on the grid during periods of extreme system stress. They can absorb electricity when supply is abundant and return it when electricity is scarce.

However, the lithium-ion batteries being installed today have short durations, for example two or four hours. For any longer period of outage or grid stress — and local congestion can create longer periods of stress than the system-wide peaks — if the data center still needs to be curtailed by the grid, where is that power going to come from?

Today, the default is the backup diesel generator, or in some cases natural gas generators. Therefore, once the battery runs out, the data center that is providing a grid resource instantly switches from doing so in a clean way with a battery to doing so in a dirty way with fossil fuels.

It is clear that data center flexibility can use the grid more efficiently, providing faster access to power at lower societal cost. However, there are many details to be worked out. As policymakers and regulators formulate and implement new data center rules, they should look carefully at how data center flexibility is likely to be met and only allow methods that are consistent with the overall goals of states. If they do not, the expediency of diesel generators could result in it being used more often, creating more pollution and noise for local communities.

Looking to the future, the most secure way to ensure that on-site fossil fuel generators are not used for data center flexibility is to not have them there at all. Why not just replace the diesel backup entirely with batteries, as recommended by the Better Data Center Project? A common objection is that the batteries do not provide enough backup duration.

One alternative is simple: long-duration energy storage, either on-site or proximate within the potentially congested area. A storage system capable of providing 24, 48, or even 72 hours of backup could give a data center a high level of resilience, while at the same time allowing the facility to strengthen the grid productively on an everyday basis.

And long-duration storage does not necessarily mean installing more lithium-ion batteries. New battery chemistries, and other clean backup technologies, are emerging that use more abundant materials, are designed for stationary rather than transportation applications, and can potentially offer different combinations of safety, cost, footprint and duration.

For data centers, footprint is particularly important. Land around a hyperscale facility is scarce. Technologies that can safely pack long durations of stored energy into a relatively small area could make it possible to replace diesel backup without requiring any more land. Additionally, LDES could be placed for the benefit of the data center at other consumer locations such as commercial and industrial sites.

The immediate objective should not be to pick a single winning technology today. It should be to make sure that data-center developers and regulators do not assume that diesel generators are the only practical answer, and that they work to incentivize and develop alternative technologies. So far, LDES has been framed as a front-of-meter bulk storage solution, and state incentives have only rewarded behind-the-meter short duration storage. There should be a signal to the data center or other customers that the resilience contribution of behind-the-meter LDES is valued for bulk system reliability and the elimination of local emissions. This could take the form of allowing behind-the-meter LDES to be accredited towards system capacity and state procurement mandates while taking into account the full effective load carrying value of their durations, as well as modifying existing behind-the-meter rebate and demand response incentives to reward longer durations rather than capping them at shorter duration.

Across the country, residents are increasingly questioning the impacts of data centers: their electricity consumption, water use, noise, and local pollution. Some of those impacts may be unavoidable. But many are the consequence of choices about how these facilities are designed.

Virginia and other states are beginning to define what a community-friendly data center looks like. The industry should meet that standard by building data centers that are clean and quiet stewards of their communities from the start, rather than locking yesterday’s infrastructure into tomorrow’s grid.
