#5793. Polestar Formula 2030 Concept Makes Us Hope Polestar Will Be Back In America By 2030

Chinese-owned Polestar was banned from selling cars in the U.S. back in June, and like a scorned ex showing you what you're missing after a breakup, it's now revealed one of the coolest designs in recent memory: the Formula 2030 concept car. Look at what they took from us.

It's got a low, wide, shooting brake shape. The big front door, little rear door setup is pure enthusiast bait, and the switchgear inside looks like it came straight off of professional audio equipment. The Formula 2030 isn't just a for-show concept car, either, because Polestar says it will indeed "point towards future design direction for the brand."

Specifically, "various design attributes" seen here will actually show up in the next-gen Polestar 2 and upcoming Polestar 7 compact SUV. The Formula 2030 is billed as a compact four-door sports car, not really fitting neatly into any existing segment. It sits lower than the Polestar 5 grand tourer, fits between the 2 compact sedan and 7 size-wise, and is probably closest to the Polestar 4 in spirit.

It already looks great at a glance, but things get way more interesting once you start opening stuff up. Both the hood and rear hatch open up clamshell-style, exposing the natural fiber composite chassis underneath and orange Öhlins dampers. The half-size rear doors are coach doors, giving the Formula no B-pillar and a huge opening when all doors are open.

Inside, a very curved instrument screen measuring 20 inches sits on top of the steering column while a 12.2-inch center touchscreen sits in the middle. That touchscreen can physically lower when the driver switches drive modes to Performance, and raise when the car is in Autonomous. Below this screen is an array of up to eight customizable rocker switches and knobs. You can swap the specific switches or knobs out, like you can with, say, a pro-grade video game controller, and their individual functions can also be configured to your liking. All four bucket seats sit low in the cabin and get inflatable bolsters and cushions for better support.

I've never really thought about how rare four-spoke wheels are, but looking at the 22s on this Polestar concept, I'm simultaneously sad that they aren't more common but also completely understand why that's the case. They're definitely weird, but I also sort of dig it?

It all adds up to a funky yet beautiful design that makes us hope Polestar will return to the U.S. market by the Formula 2030's namesake. Given the political conditions that led to its banning and how those tides are turning in this country, that scenario actually feels like a real possibility.

#5791. Michèle Mouton Driving A Quattro S1 Up Pikes Peak Is The Coolest Way To Promote Audi's New Q7

The first thing that comes to mind when you mention Quattro is probably Audi's 1980 Group B rally car. It makes perfect sense that the German manufacturer would lean on that legacy when promoting its new Q7 and SQ7 SUVs that have the trademarked all-wheel-drive system, but the company did something much cooler for its new ad campaign: Audi reunited Michèle Mouton with a Quattro S1 at the iconic Pikes Peak hill climb. The 1982 World Rally Championship runner-up still looked at home behind the wheel of the 493-horsepower rally monster.

Audi's new ad campaign titled "Performance Runs in the Family" features a pair of videos: a typical 30-second TV spot, and a 4-minute short with Mouton driving a Quattro S1 up Pikes Peak. During the visually stunning drive up the Colorado mountain, she reminisced about the animosity she faced during the 1985 hill climb, her winning run, and how the ascent has changed in the 41 years since.

Mouton was the first woman and the first non-American to win the Pikes Peak International Hill Climb. The Frenchwoman set a record time of 11:25.390 up the treacherous 12.4-mile ascent, besting the previous record set by Al Unser Jr. in 1983 by 13 seconds. Mouton is still effortlessly cool, still delivering pointed one-liners as naturally as the rest of us breathe. During the revival run, she noted, "When I was at this spot in the race, I had no time for feelings."

The 30-second TV spot mixes archival footage of Mouton during the Group B era with footage of a 2027 Audi SQ7 driving up Pikes Peak. To emphasize the campaign's legacy theme, the ad features Mouton alongside her daughter, Jessica. The third-generation Q7 isn't as nimble on the twisting mountain roads as a Group B rally car, but that isn't the point. The SUV's new styling looks just as aggressive and far more comfortable for a family. Vito Paladino, Audi of America's President, stated in a release:

"Audi's story of quattro didn't end with rally racing. That connection between performance and everyday capability is what makes quattro such a defining part of our brand. The all-new Audi Q7 family carries that legacy forward, bringing together Audi's performance heritage with the innovation and engineering that define the next generation of our vehicles."

The 1985 Pikes Peak International Hill Climb was Mouton's last event with Audi before she departed for her farewell season with Peugeot. The Frenchwoman and her accomplishments will forever be tied to Audi. Mouton's four WRC victories with the Audi Quattro are a legacy every four-wheel-drive model produced by the German automaker inherits. It's not merely laurels to rest on, but a level of performance that Audi has to honor.

#5790. Secure Solar Futures set to install 313-kW array on Virginia elementary school

Secure Solar Futures, a commercial solar contractor, will build a 313-kW solar power system at Preston Park Elementary School in Roanoke, Virginia. Construction of the system is expected to start later this year at no cost to the Roanoke Schools through a 25-year power purchase agreement with Secure Solar Futures.

A total of 531 bifacial solar panels manufactured by VSUN Solar in Houston, Texas, will be installed on the rooftop of the school, and the array is expected to offset 67% of the power used by the school annually. In its first year, the school’s solar system is projected to produce 421 MWh of clean energy, enough to power 44 average Virginia homes.

Under the PPA, the solar power equipment will be provided at no upfront cost to Roanoke Schools. For the 25-year term of the agreement, Pardee Renewable Energy and Secure Solar Futures will own, operate and maintain the solar power system and will sell the energy produced to the school division at a rate lower than the local electric utility.

Preston Park Elementary School is expected to save $581,000 electricity costs over the term of the PPA. After the conclusion of the term, Roanoke Schools can opt to take ownership of the solar power system at a nominal cost, producing its own energy for the remainder of the equipment’s lifespan.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5788. NASA Pushes Further Ahead With The Non-Space Side Of Its Mission, Teases New Experimental Plane

NASA has unveiled, sort of, its newest experimental plane. And by that, the agency appears to mean a plane meant to conduct experiments, rather than the plane itself being the experiment. Called the X-70 Fractal, the aircraft's primary selling point is its modular design, both inside and out. This means that payloads can be swapped out easily, whether they are hanging off the wings or nestled inside the plane. Even the flight controls can be reconfigured in case the agency wants to test different ways for the pilots (and it is piloted) to maneuver. The X-70 is really a platform to allow for a wide array of experiments on a single airframe, rather than creating a bespoke airframe to conduct one mission, like the X-59 is doing now.

As for the plane itself, the X-70 Fractal is... well, more announcement than vehicle, at least for now. NASA says that it can carry 2,000 pounds of science equipment and take off with up to 10,000 pounds total. It will fly up to 45,000 feet in altitude, far above most airliners and well into the stratosphere. It will go up to 1,500 nautical miles, broadly on par with an F-35. It also looks like an F-35, I think, maybe? For images, all we've got is this misty photo that looks AI generated. NASA seems to want to copy the energy of the F-47 and F/A-XX reveals, which are also foggy renderings and not actual photographs. Show me you're making an airplane without showing me you're making an airplane, I guess.

NASA achieves such historic feats in space that it's easy to forget that it is also an "Aeronautics" administration. Part of its remit is to push forward the boundaries of aviation here on this planet, which NASA Administrator Jared Isaacman has renewed focus on. Isaacman himself is a pilot who owns his own MiG-29 Soviet fighter jet, so this is an area of personal interest for him. In fact, he's promising that more experimental aircraft are on their way; rumors abound that the agency may be returning the famous SR-71 Blackbird, the fastest plane ever made, to service.

By doing so, NASA does important R&D on airplane technology that is then released into the public record, to the benefit of all aviation companies and everyone who flies on their products. That's the whole point of the X-59, which is trying to reduce the impact of sonic booms enough to make commercial supersonic planes financially viable. In addition, NASA's latest budget points to aviation as an area of strategic competition, per Wired. To that end, it may do some research alongside the Pentagon as well. This is where something like the X-70 is useful: it could do scientific, commercial, or military research as needed, simply by swapping out equipment payloads via its modular design. Now all NASA needs to do is, you know, build it, possibly with a contractor. Details!

#5787. Science For The People, Right On!

Today United for the Future launches at COP31 today with a plan to unite 100 million people in peaceful, non-partisan marches in cities worldwide. Their goal is to assemble the largest coordinated climate demonstration in history in order to put climate action back at the top of the political agenda.

Backers of the initiative include Christiana Figueres, former UN climate chief and a key architect of the Paris Agreement; Bill McKibben, author and co-founder of 350.org; Ram Ramanathan of Scripps Institution of Oceanography, UC San Diego; and Sandrine Dixson-Declève, honorary president of the Club of Rome.

Together, they beckon citizens around the world: “Protect our future. Act on the science.” Their call asks governments to stop weakening climate protections and to deliver action that cuts emissions and protects nature in line with science.

Limiting temperature rise to a 1.5 degrees Celsius, or 2.7 degrees Fahrenheit, above the preindustrial levels of the early 1800s was a goal established during the 2015 Paris Agreement. Since then, it’s been a focus of international climate negotiations, and one that’s often been politically contentious.

“For too many people, climate change is deadly,” UN climate chief Simon Stiell said at COP31. “At this COP, nations face a choice: electric or shock. Rewire the global economy for the electric age. Or accept ongoing fossil fuel cost pain for families, firms, and governments.”

Global carbon emissions from fossil fuels are projected to decrease by 0.4% in 2026, driven by expansion of solar and wind power and the Iran conflict, according to new research by the Global Carbon Project.

Then there’s the recent blue tsunami that took place in the US midterm elections, which served as a condemnation of the Trump administration. Historian and activist Rebecca Solnit exclaims, “It was a dam breaking, the dam that held back the will of the people, the people of the world’s most diverse country, the people who believed in their own rights and equality and in the rights and equality of others.”

Now, through promised agendas to increase affordability for consumers, Democrats have a keen opportunity to foreground science as a way to accomplish goals. Senator Bernie Sanders (I-VT) admonishes the new Democratic majority to deliver on its campaign jargon. “No more empty rhetoric. No more telling us how terrible Trump and the Republicans are. No more excuses,” he says. “We now have the votes to pass legislation that will demonstrably improve the lives of millions of working families. And that’s what we must do.”

Sanders insists that the time is now to “act boldly.” Democrats can create millions of good-paying jobs, he continues, “by moving our energy systems away from fossil fuels to energy efficiency and sustainable energy.”

David Fickling outlines in Bloomberg this week that renewables have already made tremendous headway, to the point that “sometimes, change comes at you so fast that you forget to congratulate yourself on what you’ve achieved.”

In 2025 clean energy sources generated nearly the same amount of electricity as the entire world consumed in 1996. About half of that electricity, Fickling continues, was sourced from wind and solar power.

“Instead of concentrating on squeezing the final tons of carbon from electricity,” he offers, “governments need to make abundant clean power cheap enough to drive fossil fuels out of everything else — in particular, electric cars and trucks, home heating systems, and the sort of low-temperature heat used in food and paper manufacturing.”

In that new cohort of leaders is William Lawrence, co-founder of the climate action movement, Sunrise. The Michigan Democrat flipped a House seat and sees clean energy as a way to fulfill campaign promises.

He describes clean energy tax credits as a good place to start.

“It’s a huge issue not just for the climate, but for our energy security,” he states. Climate is as important to energy security as it’s ever been, he tells Claire Brown of the New York Times.” People are experiencing the rising costs of fuel due to the war and volatility around the world. And we can all see the value of being able to have cheap, clean renewable energy that doesn’t depend on the global commodity markets.”

Several scientists and physicians were elected to the US Congress as part of the reinvigoration of science at the federal level. “The importance of having scientists, engineers, and health professionals in national, state, and local political positions cannot be overstated,” relates Deborah D. Stine, the executive director of the President’s Council of Advisers on Science and Technology during the Obama administration. “If the policy question is outside their own expertise, they know how to call on the best experts.”

The incoming scientists represent “a real repudiation of what has been two terrible years for science and public health,” Shaughnessy Naughton, president of 314 Action, chronicles for Alexander Nazaryan of the New York Times.

Where will the US Congress go from here? Rebecca Solnit muses, “What comes after hope, when hope is realized, when hope is no longer necessary?”

Clean energy expert Steve Hanley has an answer to that question. “The guiding star for all policy decisions going forward should be reducing carbon and methane emissions.”

Did you know that electric motors convert 90% of the energy they consume into motion, while an internal combustion engine converts less than 30%? Not everyone is aware of the efficiency of electrifying everything, though. Some scientists are already talking about the important need to draw in a new generation of clean energy advocates among Republicans in Congress and everyday life.

“In almost all circumstances, electrification is a good thing,” says Chris Bowen, Australia’s minister of climate change and energy and COP31’s president of negotiations.

Now we’ll see if Congress can translate political subterfuge into clean energy reality.

#5786. DOJ Slaps Lyft With $53 Million Lawsuit For Allegedly Discriminating Against Disabled Riders

The U.S. Department of Justice is suing ride-hailing company Lyft, accusing it of routinely discriminating against people with disabilities in direct violation of federal law. The lawsuit, filed in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of California, alleges that Lyft and its drivers "routinely refuse to serve" people with disabilities, such as folks who use stowable wheelchairs or ride with service animals. The DOJ also accused the San Francisco–based company of imposing cancellation fees on people who were denied service and charging passengers with disabilities higher fees and unnecessary surcharges.

The Justice Department sought $53 million in damages for the "hundreds, and likely thousands" of customers it said Lyft and its drivers had wronged, and it's the latest in a series of legal actions the government has taken against major ride-hailing companies related to alleged Americans with Disabilities Act violations, according to Reuters. Just last year, the DOJ accused Uber of similar behavior and sought $125 million in damages. Uber says it has since committed to expanding access and improving the experience of riders with disabilities.

The department said Lyft failed to change its policies and practices to prevent this sort of discrimination, and it failed to adequately train drivers and customer service representatives about their obligations under federal law, The New York Times reports. On top of the $53 million in damages for passengers, the government is asking the court to consider a civil penalty against Lyft.

Uber, and now Lyft, have disagreed with the allegations vehemently. Here's what Lyft told The Times in a statement:

Discrimination has no place in the Lyft community. We have longstanding policies requiring drivers to accommodate riders who travel with service animals or use foldable mobility devices, and drivers who violate these policies face serious consequences, including permanent deactivation.

It should be noted that Lyft says it is fully cooperating with the Department of Justice through this entire process.

Unfortunately for everyone involved, this is not the first time Lyft has been in hot water over discriminating against folks with disabilities. As The Times points out, the company resolved allegations that its drivers denied services to people who used wheelchairs or walkers in 2020. Admittedly, the scope and penalties were far smaller back then, but it's a big deal nonetheless. Six years ago, it agreed to revise its policies and pay four passengers thousands of dollars in damages, along with a $40,000 civil penalty.

#5785. At $17,000, Is This Meyers Manx SR2 A Super Cool Cat?

Today's Nice Price or No Dice Manx SR2 is the second VW-based kit car offered by legendary builder Bruce Meyers, and it features a design he thought would be far harder to copy than his first: the ubiquitous dune buggy. Let's decide what such a unique bit of car culture should cost.

With the recent discontinuation of the Supra, fans eager to carry the torch of non-tepid Toyotas are left with only the GR86 coupe and the crazily expensive GR Corolla as rides of choice. The fact that Toyota couldn't be bothered to develop the last-generation Supra on its own, leveraging BMW for most of the under-skirt bits, makes it questionable whether the company even has any interest in sporty cars anymore. Instead of pulse-pumping sports cars, the company offers around a dozen five-door crossover-style vehicles, since that's what sells these days.

That wasn't always the case; in addition to the Supra, Toyota once offered a rally-bred version of the stalwart Celica and a full-blown mid-engine sports car in the MR2. We looked at a 1993 Toyota MR2 on Tuesday, a non-turbo car that otherwise had all the options we'd want. Unfortunately, that non-turbo nature and a non-working A/C compressor called into question its $14,000 asking price. In the end, you all gave that a heave-ho in an 85% 'No Dice' downvote.

A lack of taint-tightening Toyotas doesn't mean that the automotive landscape has totally turned into dullsville, however. There are plenty of fun and interesting cars and, yes, even trucks, out there from which to choose when something more than sleepwalking self-driving is in order. But what if you wanted something crazier than what's on the menu right now from the ever-shrinking cadre of entertaining cars on the market? That's when a rare opportunity like today's Meyers Manx SR feels especially apt.

The SR2's creator, Bruce Meyers, grew up living the California dream. A bohemian artist and engineer, Meyers spent his early adult years surfing, off-road racing, and teaching himself to mold fiberglass as a boat builder. His talents all merged in the creation of the original Meyers Manx dune buggy, introduced in 1964.

Built on a VW Type 1 platform shortened by 14.5 inches, the Manx immediately became an icon and set off an industry of copycats. Meyers went to court to stop the copiers from making dime on his design, but to little success. Taking another tack, Meyers' next street kit, the Manx SR2 (Street Roadster), was far more complex, featuring 10 body panels (as opposed to the original Manx's two) and iconic scissor-style doors. Unfortunately, the cost of the lawsuits, along with nasty letters from the IRS, ate away at Meyers' company finances, and he closed the doors in 1971. By that time, fewer than 200 Manx SR body kits had left the Fountain Valley factory.

This is one of those rare SR models, built on a VW Beetle platform, but powered by a Chevy Corvair air-cooled six. Unlike most automotive engines, the Corvair mill spins counterclockwise, so the car also has the Chevy four-speed manual gearbox instead of a VW transmission. According to the ad, the added power (likely around 140 horsepower gross) makes the car "fly!"

The car rolls, appropriately enough, on a set of Western Bullet Torque Thrust-style wheels wrapped in white-letter BF Goodrich tires. They sit proud in the exaggerated wheel arches, looking almost comically large in comparison to the car's miniature 80-inch wheelbase.

Another way Bruce Meyers and co-designer Stewart Reed sought to improve upon the original Manx was through weather protection, fitting the SR2 with an optional fabric targa top and side curtains. This car appears to have neither, nor does it even seem to have a windshield wiper, making it a fair-weather friend. It's also missing one of the front bumperettes, which could be hard to find. Other than those issues, the car's exterior appears to be in really good shape, with the paint still shining and everything else still intact.

Make no mistake, though: this car is a project that needs some work. The seller claims the seats are "ready for upholstery," implying that they have somehow been pre-gaming for the effort. There's also apparently an issue with the scissor doors (a first, by the way), as they seem to be sitting in place in most of the pictures but aren't actually attached by their hinges. That's evident in the interior photo, where the passenger-side door is absent entirely.

The interior itself is in rough but serviceable condition. Those upholstery-ready seats are nothing more than fiberglass shells with an inch or so of ancient foam padding, and they look about as comfortable as sitting on an orange crate. The rest of the cabin is black-painted fiberglass with a bare VW floorpan. Neither element looks too egregious.

The dashboard offers a full complement of VDO gauges with the tach hilariously pegged at a 4,500 rpm redline. The speedometer's odometer inset shows 30,461 miles, but the ad claims an "I don't know" 99,999 as the mileage. On the plus side, the car's title is clean, and the seller claims it is licensed as an "asvr," which I believe means home-built, with a 1987 VIN assignment.

As noted at the outset, this Manx SR is one of those rare birds to catch in the classifieds. It's not crazy rare, though, as there's another with the same Corvair power, but in orange and with a salvage title, offered through one of the classic car classifieds.

This one, despite some questions and missing pieces, looks like a great foundation for a light restoration. That would result in a unique and fun sport-about for weekend and car show duty. Find the top and side curtains, and rain won't be the hindrance the car currently faces.

With all that in mind—and yes, it was a lot—what do you think about this Manx SR? More importantly, how do you feel about spending $17,000 to buy it? Is that a fair price, given the car's rarity and condition? Or does that price make you want to turn tail and run?

Los Angeles, California, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5784. The Netherlands Has €231 Million for Cleaner Inland Ships. It Still Needs Electric Freight Corridors.

The Netherlands is preparing to spend €231.2 million over four years helping inland ships replace fossil-fuel propulsion. The first €36.93 million tender opens on November 17, 2026, and battery-electric vessels are among the eligible investments. This is a serious step toward decarbonizing freight on the waterways that link Dutch ports and Europe's industrial heartland. But the programme's most important scoring calculation rewards emissions avoided per euro of propulsion subsidy while excluding separate subsidies for the shore infrastructure on which the ship may depend. That distinction matters when the commercial product is a complete freight service, not an electric vessel sitting beside a quay.

The new Dutch tender is considerably more sophisticated than a technology demonstration fund, yet it leaves an important accounting boundary. My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment follows that boundary from subsidized vessels to working routes, drawing on my open, 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report.

The RVO programme can support battery-electric systems, some hybrid arrangements and other propulsion technologies, together with eligible charging and refueling facilities. Its published explanation prioritizes electrification where practical. That is sensible: electric inland vessels are already operating in the Netherlands, and a subsidy should encourage the pathway that provides the required service at low emissions and acceptable cost.

The scheme also recognizes that a vessel does not operate in isolation. Applications receive points for commercial cooperation, cargo-owner agreements and the quality of supporting infrastructure arrangements. These are material criteria, not decorative promises. They are a welcome correction to programmes that fund novel equipment and assume the market, terminal and utility will somehow arrange everything else.

Yet most of the available points are tied to projected emissions reductions per euro of propulsion support. That is a consistent measure for comparing applications for individual ships. It is not an accounting of the entire investment required to turn a diesel freight route into an electric one. One barge may need access to battery exchange at both ends of its journey, reserve battery inventory, grid capacity, a crane or handling system and a dependable service agreement. Some of those facilities can serve many vessels, making it hard to allocate their cost and value to any single propulsion application.

Zero Emission Services, or ZES, has already shown what putting the pieces together looks like. Its swappable battery containers allow inland vessels to obtain charged energy without each owner purchasing and maintaining every battery required by the route. At Moerdijk, a terminal arrangement combines battery exchange, handling equipment and access to an existing electricity connection. The ZES network is extending beyond isolated demonstrations, although operating sites, planned stations and funded vessel conversions must not be counted as the same thing.

The difference between a capable ship and reliable freight is familiar to ports. Charged batteries must be available at the right time, terminals must handle exchanges without disrupting cargo work, and the energy-service provider needs enough committed traffic to finance its containers and chargers.

The Netherlands deserves credit for recognizing these dependencies. It need not discard vessel-level grants or wait until every future route is planned before making early awards. But a second programme-level measure is needed alongside emissions per propulsion subsidy: what complete, repeatable electric freight service is each funded project helping create, and which shared assets make that service possible?

This matters all the more because the freight being carried will change. Fossil-linked cargo is likely to decline as coal and petroleum use falls, and ports should not size batteries, terminals and connections around the assumption that today's bulk flows continue indefinitely. A route with strong contracted demand may justify shared charging equipment while a low-utilization route requires a different architecture or timetable. The answer depends on the freight task, not merely on the specifications of an available vessel.

The Dutch programme is a promising way to accelerate the first investments. Its next achievement should be to show that those investments connect: cargo moving on named routes, vessels using dependable shared energy infrastructure, and operating data strong enough to finance the following corridor without perpetually reinventing the subsidy. The ship is the visible part of the transition. The corridor is what makes it commercially useful.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines the subsidy rules, the Moerdijk battery-exchange arrangement and the difference between vessel awards and operational corridors. The open, 98-page Inland Freight Electrification report contains the modelled cargo, fleet, battery-pool, terminal and investment evidence behind that analysis.

#5782. HHS Vaccine Task Force Misses Report Deadline

— Report is part of an overall strategy to undermine trust in vaccines, say public health advocates

Now that the deadline has passed for an HHS vaccine task force to issue its report on the timing and sequencing of vaccines, public health advocates are concerned about what happens when the report eventually comes out.

"The overarching concern with this report, should it ever materialize, is that it will be more textual squid ink that accomplishes nothing other than reinforcing unfounded doubts about vaccines that have been cultivated by the HHS secretary for decades," Elizabeth Jacobs, PhD, professor emerita of epidemiology and biostatistics at the University of Arizona in Tucson and a founding member of Defend Public Health, said in an email. "In fact, this may be its only real purpose."

The report grew out of an executive order issued Aug. 10 by President Donald Trump. The order directed HHS to, within 90 days, present plans to "offer options to administer core childhood vaccines, starting with MMR [measles, mumps, and rubella], as single vaccines rather than combination products/doses" while still guaranteeing continued availability of combination vaccines, and to "assess the ideal timing and sequencing of all core childhood vaccines and adjust the federal childhood and adolescent vaccine schedule as appropriate based on gold standard science." It also directed the agency to "improve vaccine safety monitoring, transparency, and research."

The focus on the MMR vaccine is particularly unhelpful now, "when we are in the midst of an uncontrolled measles outbreak that is likely to result in the U.S. losing its measles elimination status this week or next," said Jacobs, adding that in a just world, "the focus right now would not be on making it more difficult to complete vaccinations for measles, mumps, and rubella. It would be on rolling out a rigorous public health campaign to ensure that all people in the U.S. are protected with the only measles vaccine we have at our disposal -- the safe and effective MMR vaccine."

Asked Tuesday about the missed deadline, an HHS spokesperson said in an emailed statement that the agency "is actively implementing President Trump's Executive Order on Gold Standard Childhood Vaccine Recommendations. The HHS Task Force on Safer Childhood Vaccines is working to strengthen vaccine safety research, improve transparency, and expand options for American families. This work requires careful scientific review, and HHS remains committed to advancing the President's priorities through gold standard science and informed parental choice."

The process of using a task force to recommend changes to the vaccine schedule does not sit well with the Infectious Diseases Society of America.

"Any changes to vaccine recommendations should be made by a legitimate, legal ACIP [Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices] whose members have the relevant expertise and who have been thoroughly vetted and appointed through the standard process," Amanda Jezek, the society's senior vice president of public policy and government relations, said in an email.

"Changes to the childhood immunization schedule that have previously been proposed by the administration lack scientific justification and would cause significant confusion among clinicians, patients, and families and could jeopardize insurance coverage and access to vaccines," she said. "Some of the vaccines that have been discussed to be offered separately are not even manufactured as separate vaccines and there is no scientific justification to separate them."

The task force is just part of a plan by HHS Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. to undermine vaccines, Paul Offit, MD, director of the Vaccine Education Center at Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, said in a phone interview. "He thinks there's gaslighting, that parents who have seen their children hurt by vaccines are dismissed," he said. "So he's going to get to the bottom of this."

"Kennedy has created a four-pronged initiative that will have an NIH clinic devoted to vaccine safety; that will mine electronic medical records to try and find data showing that vaccines are causing harm; that will pay doctors to report harms to the Vaccines Adverse Event Reporting System [VAERS]; and that will expand [VAERS] and modernize it because he is of the belief that these safety issues are being [hidden], which they're not," said Offit.

"Whenever a vaccine comes into the market, it's very quickly subjected to analysis through vaccine safety data," specifically through the CDC's Vaccine Safety Datalink, he said. "You see who's gotten vaccines and who hasn't, and you can very quickly determine whether there's a problem." For example, that system helped public health researchers figure out that the COVID vaccine caused myocarditis in rare cases.

Offit also scoffed at the idea of paying doctors to report vaccine adverse events -- "as if that's the only way they'd be motivated to do it."

He predicted that eventually, Kennedy "will do the same thing as when he testified at the Senate HELP [Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions] Committee ... he held up a paper that had never been published [because] it was horribly flawed and horribly confounded. And he said, 'Look, here's a paper that shows, using the Florida Medicaid data system, that vaccines cause permanent neurological harm.'"

Under the current plan, Kennedy will point to a series of studies to say, "I was right all along," Offit said. Under a worst-case scenario, "he will try and add some of these harms to the Vaccine Injury Compensation Program. ... If he does that, if he breaks the vaccine compensation program, we'll be right back to where we were in the 1980s when companies left the [vaccine] business."

#5781. Finland Allows Tesla FSD, So Long As It's Not Called That

Happy Wednesday! It's November 11, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift — your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition we're looking at Finland approving FSD by any other name, and Volkswagen's successes in China. We'll also look at VW's future plans in the country, and Nissan's profit targets.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Tesla desperately wants its FSD software approved in Europe, but so far it's only been adopted piecemeal. The latest adopter? Finland, which approved the tech with some slight modifications — but said that it may not vote the same way in an EU-wide decision. From Reuters:

STOCKHOLM, Nov 11 (Reuters) – Finland ‌granted provisional approval on Wednesday for Tesla's Assisted Driving system (TAD) after the US automaker made changes to the technology, allowing its use on Finnish roads ahead of an EU-wide ​vote.

Nearly a dozen countries, including Slovakia and Belgium, have granted provisional approvals for ​the technology, which allows vehicles to steer, accelerate and brake automatically ⁠under driver supervision.

However, Tesla has yet to secure backing from any of ​the EU's largest member states, whose support is likely to be crucial to ​winning approval across the bloc.

Finland's transport authority, Traficom, said it ​approved the system after Tesla renamed it from "Full Self-Driving" and tightened speed-control settings ​that critics said encouraged speeding.

FSD is, really, Tesla's flagship at the moment. The Roadster isn't real, the Model S, Model X, and Cybertruck are all dead, the Semi only sort of exists for fleet sales, and the Optimus robot will likely never be a household product. Tesla's big thing is its software, and it can't sell that software in a major economic bloc. Not a great look for a company that pretends not to be a car company — though at this point, with only two real offerings, maybe it's no longer pretending.

China has been a rough market for most automakers recently, with the country's consumers spending less and preferring to dedicate the cash they do spend on domestic brands. Volkswagen, though, is doing just fine. In fact, it just set a sales record in the country. From Automotive News:

Volkswagen Group said its namesake brand's sales of electric vehicles and hybrids in China climbed to a record last month and forecast that growth will continue this year as its turnaround efforts in the world's largest auto market start bearing fruit.

October deliveries of VW EVs and plug-in hybrids surged nearly 350 percent from a year ago to more than 28,000 vehicles, the group said Nov. 11. New models including the ID Aura T6 SUV and the ID Era 5S sedan drove the increase.

The company said it expects to sustain growth in the coming months, with a goal of ranking among the 10 best-selling New Energy Vehicle brands in the country next year.

Of course, those sales goals could be stymied by our next gear.

Despite recent sales success, Volkswagen doesn't seem to have much invested in the future of its Chinese wing. In fact, the brand isn't even working on new models for the Chinese market — at least, not new models due out before 2029. From Reuters:

BEIJING, Nov 11 (Reuters) – Volkswagen's electric-vehicle joint venture in China has not planned any new models for 2027 or 2028, two people with knowledge ​of the situation said, after it banked on far greater demand for its ID. UNYX series than what has materialised.

Without new models, money-losing ‌Volkswagen Anhui is stuck with a line-up that has not caught on with the young, urban consumers it targeted when it launched the UNYX marque in July 2024, in a market where automakers introduce models and variants on a monthly basis, with hundreds launched so far this year.

Reuters' characterization of Volkswagen Anhui as having "not caught on" seems a bit much, with the brand setting its own sales records. Even if it's growing, though, it's still small — small enough that VW corporate may not want to risk putting good money after bad.

Speaking of sales being down in China, Nissan's not doing so hot in the country. Its profit goals are undeterred, though, as the company's CEO expects sales in Japan and the U.S. to skyrocket in place of China. From Automotive News:

YOKOHAMA, Japan — Nissan CEO Ivan Espinosa is betting on rising sales in the U.S. and Japan to keep earnings on track after the crumbling China market undercut quarterly performance and forced him to downgrade the company's global sales forecast for the second time this year.

Nissan Motor Co. upgraded its sales target for the key markets of Japan and the U.S. for the current fiscal year ending March 31 citing the arrival of new product such as the Rogue crossover.

But the uptick won't be enough to offset the China slump, with Nissan forecasting global sales to fall to between 2.97 million to 3 million vehicles this fiscal year, from 3.15 million last year.

We haven't seen U.S. car sales tank like China yet, but it does feel like it's on the horizon — cars keep getting pricier and buyers keep getting broker. Eventually, something has to give. Hopefully it doesn't kill Nissan on the way.

What happened to you, journalism industry? You used to be cool. I don't mean the drugs part, I mean the "paying people a livable wage to write stuff like that" part.

Under $4! This happened yesterday, so Andy got the big celebration, but it's worth mentioning today too. Back to brunch, everyone.

#5779. Lincoln Recalls 37,000 Navigators Because The Front Seats Might Be Haunted

If the U.S. were a sane country, stores would be decorated for Thanksgiving right now, not covered in Santas and blasting Christmas music. But regardless of whether you properly appreciate our national eating holiday or insist on skipping straight to the materialist holiday, at least we can all agree spooky season should be behind us. Which is why we wish we'd caught Lincoln's latest Navigator recall last month when it was more on theme. You see, according to the recall, Lincoln's concerned some of its seats could basically become haunted.

The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration published the recall on October 20, less than two weeks before Halloween, but sadly, we missed it until our friends over at Car and Driver spotted it yesterday. Technically, neither Lincoln, NHTSA, nor Car and Driver used the word "haunted," but what other term are you supposed to use when the official description of the recall is literally, "Front Passenger Seat May Move Unexpectedly,"? Ghosts or no ghosts, those Navigator seats sound haunted, and you'll never convince me otherwise.

Per the information in the recall, Lincoln believes as many as 36,994 Navigators built for the 2025, 2026, and 2027 model years could be affected. Typically, though, recalls involve a large number of cars the automaker wants to check for a defect, even if only 1%-3% of the recalled vehicles will likely have any problems. This one's a little different, though, with the recall report estimating a whopping 100% estimated to have the defect. So if you own one of the affected Navigators, the dealer will probably have to fix it.

So what's NHTSA's theory? Well, it isn't ghosts, even though we know for a fact that it's definitely ghosts. Lincoln just doesn't want to spook everyone by confirming the supernatural is real. Officially, owners whose Navigators have the chauffeur package may notice their front passenger seat moving unexpectedly, and it's all because of your (kids') nasty, grubby little fingers. From the recall report:

Affected vehicles contain a "Chauffer Seat Feature" that can be accessed by a passenger in the rear seat via a second-row digital touchscreen console, known as the RACM. One action on this RACM is to allow the rear passenger the ability to move the front passenger seat in order to provide more legroom.

Residue left on the RACM can be registered as finger touch input allowing the front passenger seat to move unintentionally. Additionally, if the home screen button on the RACM is pressed at the same time the seat movement button is pressed, the seat may continue moving to its full travel limit without any additional input.

Personally, I still think ghosts are a more likely explanation than Cheeto fingers, but I guess you can't exactly put that in an official government recall. Could you imagine the civil unrest if the feds confirmed the existence of ghosts? Basically, what I'm saying is that you and I both know they're lying, but it's better to pretend there's a problem with the RACM. Faulty Lincolns, American can deal with. Haunted Lincolns? That's probably too much, all things considered.

#5778. Rheumatoid Arthritis Does Not Improve the Menopause Experience

ORLANDO -- Women with rheumatoid arthritis (RA) had a worse menopause experience compared with the non-RA individuals going through the transition, a researcher reported here.

Symptom severity, as evaluated with the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS), was significantly greater among menopausal RA patients versus other participants in two registries maintained by the Mayo Clinic, with mean scores of 15 as compared with 13 across both cohorts (P<0.001), according to Kristen J. Young, DO, of the Mayo's location in Phoenix.

Moreover, 72% of the RA patients reported a "clinically significant symptom burden," versus 57% in the entire sample (P<0.001), she told attendees at the American College of Rheumatology's annual meeting.

Individual menopausal symptoms including fatigue, sleep disturbance, joint pain, and mood changes appeared worse with RA. Because these are also symptoms of RA, one could also look at these data and conclude that menopause may worsen RA.

In fact, Young did just that. "Menopausal symptoms may compound the existing symptom burden of RA," she said, adding, "Women with RA will benefit" if their rheumatologists take account of menopause.

In discussing the background to the study, Young referenced a British survey of RA patients that included 612 women who were going through menopause, or had done so. Some 80% said their RA symptoms had worsened during menopause. Moreover, Young said, most (93%, according to the original paper) said their rheumatologic care teams never asked at routine visits about how menopause was affecting them.

For the current study, Young and colleagues drew on two datasets compiled at Mayo: the Data Registry on Experiences of Aging, Menopause, and Sexuality, which consisted of women seeking specialized menopause care; and a group of older women receiving broader care called the Hormones and Experiences of Aging cohort. In total, 9,356 women were included in these registries, of whom 215 had RA.

The patient-reported MRS instrument covers three domains: psychological, somatic, and urogenital. The first comprises mood and fatigue; somatic symptoms include hot flashes, sleep, and musculoskeletal complaints; and sexual, bladder, and vaginal symptoms make up the urogenital domain. Eleven questions are posed, with 0-4 points awarded on each for increasing severity. The maximum is therefore 44, but scores that high are vanishingly rare. Total scores of 17 or more are considered severe, and 9-16 is considered moderate. Young and colleagues set 12 as the threshold for a clinically significant burden.

Mean age in the two cohorts was about 54, both for the RA and non-RA participants. More than 90% were white and about 35% had obesity. Roughly 60% were in menopause when completing the MRS, about 10% were perimenopausal, and the rest were postmenopausal. Relatively few were taking hormone therapy. Also, about 500 participants had non-RA autoimmune diseases including lupus, multiple sclerosis, psoriasis, and inflammatory bowel disease.

For the main MRS-based outcomes, the RA group was compared with the entire registry sample that (for reasons Young didn't discuss) included the RA patients as well as those with other autoimmune conditions. Had the RA patients been separated, however, it probably wouldn't have changed the statistics very much, since they accounted for only 2% of the total. And some analyses did compare RA with non-RA participants.

Psychological and urogenital symptom burdens with menopause did not appear worsened at all with RA -- only somatic symptoms seemed to be exacerbated. Total somatic scores averaged 6 in the RA group versus 5 in the total sample. A greater difference was seen when looking at clinically significant burdens in the somatic domain, defined as an MRS score of at least 8. In this comparison, 20% of the total sample had a significant burden, compared with 31% of RA patients.

And joint pain was not solely responsible for this latter difference. Compared with non-RA participants, more of those with RA reported moderate, severe, and very severe hot flashes (49% vs 41%, P=0.024).

Another analysis compared the RA group with participants having other autoimmune conditions on total MRS scores, proportions with clinically significant menopause burdens, and severe hot flashes. Only with RA were these worse than in the total sample, although the smaller numbers of patients with non-RA autoimmune diseases render these differences less reliable. (Interestingly, prevalence of severe hot flashes appeared lower in lupus patients.)

Young noted that the databases didn't include information on RA severity, an important limitation insofar as RA symptoms overlap so much with those of menopause. As well, the study was restricted to women seen at the Mayo Clinic, who may not be representative of either the general population or those with RA.

#5777. The Perovskite Solar Cell Kraken Has Been Unleashed Upon The US

Perovskite is a relatively inexpensive material that has attracted thousands of researchers since 2009, when a team of scientists in Japan first demonstrated its use in solar cells. They achieved a solar energy conversion efficiency of little more than 3%, but since science loves a challenge, that was just the beginning of the story. In the latest chapter, the US firms Heliene and Caeulux have just introduced a new perovskite-enabled solar module that hits the jackpot at up to 27% efficiency, and it is already in commercial production, no less.

So far in the renewable energy transition, silicon has shouldered much of the heavy lifting due to its excellent solar conversion properties. Still, silicon is relatively expensive material that demands a complex manufacturing process. Perovskite is the reverse. Based on a naturally occurring mineral, synthetic perovskites can be lab-grown and applied to solar cell fabrication in a high volume, high throughput process similar to roll-to-roll manufacturing and other solution-based methods (see lots more perovskite background here).

There being no such thing as a free lunch, “raw” perovskite is challenged by natural conditions, namely, humidity. Tweaking the formula with other, more durable materials can resolve that problem. Researchers have also developed a tandem solution, in which a perovskite layer rests upon a silicon layer, providing an efficiency boost while minimizing the additional cost.

In the US solar market, the sticky wicket is the current state of federal energy, trade, and tax polices. Still, the red-hot demand for solar energy in the US has motivated solar stakeholders to steamroll over those obstacles, attracting overseas investors (here’s one example) as well as new domestic ventures.

On the domestic side, the Corning-backed manufacturer Heliene has been focusing on silicon while the startup Caelux has tackled the perovskite solar cell challenge with a glass substrate that can be layered onto other materials in a solar module. Now the two manufacturers have combined forces, with Caelux’s perovskite-doped glass boosting the conversion efficiency of Heliene’s N-type solar cells by up to 20%.

The Caelux-Heliene mashup comes at a time when Congress has finally put the wheels in motion to streamline the permitting process for energy projects. The two firms already have a running head start. On November 10, the two firms announced that commercial-scale production has already begun on their Hybrid-Tandem™ solar module

“Manufactured by Heliene in Minnesota, the perovskite plus silicon technology combines Caelux’s Energy Producing Glass with Heliene’s established silicon module platform to construct a solar module with two separate power generation layers that deliver higher power density,” Heliene explains.

“One of the advantages of Caelux’s technology is that we can integrate its Energy Producing Glass into our existing manufacturing lines without having to build an entirely new production setup,” added Martin Pochtaruk, Heliene CEO and Founder, in a press statement.

“With both companies manufacturing in the U.S., we’re bringing together two fully domestic supply chains to advance what’s possible with American-made solar,” Pochtaruk emphasized.

Stay tuned for the next development. The new modules are now in production at Heliene’s manufacturing facility Minnesota, with deliveries set for 2027. That’s just for starters. Caelux and Heliene have already targeted 29% efficiency and up for their next-generation modules.

The conversion efficiency improvement is the gift that keeps on giving. In the rooftop solar sector, for example, high-efficiency solar cells enable developers to squeeze more electricity out of a given space, making a rooftop array more economical for smaller properties that are otherwise shut out of the market. The cost of a smaller, more effcient array can also ripple downwards in the form of savings on labor, hardware, and transportation expenses.

For rooftop solar in general, the boost in efficiency provides an opportunity to take full advantage of power sharing programs with local utilities, which enable solar owners to earn revenue and qualify for time-of-use discounts. The benefits can ripple into home energy storage systems and home EV charging as well.

On a larger scale, the space-saving angle can help alleviate land use conflicts that otherwise stymie solar development, particularly in the area of farm-to-solar conversion.

Although real estate development is by far the primary factor driving the loss of farmland in the US, solar opponents (looking at you, Brooke Rollins) have played the farm card to gin up resistance to solar development in rural communities. With efficiency improvements, the economic case improves for solar arrays on marginal lands and smaller parcels. That can be partcularly helpful in the community solar field, where solar arrays are typically smaller.

Additionally, the improved efficiency can help accelerate the uptake of agrivoltaic practices that balance energy-producing solar panels with grazing and croplands. These dual-use arrays can enable farmers to continue working their land while guaranteeing a revenue stream from the solar developer.

Furthermore, high-efficiency solar opens up new opportunities in the field of floating solar, where developers can make use of reservoirs and other human-engineered bodies of water instead of taking up space on land. By taking up less room on water for the same power generation capacity, floating solar can also squeeze into reservoirs where recreation and habitat conservation also compete for space.

Perovskite solar cells represent just one key breakthrough area in the US solar industry. Easing the federal energy permitting bottleneck is another. That can be a two-edged sword, enabling more fossil energy resources to flow through the pipeline. However, permitting reform could also prevent the fossil energy industry from running amok through federal energy policy.

Members of Congress are currently negotiating over the particulars of Senate Bill 5653, aka the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act. If enacted as envisioned by the solar industry, the reforms will put a crimp in Trump’s “American Energy Dominance” policy, which has placed new and burdensome restrictions on both wind and solar activity in the US while aggressively supporting fossil fuels and nuclear, alongside other renewables including biomass, geothermal, hydropower. Even energy storage has come in for a share of love from the Trump administration, further underscoring the remarkable exclusion of wind and solar from federal energy policy.

The trade organization Solar Energy Industries Association, for one, is urging members on both sides of the aisle to ensure that permitting reform focuses on fairness, predictability, and certainty. In a letter to the House and Senate, SEIA emphasizes that solar and storage developers “cannot finance multi-year, capital-intensive projects without the predictability and certainty needed to build projects and connect them to the grid.”

“According to researchers at Wood Mackenzie, solar and storage represent 70% of the new capacity added to the grid thus far in 2026. Yet $120 billion in clean energy investment is caught up in current permitting delays, let alone the impact of permitting challenges to future deployment,” SEIA adds.

Of course, even if such a bill passes both Houses of Congress, US President Donald Trump could exercise his veto power, and he has already threatened to make copious use of it during his last two years in office. Nevertheless, US Presidents come and go, and this one is scheduled to go in January of 2029 — peacefully this time, one hopes.

Image: The US perovskite solar cell specialist Caelux is combining with the startup Heliene to manufacture high efficiency perovskite-silicon modules in the US (cropped courtesy of Caelux).

#5776. 2027 Mercedes C-Class Follows The Formula And Sticks The Landing

As computers get cheaper, electronic features more important, and new automakers enter the U.S. market, it's harder to understand where each luxury brand positions itself. BMWs were traditionally the sporty ones, but with an adjustable suspension, you can make a sporty BMW feel luxurious. Is Genesis a value brand or a design brand? Who buys an Audi and why? Mercedes, however, remains pretty easy to pigeonhole. It's the tech and luxury brand, whether you buy a six-figure GLS or a more affordable alternative, like the facelifted Mercedes-Benz C-Class.

If you don't like screens and wish Mercedes would go back to building the kinds of cars it did in the late 1980s, I'm sorry. The new C-Class probably isn't for you. Mercedes is looking forward, not back, and that means packing as much tech into its cars as it can. That may not be to your personal taste, but it does give Mercedes a clear identity. So even if you haven't driven the new C-Class, in some ways, you already know what to expect. You're going to get a comfortable, spacious luxury car with big screens, more features than you need or may even be able to remember, and all the refinement Mercedes can muster. It just doesn't hide its secrets.

Mercedes likes to call its cars "new" any time it updates them, but really, this is a refresh. That was also to be expected, since this W206 generation that first went on sale for the 2022 model year still hadn't received a mid-cycle refresh. Sometimes, an update means significant styling changes both inside and out, but here, Mercedes takes a more restrained approach. You get a bigger grille and the star headlights and taillights that we first saw on the CLA, but pretty much everything else about the exterior styling looks familiar. The new C-Class makes it clear from the start that you won't find too many mysteries or wild changes inside.

Full Disclosure: Mercedes-Benz wanted me to drive the new C-Class so badly, it bought me the necessary flights and train tickets, booked two different hotel rooms for me, fed me and kept me hydrated, provided transportation to various locations, and regularly offered me snacks. I then crashed from exhaustion when I got back, got sick, and have finally gotten my thoughts together about the new C-Class. And yes, you read that right, I got to ride on a European train!

The car keeps its word, too. If you've driven the pre-facelift C-Class, you'll be right at home in the new one's largely unchanged cabin. That does mean it's time for the big screen haters to look away from the screens they're reading this on, because yes, Mercedes kept the 12.3-inch driver display and 11.9-inch infotainment touchscreen from the outgoing model. The big change is that it now features Mercedes' latest MB.OS infotainment system, which has AI built in. In fact, the new C-Class has multiple AIs built in, allowing you to get use ChatGPT, Gemini, or (for some reason) Bing.

The AI integration doesn't really add much to the experience, but if you use voice commands, the AI does process language more naturally and is more helpful as a result. Of course, you're still using AI, and that comes with a whole host of other ethical issues, but functionally, yes, it is better than before. Anyone who gave up on voice controls that were more frustrating to use than they were helpful may want to give them a try, though, because we're finally at a point where it isn't always easier to find the screen controls yourself.

The bigger story is the engineers upping the C-Class' processing power to the point that it qualifies as a supercomputer. That makes it possible for Mercedes to offer a more advanced version of its augmented reality head-up display, and it needed to upgrade the processing power before it could add its MB.Drive Assist Pro "Level 2++" advanced driver-assistance system that should rival Tesla's so-called Full Self-Driving, only made by a company that cares about safety. Still, if you're familiar with the previous version, the C-Class' infotainment system should feel incredibly familiar, so no need to worry about getting lost in menus or being forced to use the voice controls after giving up.

While the exterior styling helps keep the C-Class in line with other Mercedes models, and the software updates help keep the cabin current, you could also forgive someone who didn't know for not realizing Mercedes even updated its compact sedan. Still, I can't imagine that person would have any complaints about the fit and finish of the cabin, how comfortable it is, or how little noise intrudes once the doors are closed and the windows are up, which will be even more appreciated if you spring for the optional Burmester 3D surround-sound system with Dolby Atmos.

Since we were in Europe, Mercedes had us driving European models, which meant I had access to the wagon, a plug-in-hybrid powertrain, and more. The U.S., however, only gets the C350 for now, with a turbocharged 2.0-liter inline-4 making the same 255 horsepower and 295 pound-feet of torque as the pre-facelift C300. Mercedes promises a 5.8-second 0-to-60-mph time with the help of its familiar 9-speed automatic transmission. That's not especially quick, but Mercedes-AMG will have more powerful versions coming before we know it — in fact, the brand has already confirmed the old four-cylinder C63 will be replaced by a six-cylinder model.

And let's not get too jaded here. Nearly 300 pound-feet of torque is more than enough to enjoy in a daily driver, and you now get a 48-volt mild-hybrid system with an integrated starter-generator that produces 23 hp and 151 lb-ft for better responses and smoother operation, plus a new electric auxiliary compressor that promises a near-instant boost of 27 hp for up to 20 seconds whenever you want it. It's also 0.2 seconds quicker to 60 mph than the outgoing C300, so it works, too.

As a luxury-focused luxury sedan, the C350 isn't the car you want if you live up in Blue Ridge, Georgia, and spend most of your time driving on winding mountain roads. It's exceptionally quiet and solid, comfortable enough that I would have happily driven it to Paris if we could have gotten away with it. While it may not encourage you to drive faster through the corners, the C350 is plenty competent when the road gets less straight. If you accidentally turned onto Topanga Canyon Road, you'd be fine. In fact, you'd probably have fun, but you'd be wishing you had performance brakes and a performance suspension, at the very least. Having driven a few sedans on roads that made me regret my decision, it's at least nice that the C350 brings some handling to the table.

The C350 may not inspire you to drive more aggressively, but it could cause you to take more road trips, because a nice, smooth cruise is where it's truly the happiest. You want gentle sweepers, rolling hills, and maybe even some forest, not plunging cliffs and switchbacks. Around town, you're more likely to notice bumps and potholes compared to, say, an S-Class or GLS, but once you find the right road, the C350's mix of mild sportiness and extra comfort delivers the kind of driving experience that has, "I could just keep driving," written all over it.

However, one decidedly non-luxurious thing about the C350 I drove was the engine's sound. At one point, I had to ask my co-driver whether we'd ended up with a diesel, but nope. It had a gasoline engine. It just sounds a lot more agricultural than you'd expect from a four-cylinder sold by a brand that prioritizes the luxury experience. Then again, I'm also willing to admit that the overwhelming majority of people who care how an engine sounds will simply wait for one wearing an AMG badge.

Driving the other versions that we don't get here, on the other hand, left me far more disappointed than the lack of exciting updates for me to write about. Plans may change as we see how the political landscape shifts in the coming year, but it's incredibly disappointing that Mercedes doesn't currently plan to bring the plug-in-hybrid C-Class to America. Not because of how much I care about the planet, but because it's just a better luxury car. The C400e has a total of 395 horsepower and 479 lb-ft, with the larger electric motor's torque making acceleration smoother and near-instant. Its 19.53-kWh battery gives it a range of 62 miles on the European WLTP cycle, too.

I can't get too mad that Mercedes has no plans bring the wagon version to a longroof-averse country like the U.S., since car companies have to sell vehicles if they want to stay in business, but I'm definitely disappointed we won't get the PHEV version that drives better and will be cheaper to operate. Then again, I'm one of those car-hating lefty auto journalists who likes the feel of instant torque. As we all know, real car enthusiasts would rather wait to accelerate. (If you do want electric power, you can always go for the C-Class EV, which is its own car entirely.)

Unless you drive the new C-Class back-to-back with the old car, you may not notice many changes aside from the lights, but that doesn't mean they aren't there. Like the electric turbocharger, they tend to be present only in the background. In a lot of ways, that feels like the car's attitude in general. It just wants to disappear. Don't think too much about the car while you're driving it. Just get from one place to the other in comfort. Need to give some friends a ride? They'll be comfortable, too. Headed out on a road trip? As long as your luggage fits, take the C350.

So while I can't say that you desperately need to experience the C-Class's incredible handling for yourself or anything like that, I can tell you that if you just want the most comfortable and tech-forward compact luxury sedan you can buy, it's going to be hard to beat the updated 2027 Mercedes-Benz C-Class. What it will cost you, however, remains to be seen. The current C300 starts at exactly $51,000 with destination, and the '27 C350 is so similar, don't expect the price to climb too much higher. As for when we'll be able to buy the new C-Class in the U.S., expect deliveries to begin before Q3 of next year.

#5775. Two Biologics for Psoriatic Arthritis Square Off in Big Trial

— Winner emerges (maybe) from first study to compare anti-IL-23 with anti-IL-17A/F

ORLANDO -- A large head-to-head trial reported here that pitted bimekizumab (Bimzelx) against risankizumab (Skyrizi) as treatment for psoriatic arthritis (PsA) yielded a winner, albeit with a couple caveats.

Arthritic symptoms were relieved more effectively with bimekizumab, an inhibitor of interleukin (IL)-17A/F, with 49.1% of patients achieving ACR50 responses (50% decrease in symptom severity by American College of Rheumatology criteria) after 16 weeks of treatment, compared with 38% of those treated with the IL-23 inhibitor risankizumab, according to Joseph Merola, MD, of the University of Texas Southwestern Medical Center in Dallas.

Bimekizumab was already superior by this measure by week 4, and the advantage was maintained out to week 24 (55.6% vs 43.8%, P=0.0037), he told attendees at the ACR's annual meeting. Effects on skin manifestations were similar at study end, but Merola reported that resolution of psoriatic symptoms came on faster with the IL-17A/F inhibitor.

But about those caveats: Safety data favored risankizumab, because Candida and other fungal infections were more common with bimekizumab at 5.8% versus 1.5%. And cynics may find it relevant that the trial was sponsored by UCB, bimekizumab's manufacturer.

Both drugs are currently approved for PsA, which has become a very competitive market for treatment. Naturally, clinicians and patients want to know how the different options differ and whether any can be said to be superior. Merola observed that IL-23 acts at an upstream point in the pathological sequence underlying PsA, helping to drive production of adaptive lymphocytes; IL-17 species, meanwhile, mediate their proinflammatory activity. He said that by acting on this downstream segment to prevent inflammation, bimekizumab might yield greater clinical benefits.

The current trial, dubbed BE BOLD, randomized more than 550 PsA patients in equal numbers to the two drugs, administered according to their labels. The primary outcome measure was the ACR50 responder percentage at week 16, with treatment and outcome tracking continuing to week 24. Secondary outcomes included achievement of minimal disease activity (MDA), and the proportion of patients achieving both ACR50 and PASI100 (100% reduction in Psoriasis Area and Severity Index scores). He also reported PASI100 response numbers separately.

Mean patient age was 51 and just over half were men. Time since diagnosis averaged about 6 years; about 20% had previously used tumor necrosis factor inhibitors. Tender and swollen counts averaged 17 and 10, respectively. Psoriatic lesions were rated moderate to severe in some 11%, and the mean PASI score was about 8.5.

Bimekizumab proved numerically better than risankizumab in the MDA analysis, but the gap was much narrower and statistically significant only at week 8. At weeks 16 and 24, the respective MDA responses were as follows:

Something similar was seen for achieving both ACR50 and PASI100. At week 8, about 20% of the bimekizumab group got there, compared with less than 10% with risankizumab (P=0.0015). The significant advantage for bimekizumab continued to week 12, but the gap narrowed thereafter.

This was true as well for PASI100 by itself, with a big difference during weeks 4-12, then closing to almost nothing by week 24, at which point 59% of both groups had achieved it.

Another "skin-facing" metric, as Merola put it, was achievement of low disease activity or remission with Disease Activity Index for Psoriatic Arthritis scores. This, too, followed the same pattern: an earlier response with bimekizumab with risankizumab catching up as the trial continued. By week 24, over 60% in both groups reached these milestones.

In general, Merola said, safety findings were as expected from previous studies. Rates of treatment-emergent adverse events overall were similar with the two drugs. Just seven participants stopped therapy because of adverse events, split evenly. One patient with a history of cardiovascular disease receiving risankizumab died of a myocardial infarction, which was considered unrelated to the drug. Upper respiratory tract infections were a bit more common with bimekizumab.

That fungal infections were increased with bimekizumab was not surprising, Merola said, because IL-17 is important in resisting these organisms. In randomized trials leading to the product's approval, 9% of patients developed oral candidiasis, actually a much higher rate than in the new study, in which it occurred in just 2.5% of patients.

#5772. New Lotus Supercar's 670-HP Twin-Turbo V8 Revealed By Powertrain Supplier Horse

Back in May, Lotus announced that it would be introducing a new supercar for 2028. Known as the Type 135, it will be a blank-sheet development and get a hybrid V8 powertrain making more than 986 horsepower (or 1,000 metric horses) combining electricity and internal combustion. The company's new powertrain supplier, Horse — yes, that's the company's name — has now revealed details of what'll power the car much of automotive media has unofficially took to calling the new Esprit.

Lotus' first proper supercar will use an engine called the Horse W40, a twin-turbo 4.0-liter V8 making more than 670 hp and 590 lb-ft of torque by itself. Weighing 441 pounds, this V8 is engineered for longitudinal layouts and has its two turbos mounted on top in a "hot-V" configuration — that's when the turbos sit inside the "V" of the engine, minimizing the distance gases have to travel and thereby minimizing turbo lag. Horse says the W40 makes "exceptional low-end torque along with high peak power" and is ideal for everything from sports cars to pickups and off-roaders, but of course it'd say that.

This engine is also said to be designed from the get-go to work as part of a hybrid powertrain, and Horse also makes the 8-speed dual-clutch hybrid gearbox that it'll be paired to. Known as the Horse 8LDCT, this transmission can withstand up to 774 lb-ft of torque. It uses a "sports car-grade" wet dual-clutch, can pull of shifts in under 20 milliseconds, and gets a built-in silicon carbide control system and power module, plus a variable-displacement mechanical pump.

In case you're unfamiliar, Horse Powertrain is a joint venture between Renault and Lotus' Chinese parent company, Geely. Horse also happens to build that waterproof B20 four-cylinder that goes into Geely's floating Galaxy Cruiser 700 off-roader, and the W40 is the powertrain supplier's first V8.

Lotus says its new supercar is expected to be manufactured in Europe, get revealed in 2028, and start deliveries that same year.

#5771. 300 Glaciers Have Disappeared from the American West Since the Mid‑20th Century — the Survivors Have a Few Things in Common

From an airplane, most of the glaciers of the Rocky Mountain West look like small patches of white nestled into rugged landscape where the topography folds. The white is the snow cover, and lower down you can see the gray-blue bare ice.

In the middle of the 20th century, over 1,450 glaciers could be found in the contiguous U.S. Together, they covered 217 square miles (562 square kilometers) in six western states. Today, a comprehensive review my colleagues and I just published in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences shows their total area has shrunk by 35% and they are still shrinking, perhaps faster.

A little more than 300 glaciers have disappeared, way more than I had anticipated.

Those that survive reflect just how vulnerable mountain glaciers have become as global temperatures rise. Where glaciers once covered large expanses of the western mountains thousands of years ago, most of the remaining glaciers in the Rockies and Sierra Nevada are small and found in topographically protected places.

When people think of glaciers, they often picture huge ice streams with icebergs calving off the front, like you’ll find in Alaska or Greenland. In the Lower 48 states, with the exception of a few large glaciers in Washington, they’re much smaller.

We found that the average glacier area is about one-tenth of a square mile (about a quarter of a square kilometer) in size. The area of the largest glacier, found on Washington’s Mount Rainier, is about 4.3 square miles (11.24 square kilometers).

I was surprised to find that glaciers in the Rocky Mountains – those in Colorado, Montana and Wyoming and in the Sierra Nevada of California – subsist because of their unique position on the landscape, which somewhat insulates them from the otherwise less-favorable regional climate.

The surviving Rocky Mountain glaciers inhabit shaded niches less exposed to direct sunshine and gain additional snow from avalanches. If these glaciers were exposed to more sun and less snow accumulation, they likely wouldn’t exit at all.

We found the fastest-thinning glaciers are in the Beartooth-Absaroka Mountains of the Rockies in southern Montana, which are losing about 1.5 feet (about half a meter) of ice thickness per year.

It’s a different story in Washington, where the glaciers on Mount Rainier and Mount Baker are much larger, have cooler summers and get a lot more snowfall. Although they too are shrinking, they do so at a much slower rate, about half a foot of ice thickness per year. The glacier area on Mount Rainier alone is greater than the total sum in all the other states.

In Washington, generally speaking, the higher you go, the less glacier loss you find. That makes sense; it’s cooler at higher elevations. But in the Rocky Mountain region there is no similar trend, suggesting the survivors are niche glaciers that depend on local topographic benefits.

In a study a few years ago, colleagues and I modeled the glaciers on Washington’s Olympic Peninsula and found most would disappear by 2070 under current climate trends. That indicates that glaciers elsewhere are probably not far behind.

The Olympics are vulnerable because they’re close to the Pacific Ocean at somewhat lower elevation. The other Washington glaciers, particularly on Mount Rainier and Mount Baker, will likely survive past 2100, but how much longer is an open question.

The principal importance of the glaciers is to provide water to high alpine ecosystems. The glaciers partially melt in late summer, providing cool water for cold-adapted fish and other stream life. They provide a water supply that’s otherwise absent in the dry late summer, after the seasonal snow disappears, when the region doesn’t get a lot of rain.

While the glaciers of the Rockies are primarily important for ecosystems at this point, those in Washington also contribute to hydropower production.

An important hazard is also associated with the glaciers on the steep-sided volcanoes of the Cascade Range, which stretches from California to Washington. The moraines formed by these glaciers are large, partly due to the highly erodible volcanic rock beneath the ice. Moving ice deposits eroded rocks, forming aprons at their lower ends. As the glaciers retreat, they leave the accumulated rocks behind as steep-sided moraines that are no longer buttressed by the ice.

High water flows from rain or glacier melt can undercut the moraines, triggering landslides and creating debris flows that go rocketing down the mountains. This happened in 2006 on Mount Hood and Mount Rainier and closed a number of highways. We’ll see that happen more often as the glaciers retreat.

The shrinking and eventual loss of all of these glaciers will affect ecosystems across the West, as well as waterways humans and wildlife rely on.

By Andrew Fountain, Professor of Geography, Portland State University
