#5770. Average New Car Price Hits New Record High (And Even The Good News Doesn't Sound So Good)

Great news everyone! We just set another new record, and this time it has nothing to do with diesel prices. Sadly, though, it isn't nearly as cool as the Chevrolet Corvette ZR1X destroying the Pikes Peak production car record over the summer. No, unfortunately for everyone who still has to work for a living, Kelley Blue Book reports the average transaction price for new cars hasn't just crossed the $50,000 mark. In October, the average transaction price rose to a level never before seen in the U.S. So that's pretty exciting. Aren't you thrilled to be alive right now to experience it?

Using data from its parent company, Cox Automotive, KBB says the average price consumers paid for new cars in the U.S. hit $51,109 in the month of October. That's 1.3% higher than it was in September and a 2.1% increase year-over-year. Higher prices aren't usually a good thing, especially when those higher prices are on things most people need to use every day (cars) instead of luxuries that you don't have to own to participate in society. The average MSRP of new vehicles is also up, reportedly hitting $52,933 in October, up 1.4% versus September and 2.4% higher year-over-year.

That said, it isn't all bad news. There's always a silver lining, and this time around, KBB says, "The Kelley Blue Book ATP estimate marked an all-time high even as vehicle price inflation in October was below long-term averages." Yes, you read that right. The average price people are paying for new cars hit an all-time high in October, but at least the rate of inflation isn't as high as it could be. Don't you feel grateful for that? I know I do. From the sound of it, dealers are also perfectly happy to let customers pay those high prices, as opposed to chasing faster turnover:

The higher ATP – an estimate of the average product prices paid in October before all available incentives, taxes and fees are factored in – was driven in part by both price inflation and strong sales of expensive midsize and full-size SUVs and full-size pickup trucks. Incentive spending increased modestly in October but remains below long-term averages, suggesting automakers and dealers are not aggressively pursuing sales and inventory reduction, despite record prices.

KBB also found that incentive spending accounted for 6.8% of ATP in October. With September 2026 at 6.7% and October 2025 at 6.5%, incentives are trending upward but it's been slow and doesn't appear to have contributed significantly to October's ATP record. Plus, not every car costs the same, and some segments will naturally cost less to buy than others. Once you break pricing out by segment, only one really feels like an outlier. Here's how KBB's breakdown looks:

As you can see, midsize SUVs were number one in sales volume and had an ATP of $50,570, but the people buying compact cars only paid $28,000 for them on average. That probably seems high for a compact, but remember, a 2027 Nissan Sentra without any options has an MSRP of almost $25,000 with destination, and an extra $3,000 to drive something nicer than a stripped-out, base-model Sentra sounds pretty fair to me.

Meanwhile, full-size pickup trucks placed third behind midsize and compact SUVs, but the average transaction price is close to double what people are paying for Honda CR-Vs and Hyundai Tucsons. Yes, on average, full-size truck buyers pay $67,912 for their trucks. Some Ford F-150s will cost more, some Chevrolet Silverados will cost less, but on average a full-size pickup truck buyer spent more than $65,000 to buy their truck.

That said, it's also worth reminding everyone that if you adjust for inflation, the 1997 Toyota Camry cost more than a brand new 2026 Camry does today. Truck buyers' obsession with expensive trucks skews the average, but if raises had kept up with inflation, it wouldn't be nearly as scary to hear the Camry now starts at $30,995. Cars haven't gotten too expensive. Our bosses denying us raises has simply left us too poor to afford these new cars. Meanwhile, they're out there buying $70,000 trucks like its nothing. Or at least they did before Woke 3 arrived. Maybe under Woke 3, we're all getting raises.

#5769. Strengthening Healthcare Delivery Through Proactive Primary Care

Strengthening Healthcare Delivery Through Proactive Primary Care" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

A stroke can happen without warning. In the months beforehand, however, there may have been a blood pressure reading that needed attention, a prescription that lapsed or a follow-up visit that never got scheduled. Primary care is usually where those signals surface first, and where there is the best chance to act before an emergency.

Much of that work happens between appointments. Care teams review charts ahead of visits, call patients after a hospital discharge and follow up on overdue screenings. Traditional fee-for-service pays for the visit itself but offers little support for the proactive work around it. Value-based care shifts the focus from the volume of services delivered to the quality, outcomes and total cost of care. By aligning financial incentives with prevention, it helps keep patients healthy and out of the hospital while strengthening the long-term financial sustainability of the practice.

For people with traditional Medicare, the Medicare Shared Savings Program (MSSP) enables primary care practices to be paid for that preventive work. MSSP is a permanent, voluntary accountable care program run within traditional Medicare by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS). Physician groups, health systems and community health centers participate by joining an accountable care organization (ACO), which takes responsibility for the quality and total cost of care for its assigned patients.

Clinicians continue to bill Medicare for covered services under fee-for-service, and MSSP adds a layer of value-based savings on top. When proactive primary care lowers the total cost of patients' care against a historical benchmark, often by preventing avoidable hospitalizations and emergency visits, and the ACO meets quality standards, Medicare shares a portion of those savings with the ACO. Because savings stem directly from keeping people healthy, clinical goals and financial rewards finally pull in the same direction. Shared savings are not guaranteed and depend on multiple factors.

The program operates at significant scale. In performance year 2026, 511 ACOs are serving 12.6 million people with traditional Medicare.¹ In performance year 2024, 75% of participating ACOs earned shared savings, and the program produced roughly $2.5 billion in net savings for Medicare.²

Physician-led value-based care puts primary care physicians and practices at the center of care delivery and decision-making. Shared savings give primary care organizations a revenue stream tied to prevention, which they can reinvest in staff, outreach and technology.

An ACO enablement partner can provide the data, care model support and operational infrastructure needed to identify patient needs, coordinate care and manage the ACO's reporting to Medicare, while participating primary care organizations retain control over how they deliver care. Aledade is one such partner. In fact, practices with five or more years with Aledade deliver approximately three times higher savings than the national average for non-Aledade ACOs of similar tenure.3

With that support and the actionable insights and clinical guidance it brings, primary care teams can reach the right patient at the right time, and the results show up as an emergency visit avoided, a stroke prevented or a hospital stay that never happened. For patients, prevention can mean more birthdays, more holidays and more time with the people they love.

Aledade is a physician-led value-based care company and the leading value-based care participant in MSSP.4 Aledade works alongside primary care organizations to put proactive care into practice through true financial alignment. Within its ACOs, savings are generally allocated approximately 50% to Aledade and 50% to its practice partners, with individual practice distributions weighted by quality performance and other contractual terms.⁵ That means Aledade succeeds only when its primary care partners do.

For more than a decade, Aledade has partnered with thousands of primary care organizations through the ACOs it forms and manages, providing data, technology, care model support and operational infrastructure. Together, these capabilities help clinicians identify patient needs earlier, coordinate care and build a sustainable financial model that helps direct more revenue to primary care. When primary care is financially incentivized to keep patients healthy, warning signs can be caught earlier and life-threatening events can be prevented.

1 2026 Medicare Accountable Care Organization Initiatives Participation Highlights, CMS.

2 CMS Proposes Transformational Medicare Reforms to Expand Accountable Care, CMS, July 2026.

3 Aledade analysis of CMS Public Use File of MSSP annual results through 2024; for Aledade data incorporates private CMS data for 2025; weighted average of performance for all data 2015+, incorporates Aledade 2016+ cohorts.

4 Based on internal analysis of data from the 2024 CMS ACO Public Use File.

5 Based on Aledade's standard ACO contractual terms as of January 1, 2026.

#5767. SpaceX Wants To Build Data Center Powered By Its Newest Product: Natural Gas

SpaceX filed its intention to build a new AI data center at its facility in Starbase, Texas, which will be powered by a new natural gas plant that it will also build. None of that sounds like space, but this is the company that SpaceX has become. The initial phase of this build-out is projected to cost $24 billion, though more phases are to come. Critically, this gives the rocket company, which is also an internet company, which is also an AI company, which is also a social media company, a way to make use of its newest product: natural gas. Again, space!

The company has been moving towards an expansion into fossil fuels for the last few months. In June, SpaceX president Gwynne Shotwell said the company was making plans for "basically mining our own natural gas... to develop our own propellant and bring it to the rocket." The rocket in question being Starship, which SpaceX is essentially betting its future on. That takes a mixture of methane and liquid oxygen to fire up its thrusters; methane can be extracted from natural gas. For that reason, the company has made major moves to ensure its own supply. It hired for its own natural gas trader, normally an oil industry job for buying and selling the stuff, and is building its own pipelines in both Florida and Texas, per Bloomberg.

In addition, SpaceX rapidly spun up AI data centers in Tennessee and Mississippi earlier this year, powered by, you guessed it, natural gas. It spun them up so rapidly that the company is now being sued for ignoring environmental laws. SpaceX is apparently trying to go fast again for this new Texas endeavor, likely trying to get things rolling before a newer, bluer state government takes over. SpaceX wants to grab both the air pollution permits it needs plus a few tax breaks; Texas is expected to make both harder to get early next year, per Politico. To that end, Starbase's city government will convene on the matter in December. There's good reason to think the company will get what it wants: two of the city's three commission members are SpaceX executives. That's handy.

As for the power plant itself, it will feature 35 mobile natural gas turbines and produce around 750 megawatts of electricity. So it's now in the business of building power plants, buying and trading natural gas, and laying out pipelines, all in service of the enough data centers it's putting up. No wonder Vice President of Launch Kiko Dontchev is now saying, "Fundamentally we are an infrastructure company at this point." You don't say, Dontchev.

At this point, it's not too much of a stretch to say that SpaceX is just the Elon Musk Show, going into whatever industry he feels like at the moment. If his long-desired merger with Tesla goes through, then it will really just be a conglomerate with all sorts of fingers in all sorts of pies. Space will still be a part of what SpaceX does, but now that AI is earning it more revenue than rockets, it may become a smaller and smaller part. Mars has never looked so far away.

#5766. Democrats Are at COP31 to Show That the US Has Not Abandoned the Fight Against Climate Change

The results of the US midterms on November 3 delivered a resounding victory for the Democrats, and a major loss for US president Donald Trump and the Republican party.

Gaining control of the Senate and the House of Representatives has emboldened the Democrats, giving them a renewed sense of political legitimacy and a mandate to pursue their agenda. But will this shift translate into meaningful changes in US climate change policy?

The Democratic sweep will change the political dynamics around US climate policy and how the country cooperates with the international community on this issue. Although there was no official US presence at the 2025 COP conference, held in Brazil, a coalition of 100 local US leaders, including governors, mayors and other top city and state officials, attended as part of the US Climate Alliance. Former US vice-president Al Gore, and former US secretary of state John Kerry are part of an unofficial US group at the 2026 gathering.

Kerry said in a speech there: “Americans are committed to taking back their future, and we will build and rebuild our partnerships as we do that.”

Being on the ground at COP is important. As the US has a decisive role to play in global climate policy, sending a non-official coalition signals that many Americans are still working to combat climate change.

Trump has repeatedly stated that climate change is a hoax and refused to attend the COP31 climate talks being held in Turkey. But 20 Democratic members of Congress will be attending, although they have been denied official state department funding for the trip.

During the first and second Trump administrations, the US has been an outlier among wealthy countries when it comes to fighting climate change. Trump has actively backed the use of fossil fuels, undermining clean energy projects, and rolled back legislation that stipulated emission standards for vehicles.

Trump also weakened greenhouse gas reporting requirements and methane regulation. Then in February 2026, the Trump administration rescinded the Environmental Protection Agency’s 2009 finding that greenhouse gases threaten the health and welfare of the public, which had regulated the emissions from new cars and other vehicles. This had provided the legal foundation for regulation under the Clean Air Act.

On the international front, Trump was equally determined to undermine global efforts to fight climate change. Famously, he withdrew from the Paris agreement, part of a global initiative to address climate change, and refused to attend COP summits. But the Democrats’ seismic midterm win may be a sign of optimism for those fighting to address climate change.

The biggest issues of the midterms were affordability, corruption and the Iran war, but issues related to fighting climate change were also on voters’ list. There is growing concern about data centres. As many as 56% of US voters felt that data centres and AI were either a major or even crucial factor in influencing who they voted for, with only 13% saying it was not a factor at all.

Data centres require millions of gallons of water for cooling systems, and this is expected to rise to 73 billion litres of water a year by 2028. Over half of planned AI facilities in the US will be situated in areas that have significant water shortages or drought conditions.

After the midterms, Zoe Lofgren, a Democrat, is likely to become the chair of the House of Representatives’ committee on science, space and technology once the new session starts in January. She wants to tackle the administration’s “climate denialism”.

Lofgren’s leadership, along with new Democratic members of the committee, may offer it more power to reverse some of the Trump administration’s changes to science policy.

However, the Democrat midterm wins will not mean that the US is automatically back at the negotiating table at COP nor that the US will be able to rejoin the Paris Accords while Trump is president. Trump still has the power to make these decisions and can continue to keep the US out of international climate initiatives, regardless of who controls Congress. The US Constitution says nothing about withdrawing from treaties, which creates uncertainty for what the Senate’s role might be.

The election, however, does signal that there is considerable domestic opposition to his approach. And while Congress has not passed follow-up legislation prohibiting presidential withdrawal from the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), it has continued to fund several UNFCCC initiatives. During the Biden administration, the US significantly increased climate finance from US$1.5 billion (£1.1 billion) a year in 2021 to US$9.5 billion in 2023.

Democrats could also push back on Trump’s current policies, using their power to hold hearings, investigate the administration’s dismantling of climate programmes and challenge cuts to scientific research. The Democrats can also use the power of Congress’s control of spending to protect or restore funding to some of these environmental initiatives.

They could also try to introduce legislation that reinstates some of the clean energy initiatives that had been implemented in past administrations to strengthen emissions standards and support clean energy. The big issue, however, is that Trump has made it clear that he is going to be very active in using his veto power, and without a two-thirds majority in Congress, the Democrats will not be able to override this.

What this all means is that the world cannot expect the US to be a reliable and constructive partner at COP with Trump still in power. Trump will probably continue to forge ahead with expanding fossil fuel production, while weakening international climate cooperation, even if the electorate disapproves of these policies.

Ironically, Trump’s greatest contribution to the fight against climate change was his initiation of the war with Iran, which helped to cut global fossil fuel carbon dioxide emissions by 0.4% due the closure of the Strait of Hormuz. But climate scientists note this is not a long-term solution.

While the Democrats’ electoral victory offers a glimmer of hope for renewed climate action, both domestically and internationally, restoring US climate leadership will take time, especially while Trump remains in power.

By Natasha Lindstaedt, Professor in the Department of Government, University of Essex

#5765. From Fragmentation to Integration: A New Model for Specialty Care

From Fragmentation to Integration: A New Model for Specialty Care" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Health systems are navigating growing demands with increasingly limited resources. As costs rise and teams are stretched, technology has become essential to helping people work smarter. But technology alone isn’t the answer. Many health systems and specialty care teams are discovering that more tools don’t always mean a more efficient process or better patient outcomes.

Approximately 25% of healthcare spending in the U.S. goes toward administrative expenses. At the same time, 60% of hospitals and health systems use more than 50 software solutions for operations. Yet more technology hasn’t meant less complexity. Care teams still navigate disconnected tools, siloed data, fragmented workflows, and manual handoffs. The opportunity isn’t to add more technology; it’s to make everything work better together.

Health systems want to better manage the specialty patient journey, but administrative burdens get in the way of more coordinated care. What they need is an integrated approach – one that improves patient experiences and outcomes by connecting workflows through technology and adding dedicated human expertise to serve as an extension of existing care teams.

When specialty care teams spend more time navigating processes and technology than focusing on patients, trust erodes, costs rise, and turnover climbs. It also limits teams’ ability to apply the clinical expertise and personalized attention that complex specialty care requires. This friction shows up in three core areas:

The rapid adoption of specialized software and standalone tools for tasks such as scheduling, patient intake, and remote monitoring fragments care delivery. Teams constantly toggle between platforms, manually piecing together a single patient’s record. This can contribute to cognitive overload among care teams, a factor in burnout, and it diverts valuable time and attention from clinical decision-making and personalized patient support that require human expertise.

Point solutions may solve individual challenges, but they often operate in isolation. When systems don’t fully connect, patient information becomes fragmented across applications, leaving specialty care teams without visibility into the full context of a patient’s broader care journey. This can increase the risk of redundant testing, duplicated work, and inaccurate reporting, while making it harder for clinicians to identify when patients may need additional interventions.

The care journey includes multiple critical transitions, from cross-departmental referrals to prescription routing to external coordination. Despite healthcare’s digital process, these transitions still rely too frequently on faxes, phone calls, and manual spreadsheets. The added administrative burden makes it harder for care teams to coordinate timely interventions and provide consistent, personalized support. Without an integrated model that connects the entire care journey, referrals or post-procedure care plans can fall through the cracks.

Complexity comes at a cost: time teams could be spending on patient care. According to AMA research, for every hour physicians spend with patients, they spend nearly two more on EHR and desk work.

For specialty care teams, disconnected technology and fragmented workflows can make it even harder to see the full picture of a patient’s care. Patients with multiple chronic conditions, for example, often see several specialists. When any one of them lacks complete visibility into the patient’s full treatment plan, specialty performance breaks down. The risk of prescribing therapies that shouldn't be combined rises, potentially compromising patient safety and delaying therapy.

Solving today’s specialty care challenges requires more than another point solution. It requires a connected operating model that brings workflows, information and expertise together, giving care teams the visibility and support they need to act with greater confidence and keep patients moving forward.

Shields Health Solutions’ integrated care model combines technology, data, and clinical expertise to reduce administrative burden, streamline care coordination, and provide specialty care teams with more time to focus on patients. Rather than relying on technology alone, Shields’ clinical pharmacists and liaisons work as an extension of specialty care teams. They translate insights into action, resolving barriers to therapy, coordinating care, and supporting patients throughout treatment.

That combination of human expertise and connected technology delivers measurable results. With average time to therapy in under 48 hours compared with the seven-to-10-day industry average, Shields’ integrated care model drives connected performance, alleviating operational strain on care teams while improving access to therapy, better patient outcomes, and long-term growth.

#5764. The Bat Signal Is Calling: Batman's 'The Dark Knight' Motorcycle Hits The Auction Block

The season of giving is almost here, and the perfect gift for the Batman fans in your life, or shamelessly just you, might be this very real Batpod. The Batman motorcycle, featured in Christopher Nolan's "The Dark Knight" and "The Dark Knight Rises" just so happens to be on on auction right now with Propstore, as part of its Entertainment Memorabilia Live Auction in London. It also happens to close just in time to get it home and under whatever shrine you celebrate for the winter solstice.

Originally sold by Propstore in 2016 as part of its Entertainment Memorabilia Auction, this is the bike used in notable "The Dark Knight" moments such as when Batman (Christian Bale) evaded the Joker (Heath Ledger), demonstrating the Batpod's abilities to climb walls and turn around without moving forward thanks to the wheels' ability to flip side-to-side, making the Hummer's crabwalking capabilities look like ancient technology. In "The Dark Knight Rises," Batman gave Catwoman (Anne Hathaway) the Batpod to use as a getaway vehicle while he took on Bane and the League of Shadows.

Batman's Batpod was born from the minds of Nolan and production designer for the films, Nathan Crowley, whose work has included "Wonka," the "Wicked" films, Dunkirk," "Westworld," and "Interstellar." It was made to be an alternative for Batman's Tumbler, the armored-tank vehicle that happens to also be quite multi-faceted, like when it transformed into a heavy duty flight vehicle to escape Gotham Police as they cornered Batman in an alley. The man who brought the bike to life was Chris Corbould, who was special-effects supervisor for both films but might be best known for his work on the James Bond film series and "Inception," for which he won an Academy award.

The Batpod is being sold as-is, in non-functional, "rolling only" condition with no fluids, battery, fuel tank or throttle controls. You'd have to be fairly innovative to get it moving. That might be for the best, though, as the rider position is prone. If you were to pilot it, you'd be lying down in a stance more awkward than a MotoGP rider and more like a sprint stance used in spinning classes, then forced to steer with your arms and shoulders. At least there is a little bit of black foam lining the body, saddle, and knee rests to give your body a little respite.

The custom-fabricated chassis wears 31-inch Hoosier racing tires and a Honda 750 engine. Its cannon barrels are made of plastic, resin and fiberglass, and it also has a light-up laser-targeting unit above the right hand grip, which doesn't actually work. Despite its nonworking state, it at least bears some battle scars from its use in scenes between both movies.

Included in the sale is a custom metal support stand for display, as well as a certificate of authenticity from Warner Brothers. Bids for this Hero Batpod close December 4 at 10:00 a.m. EST. As of this writing bids sit at around $300,000, with Propstore estimating the sale to range between around $600,000 to $1.2 million. If that's too rich for your taste, maybe you could look into building your own Batpod.

#5763. What the VA Gets Right About Death

— Its palliative care model protects the final, fragile moments families have together

On November 11, 1991, I called my father, a taciturn 24-year senior non-commissioned officer, to wish him a happy Veterans Day. I was a newly minted second lieutenant, and his pride in my becoming an officer and first-generation college graduate was palpable. That call evolved into an annual tradition spanning 34 years.

As I mourn him this Veterans Day, I'm grateful for what the system got right during the terminal months of his illness. It is easy to parrot the critique that the Department of Veterans Affairs (VA) is a broken, bureaucratic mess. But at its best, the VA healthcare system is a model of compassion that guards the dignity of those who served.

As a trauma surgeon and critical care specialist, I saw how the VA gave my father something I can't guarantee my own patients: superb hospice care that allowed him to die with dignity in the comfort of his own home. This dignified, holistic approach is a national model of excellence that proves why threats to privatize the system are a dangerous mistake.

This level of care is by design. In 2003, the VA did something revolutionary and became the nation's first health network to launch a systemwide palliative care mandate. That policy required every VA medical center -- whether a massive urban hospital or a small regional clinic -- to establish interdisciplinary teams dedicated to the physical, psychological, and spiritual needs of dying veterans and their families. As the largest integrated health network in the nation, the VA transformed the interdisciplinary model from a localized specialty into a universally funded standard of care.

The VA is often derided, and some of the criticism is valid: Long wait times for routine care and inconsistent quality have fueled the push to privatize the system. But those same criticisms apply to the many ivory-tower healthcare systems where I have worked: Insurance coverage gaps, delays caused by prior authorization reviews, and a national shortage of palliative care specialists are all barriers to care in the private sector.

In my three decades of medicine, no system where I've worked surpasses the VA for end-of-life care. The system operates on a massive scale -- nearly 86% of veterans access hospice or palliative care in their final 6 months of life -- and provides superb quality. Families consistently rate the VA's care higher in satisfaction than civilian alternatives. By working in tandem with local hospice agencies, the VA manages a patient's physical pain while wrapping the entire family in bereavement support. In practice, this model elevates end-of-life care for our veterans in three critical ways.

First is speed when it matters most. While a veteran might wait months for a routine specialty appointment, the system moves with impressive urgency at the end of life. Palliative and hospice consults bypass the notorious waitlists. When a veteran is dying, red tape is stripped away. End-of-life care is triaged as an acute, time-critical medical need.

Second is the shield against bureaucracy. End-of-life care in the VA is managed by dedicated interdisciplinary teams -- physicians, nurses, social workers, and chaplains -- who act as fierce advocates. They handle administrative heavy lifting, insulating families so they can focus on their loved ones. By removing the burden of navigating a complex medical system, these teams give families the invaluable gift of uninterrupted time together.

Third, the VA offers the advantage of concurrent care -- this is where the VA truly outperforms the private sector. Under standard Medicare rules, patients are usually forced to forgo life-prolonging treatments to qualify for hospice. The VA allows veterans to receive disease-modifying therapies, like palliative radiation or dialysis, while also receiving hospice support.

To die peacefully at home with autonomy is a blessing many Americans are denied. In this respect, we are doing right by our veterans. Were it not for the VA, my father could have been languishing in a hospital bed -- the fate of too many of my own dying patients. Instead of gutting the VA through privatization, Congress must fully fund it. Even better, civilian Medicare should adopt the VA's humane concurrent care model for all Americans, a move that would reduce the costly emergency department visits and lengthy intensive care stays I see in my practice.

Ultimately, the VA's superior palliative model protects the final, fragile moments families have together. Because they handled the bureaucracy, my final memories aren't of fighting insurance companies, as many of my patients' families do, but of just being his son.

There are so many things I wish I could do with my father one last time: fish with him off the base pier, listen to him belt off-pitch to Prince's "Purple Rain," or take our annual birthday trip to Vegas for the breakfast buffets, blackjack, and steak dinners he loved.

Still, knowing he was granted the dignity he deserved at the end, I would trade all of that for one more chance to pick up the phone today, call home, and hear his raspy laugh when I say, "Happy Veterans Day, Dad."

#5762. The Trump Administration’s Pro-Cancer Agenda

Under Trump, Americans could face an extraordinary increase in exposure to cancer-causing toxics and radiation.

Over the nearly two years of President Donald Trump’s second term, his administration has taken more than 500 actions that harm public health or the environment. When faced with this onslaught of harm, it can be difficult to see the strategic agenda at play. However, NRDC’s new analysis reveals that the Trump administration is executing a sweeping and long planned—but quiet—government-wide campaign to dismember major programs that are intended to prevent exposure to cancer-causing toxics and radiation. In sum, this administration has reversed course: America’s government is no longer fighting a “War on Cancer” but fighting a “War to Promote Cancer.”

This is not an accident. Much of this campaign can be found throughout the Heritage Foundation’s Project 2025 blueprint and has long been advocated for by industry. But a healthy life should not be treated as an obstacle to somebody else’s profit margin. The freedom to breathe clean air, drink safe water, eat safe food, work without needless exposure to carcinogens, and raise children without avoidable toxic risks should be among the most basic expectations of life in America. Americans should not have to wonder whether the water coming from their taps contains cancer-causing chemicals, whether the air they breathe carries toxic pollution from a nearby industrial facility, or whether going to work means being unnecessarily exposed to carcinogens.

Instead of ensuring these basic freedoms, the Trump administration is creating a system in which polluters are given greater leeway to expose everyone in the nation to cancer-causing toxics and radiation.

#5761. Deerfield taps AWS to fuel healthcare AI ambitions

Deerfield taps AWS to fuel healthcare AI ambitions" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Investment firm Deerfield Management is tapping into Amazon Web Services' AI and cloud technology to help AI-native healthcare and life sciences companies build and scale.

The collaboration brings together AWS’s generative AI and cloud services with the investment firm's team of AI engineers and the innovation ecosystem of the firm’s affiliate, Cure.

The firm’s Deerfield Intelligence and Deerfield Discovery and Development (3DC) teams will receive access to AI and cloud technology from AWS, as well as technical support, to scale proprietary healthcare AI products. Deerfield 3DC is the firm's internal research and development engine, while Deerfield Intelligence is the firm's data, technology and AI arm.

Select portfolio companies will get access to AWS' technical enablement and training on topics including large-scale model training, inference optimization and healthcare-specific managed services.

"This collaboration aims to drive the development of the next generation of AI-powered healthcare organizations,” said Dan Sheeran, vice president and general manager of Healthcare and Life Sciences at AWS, in a statement. "With support from AWS, interested Deerfield portfolio companies can be equipped with AI services specific to supporting healthcare and life sciences needs like Amazon Bio Discovery, as well as agentic services like Amazon Bedrock AgentCore to build AI agents and support organizations at a scale that wouldn’t have been feasible only a few years ago."

RELATED: Deerfield unveils $600M for emerging healthcare therapeutics and tech

Deerfield is working to build an ambitious AI-native platform in healthcare, according to Jim Flynn, managing partner at Deerfield. "Our Intelligence team is developing models that aim to transform how we source opportunities, evaluate science, and accelerate portfolio companies," he said.

The investment firm supports a portfolio of more than 200 private and public healthcare and life sciences investments. In May 2025, Deerfield unveiled the third installment of its healthcare innovations fund, earmarking more than $600 million for promising therapeutics and technologies.

Through the AWS partnership, Deerfield is taking steps to build AI infrastructure across its investment ecosystem. AWS will become a common technology layer that Deerfield can use internally and make available to portfolio companies, potentially helping startups move from AI development to deployment more quickly.

Ascertain, a Deerfield-backed healthcare technology company, is already using AWS infrastructure for its agentic AI platform, which automates workflows such as case management and prior authorization.

"We built Ascertain to give clinicians time back by automating the administrative burden in care, and we believe AWS’s purpose-built services for the healthcare industry will help us scale these capabilities," said Mark Michalski, M.D., chief information officer at Deerfield and CEO of Ascertain, in a statement. "We believe Deerfield’s collaboration with AWS and the AI and cloud services it makes available to Ascertain and other portfolio companies is part of a foundation with the potential to make AI-driven transformation possible at scale."

Deerfield’s headquarters at Cure, a healthcare innovation campus in New York City, sits at the center of one of the world’s densest healthcare ecosystems, home to leading academic medical centers, a growing biotech sector, and a deep network of clinicians and investors, according to the investment firm.

#5760. After CAR-T Failure in Diffuse Large B-Cell Lymphoma, What Comes Next?

— Bispecific antibodies have become the go-to option

CD19-targeted chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cells have emerged as a second- and later-line treatment strategy for patients with diffuse large B-cell lymphoma (DLBCL), with three FDA-approved products.

"Across CAR T-cell products in patients with large B-cell lymphoma, we see durable, ongoing responses in about 30% to 40% of patients," Alex Herrera, MD, of City of Hope Comprehensive Cancer Center in Duarte, California, told MedPage Today.

A recent landmark trial found that 12 of 38 patients -- half with heavily pretreated large B-cell lymphoma -- remained lymphoma-free 10 years after receiving a single dose of CD19-targeted CAR T-cell therapy.

However, "a majority of patients ultimately do not achieve long-term remission with CAR-T," Herrera observed.

For example, 55 of 116 people with aggressive B-cell lymphomas in a cohort study had treatment failure, with about half of those patients relapsing within 8 months of receiving CAR T-cell therapy.

"CAR T-cell therapy has offered hope to a lot of patients, but has created a whole new unmet need," said Lorenzo Falchi, MD, of Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center in New York City. "What happens for that majority of patients in relapse?"

As for what might come next, he noted that chemotherapy would have been first- and second-line options for DLBCL in the past, and patients might have received several different types.

"Now, where you have CAR T-cell therapy, patients might in the third line have seen only one line of chemotherapy," Falchi told MedPage Today. "So, there is space for chemotherapy as a salvage option."

That said, the most likely option would be a bispecific antibody targeting CD20 and CD3. Epcoritamab (Epkinly) and glofitamab (Columvi) have received FDA approval for relapsed/refractory DLBCL after two or more lines of therapy, and have "become a standard in that post-CAR-T space," Herrera said.

These drugs have transformative potential for several reasons, Falchi noted. "Most important is that in patients who experience the failure of CAR T-cell therapy, as many as 35% to 40% can achieve a complete response with bispecific antibodies used alone," he said. "That is not achieved with anything else in that space, including chemotherapy."

In a phase I/II study, 35% of 52 patients with relapsed or refractory DLBCL who had previously received CAR T-cell therapy achieved a complete response with glofitamab monotherapy. "And we know now with longer-term follow-up that among the patients who respond, a good majority remain in response in the long term," Falchi added.

However, even with a promising therapy like a bispecific antibody, efficacy is likely limited in patients who had early relapse or progression on CAR T-cell therapy.

"If a patient never responds or has a very rapid progression right after CAR T-cell therapy, that in and of itself is associated with a poor prognosis," Herrera pointed out. "That's just logical, right? The therapy really didn't work, and kinetically the disease is progressing very rapidly. We know that those patients are less likely to respond to whatever we give them next."

In a retrospective analysis of patients with large B-cell lymphoma who experienced relapse after CD19-directed CAR T-cell therapy, those receiving bispecific antibodies during early relapse (≤3 months) achieved a significantly worse outcome, with an overall response rate (ORR) of 29% and progression-free survival (PFS) of 2.2 months, compared with patients with an intermediate (4-6 months; ORR 54%, PFS 3.7 months) or late relapse (>6 months; ORR 60%, PFS 10.5 months).

Antibody-drug conjugates such as polatuzumab vedotin (Polivy) and loncastuximab tesirine (Zynlonta) provide another option after progression on CAR T-cell therapy.

A "cutting-edge" approach is to combine them with bispecific antibodies, Falchi said. "That's where we're seeing some of the highest response rates and some of the more tolerable safety profiles."

For example, in a phase Ib/II trial of glofitamab plus polatuzumab vedotin, the ORR was 78.3%, with a complete response rate of 59.7%, among patients with relapsed/refractory large B-cell lymphoma, including patients with previous CAR T-cell therapy failure.

Several studies being presented at the upcoming American Society of Hematology (ASH) annual meeting will address relapsed/refractory DLBCL, including patients who received prior CAR T-cell therapy.

Initial results from a study evaluating venetoclax (Venclexta), ibrutinib (Imbruvica), prednisone, obinutuzumab (Gazyva), and lenalidomide (Revlimid) in patients with relapsed/refractory DLBCL, including 20 patients with prior CAR T-cell therapy, showed the regimen achieved an ORR of 54%, with 38% of patients achieving a complete response.

In long-term results being presented at ASH, 65% of all responses and 78% of complete responses were ongoing at 5 years.

Falchi also pointed out that recently released results from the phase III EPCORE DLBCL-2 trial had a "very strong potential to change the standard of care" for frontline therapy.

In this study of newly diagnosed patients with DLBCL, fixed-duration epcoritamab in combination with standard-of-care rituximab, cyclophosphamide, doxorubicin, vincristine, and prednisone (R-CHOP) reduced the risk of disease progression or death by 51% compared with R-CHOP alone (HR 0.49, 95% CI 0.36-0.67, P<0.0001), "which is kind of unprecedented, really, in the frontline space," Falchi noted, adding that the study is "almost certainly going to change practice."

"And what that will do is move many of the patients who would have gotten bispecifics in the third line into the front line, and that will create another new unmet need because now you've used them in the front line," he said.

Falchi added that he and his colleagues are waiting for readouts on a half-dozen or so randomized trials, "and we're going to find ourselves with an embarrassment of riches."

"We're going to have maybe five or six positive frontline trials, and all of these trials are using some of the [therapies] that we're using now in the third or fourth line," he said. "So I truly think that this conversation will completely change in the next year or two."

#5759. Over One-Third Of Audi Nuvolari Production Will Be Sold In The U.S.

The 2027 Audi Nuvolari isn't a successor to the R8 because whereas that supercar was mass-produced in the tens of thousands over 18 years and two generations, just 499 Nuvolaris will ever be made. Audi has now announced that 182 of those are earmarked for the U.S., which means 36.5% of all Nuvolaris will land stateside, or one in every 2.74 cars.

Each will go for an official starting price of $700,000 before destination or taxes, and U.S. deliveries are scheduled to start in the first half of 2028. That's almost twice as much money as the Lamborghini Temerario on which it's based, but the Lambo isn't limited production or quite as special. Despite the numbered production run and how this sort of car tends to sell out before the public even knows about it, Audi of America says there are still build slots available, as "orders continue to be accepted at authorized Audi dealerships throughout the United States for a limited time."

Sharing its powertrain with the Temerario, the Audi Nuvolari uses a twin-turbo 4.0-liter V8 and three axial-flux hybrid electric motors to make 987 horsepower combined. 0 to 60 mph happens in just 2.5 seconds, and its top speed stands at 217 mph. It's said to use Formula 1-derived tech — Audi is in F1 now, in case you forgot — in the hybrid energy management system, by-wire brakes, active aero, and launch control programming.

It's also a new luxury performance car that manages to look good and original without being "polarizing." It's brutalist and a departure from existing Audi styling, but most colleagues I've talked to about it view its design favorably. For what it's worth, I think it's hella cool, particularly from the rear three-quarter view. Audi says there are 50 gloss paint colors and over 10 matte finishes, but you can of course make it look however you want with Audi Exclusive. You can get Audi's first exposed carbon-fiber body on the Nuvolari, and on the inside there's dozens more options.

In any case, if you're in the States and have 700 grand to splash on a German supercar (or, let's be real, almost a million after accounting for options, fees and taxes), there's apparently still time to go to your local Audi dealership and put your name down for a Nuvolari. You'd be joining a small club that only 181 other Americans will be a part of.

#5758. University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center unveils $3.4B expansion plans

University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center unveils $3.4B expansion plans" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The University of Texas MD Anderson Cancer Center is expanding its cancer care facilities through the addition of two new buildings—totaling a $3.4 billion investment.

The new facilities, dubbed Inspiration Tower and Prism, will make up nearly two million square feet and allow for MD Anderson to care for nearly 250,000 patients annually, executives say. The expansion is funded by UT MD Anderson and its philanthropic support.

“Every detail of these buildings, from the technology to the treatment rooms, was designed around our patients who will receive their care in them and our employees who will work in them,” said Jeffrey Lee, M.D., UT MD Anderson president ad interim, in a statement. “The facilities will serve as a physical representation of hope for the patients and their families who will walk through the doors.”

Lee said the two new facilities are the “future of cancer care,” adding the organization is grateful for support and guidance from the UT System Board of Regents.

“These facilities are part of the most impactful capital expansion in UT MD Anderson’s history,” Lee said.

Inspiration Tower will feature disease-specific clinics, with up to 330 exam rooms, 184 short-stay beds, 162 ambulatory treatment stations, 56 imaging rooms, 24 operating rooms and 38 procedure rooms. It is slated to open in 2032.

The 1.4 million-square-foot tower will feature 25 floors, making it the largest cancer-focused clinical facility in the U.S., according to a Nov. 5 press release.

Connected to Inspiration Tower by a sky-bridge, Prism will be a 292,000-square-foot hub dedicated to radiation oncology and diagnostic imaging. The facility will serve up to 150,000 patients annually and will feature 12 MRI machines, four CT scanners, two PET-CT units, 18 radiation units and seven radiation simulators, the release said.

“Every patient we treat deserves an environment that reflects the excellence of the care they receive at UT MD Anderson,” said Chris McKee, UT MD Anderson EVP and chief strategy officer, in a statement. “Our new facilities will be innovative, agile, adaptable and beautiful, and they will allow us to care for more patients, more effectively—in an environment that enhances healing and well-being. We are building for our future and for every patient who will be counting on us.”

#5757. To Honor Veterans, How About We Stop The Oil Wars?

Today is Veterans Day in the United States. Naturally, across the country, people have spent at least part of the day thinking about veterans they know as well as veterans they don’t and thanking them for their service. Whether you agree with the various wars the United States has gotten into or not, you have to honor and appreciate the human beings who offered to put their lives on the line in order to support their country (or think that they’re doing so, at least). I know several people who are veterans, and they typically sacrificed a lot in their previous military roles.

However, I do have to come back to the rationale for the wars we as a country get into. I’m 44 years old, and it seems like we’ve been involved in wars related to oil and gas for more than half of my life. It’s just a continual process. We get into a war in the Middle East connected to XYZ, but it’s really about oil. After years of over-involvement, destruction, and death, society realizes it’s been a super counterproductive, idiotic war and we pull out. The, after a few years, we begin again and the story repeats.

Well, how about we just stop getting involved in oil wars? How about we stop depending so much on oil supply from foreign nations and global oil markets? How about we respect our countrymen and countrywomen who sign up to serve by not sending them into pointless forever wars in the Middle East to get traumatized, injured, or killed? That would be truly honoring veterans of the past as well as protecting current and future Americans.

#5756. 1967 Ford Ranchero For Sale With 3,100 Miles Shown Is 351 V8 Swapped, Bright Red And Ready To Roll

I'm known around these parts as a ute fan. Any car you can dream up, I've probably come across it built into a ute, and I've almost certainly liked that version better than the original. Once in a while, though, we have to pay respects to the originators of the ute life — the El Caminos, the Holdens, and of course the ever-gorgeous Ford Rancheros. Take this one, for instance: A 351-swapped 1967 Fairlane Ranchero that's not a hideous restomod.

This Ranchero, while modified and maintained over the course of its life, is gloriously free from any terrible four-spoke steering wheels or gaudy blue LED gauges. It does have an air conditioning system of questionable provenance (the control/vent unit doesn't appear to be either the factory Ford in-dash option available in 1967 nor the period-appropriate round-vented dealer accessory) as well as a modern single-DIN radio, but those are welcome and unobtrusive changes — they don't distract from the classic character of the car. The wheels certainly do, but some five-spoke American Classics will fix that handily.

Under the hood, though, is the mod that really matters: The Ranchero's factory 289ci V8 engine and its associated three-speed manual gearbox have been replaced with a 351 ci V8 and a four on the floor (with a Hurst shifter, even). It's not technically a period-correct swap, as the 351 postdates this Ranchero by a couple of years, but it's close enough that I'll count it. Better than putting a Coyote under there and pretending that the Ford badging maintains the car's character.

This OEM-plus Ranchero is on Bring a Trailer right now at a bidding price of just $2,500, which falls firmly into "Amber wants this" territory. I'm going to need you all to bump that number up until this ute starts to look like more of a bad idea to me, or Jalopnik's local kei car and motorcycle girlie might just end up with a giant hunk of American muscle street-parked outside her apartment. Surely you'll save me from that fate, right?

#5754. The High-Rise Bottleneck: Why EV Charging in Indian Apartments Is an Engineering Puzzle

Most Western clean-energy reporting takes a very specific picture of home charging for granted. You pull into a private driveway, plug into an attached garage wall-box, and pull cheap power while you sleep.

That reality does not exist for most urban drivers in India.

Data from Kazam and the Alliance for an Energy Efficient Economy (AEEE) shows that roughly 70% to 75% of urban households in major Indian metros live in multi-family apartments and cooperative housing societies. Parking means an open ground level slot or a space two levels down in a concrete basement. When someone here buys an electric car, their biggest hurdle isn’t battery degradation or highway chargers. It’s their own building.

As an electrical engineer in India, I watch this play out every week. Someone buys an EV, assumes they can hire a local wireman to pull power from their flat’s meter to their parking bay, and runs straight into transformer limits, cable physics, and nervous managing committees.

Here is what is actually going on beneath the floorboards.

Indian housing societies buy electricity under a bulk “sanctioned load” from their local utility (DISCOM). The utility sizes the local transformer using an electrical diversity factor the simple assumption that not all 100 flats will run their air conditioners, geysers, and induction stoves at the exact same moment.

A domestic geyser pulls 2 kW for ten minutes and shuts off. A 7.4 kW AC wall-box pulls 32 amps continuously for six to eight hours. When several residents plug in around 8:00 PM, that heavy, unbroken draw lands on the transformer right as domestic air conditioning and kitchen loads hit their evening peak.

Let’s look at the numbers on a 100-flat complex running on a standard 250 kVA transformer:

Here is where the grid cracks: just five or six cars charging at 7.4 kW pull 37 kW to 44 kW. That wipes out the 30 kW buffer completely. If eight cars plug in at once (59.2 kW), the transformer goes straight into thermal overload.

When that happens, the main incomer breaker trips plunging common areas and elevators into darkness or the utility slaps the society with massive Maximum Demand penalties on their next power bill. Getting a utility to upgrade a transformer or approve higher sanctioned load requires significant capital, transformer yard space, and months of red tape.

In a detached house, wiring an EV charger means running 5 or 10 metres of cable from your distribution board to the garage.

In an Indian high-rise, your electricity meter sits in a ground-floor panel room or inside a service shaft on the 14th floor. Your car is parked two floors beneath the earth in Basement 2. Connecting the two is an expensive engineering problem:

Even if an owner is willing to foot the entire wiring bill, they still need a No Objection Certificate (NOC) from their housing society committee or Resident Welfare Association (RWA).

Most RWA committees are volunteer residents without technical backgrounds. When handed a request for high-power continuous charging, their hesitation usually comes down to three worries:

The courts are starting to step in. In Amit Dholakia v. The State of Maharashtra (2025), the Bombay High Court pushed state authorities to issue clear guidelines preventing arbitrary NOC rejections. More recently, in early 2026, the Supreme Court of India issued notice on a Public Interest Litigation challenging arbitrary charger bans across states. While the legal momentum is clearly moving toward protecting a resident’s right to charge, taking a court ruling to a heated Sunday morning society meeting rarely makes for a quick installation.

Treating an electric car like an extra microwave where everyone pulls their own line from their domestic meter does not scale. Societies have to build shared electrical infrastructure.

India’s EV transition is moving fast on two and three wheels. But passenger electric cars won’t reach mass adoption until high-rises solve the overnight charging puzzle.

The vehicles are ready, and buyers want them. But until building codes, dynamic load balancing, and society committees catch up with automotive engineering, the hardest part of driving electric in India will simply be charging at home.

#5753. Don't Overlook Cancer Risks in People With HIV, Experts Say

— Common cancers should be a key component of HIV care

Preventing and screening for common cancers seen in the general population should be a key component of HIV care, experts said at the recent IDWeek annual meeting.

Given cancer risks, "prevention becomes a key part of HIV care," noted Ma Somsouk, MD, of the University of California San Francisco, during a presentation at the meeting. That includes smoking cessation, treating hepatitis B and hepatitis C infection, managing diabetes and metabolic dysfunction-associated steatohepatitis, and screening for cancer.

Of note, AIDS-related cancers, including Kaposi sarcoma and non-Hodgkin lymphoma, are on the decline with improved therapies, but remain more common among people with HIV compared with the general population.

According to a Swedish retrospective case-control study, people with HIV were significantly more likely to develop human papillomavirus (HPV)-associated anal, penile, vulvar, and cervical cancers. An observational study showed that rates of oropharyngeal cancer among people with HIV have increased since 2000, particularly among heterosexual men ages 50 and older.

Somsouk noted that "the cancer burden is shifting with age in the HIV population." An estimated 70% of all people with HIV are 45 and older, he said, with greater risks of non-AIDS-related cancers like lung and prostate cancer.

Research has shown that people living with HIV have an increased risk for lung cancer, with some studies estimating risk to be 1.5 to 3-fold higher compared with the general population.

Furthermore, in a study of Medicaid beneficiaries, HIV was associated with a higher incidence of lung cancer (HR 1.75, 95% CI 1.56-1.96), with Black women at particular risk (HR 2.48, 95% CI 2.09-2.95).

While declining lung cancer deaths in the U.S. are tied to lower smoking prevalence, smoking risk among people with HIV remains "astronomical," said Chyke Doubeni, MD, MPH, of the Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center in Columbus, at the IDWeek annual meeting.

A 2016 meta-analysis showed that while 20% of U.S. adults smoked, more than half of people with HIV (54%) smoked -- and that rate rose to 74% among those with HIV and injection drug use.

Lung cancer screening among those with HIV isn't a one-time visit, Doubeni noted. Instead, it's a process that includes prevention, detection, and follow-up care. For clinicians, documenting the smoking history of a patient with HIV is the crucial first step in the prevention process, he said, followed by directing patients to smoking cessation resources.

Understanding how screening recommendations may differ, and how to apply their criteria to patients is also important.

For anyone ages 50 to 80 years with a 20 pack-year smoking history who smokes or quit in the last 15 years, the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) recommends annual lung cancer screening with low-dose CT. But Doubeni cautioned that many patients who die from lung cancer don't meet those criteria.

Recent American Cancer Society guidelines dropped the 15-year look-back and widened the potential screenable population. Why? "If you quit 20 years ago, your risk is lower, but it doesn't actually go away," Doubeni explained.

People with HIV are at average risk for colorectal cancer and standard screening recommendations apply. In Somsouk's presentation at IDWeek, he recommended colorectal cancer screening at ages 45 to 75 for those with HIV, with any test recommended by the USPSTF.

"In general, the best test is the one that you can get your patient to complete," Somsouk noted. But patients who have a positive fecal immunochemical test need a colonoscopy within 6 months, he added.

As for other gastrointestinal cancers, Somsouk advised screening for esophageal cancer with upper endoscopy or a capsule test for HIV patients with chronic gastroesophageal reflux disease plus three or more risk factors. He also recommended Helicobacter pylori testing for those with dyspepsia, peptic ulcers, or a family history of gastric cancer, as well as in high-risk groups, including immigrants from high-incidence regions. For those with cirrhosis of any cause or chronic hepatitis B virus infection, he advised alpha-fetoprotein testing and ultrasound to screen for liver cancer.

#5752. At $10,250, Could This 1995 Mercedes-Benz E320 Estate Bring Class To Car Line?

It's arguable that the W124-series was the last of the bank-vault-era Benzes. Today's Nice Price or No Dice E320 estate is one of the most desirable of those vaults to potentially invest in, and it has the rare third row. Let's see if its price tag has us opening up our hypothetical bank accounts.

Perhaps the polar opposite of a Mercedes of any era in build quality, safety, and engineering is the backyard-built kit car. Arising in the late 1950s and carrying through its heyday in the 1970s, home-built kit cars typically started with a donor chassis, often from an old Triumph or VW. A fresh fiberglass body gave that chassis new life, and the builder a sense of pride in creating a working, drivable car.

The Meyers Manx SR2 we looked at on Wednesday was an example—and an extremely rare one at that—of a '70s-era kit car. Created by industry legend Bruce Meyers, the SR2 proved to be his swan song, as his company folded shortly after its launch. That makes the small clutch of original SR2s (about 200 total) far more dear when they do come on the market. Our SR2 came with Corvair power and an interior ready for the next owner's taste in upholstery. At $17,000, however, that wasn't a winning combo. You all expected more for that amount and sent the Manx packing with a massive 93% 'No Dice' loss.

The closest today's 1995 Mercedes-Benz E320 Estate comes to a kit car is that it's the same model year Mercedes-Benz India, Ltd. began building similar models from knock-down kits at its Chakan assembly plant. This is also nearly the last year for the W124 series as a whole, as it was replaced, body style by body style, over the course of the next three years: 1995 (saloons), 1996 (estates), 1997 (coupes), and 1998 (cabriolets). Mercedes is nothing if not methodical.

This generation, which represents the facelifted version of the W124, was also the first to carry Mercedes' new naming convention of a class letter followed by a number indicating—sometimes only semi-accurately—engine displacement. The company has carried on this convention to this day. Because of that simple nomenclature, we can tell from the name that this is a mid-size E-Class (Executive class) model with a 3.2-liter engine. See? Easy-peasy.

That 3.2-liter engine is an M104.992 straight-six with double-overhead cams and four valves per cylinder. In North American guise, it produces a workmanlike 217 horsepower and 229 pound-feet of torque. As in all U.S.-bound cars, here it's matched with a five-speed 4G-Tronic automatic actuated through Mercedes' traditional pixelated snake shift gate.

According to the ad, this car has undergone significant repairs and maintenance over its 159,850 miles. The seller notes that under their ownership, they replaced the engine wiring harness, head and head gasket, radiator, and water pump. In addition, the car has a new climate control panel and has had two failed window regulators replaced. Other plusses include Continental tires with plenty of tread left and "flawlessly working" A/C.

With all that in the rearview, the seller now describes the car as "sorted and in excellent working condition." The receipts for the work will accompany the car in the sale for the next owner's reading enjoyment.

The car's appearance matches its mechanical status. The exterior is painted Smoke Silver, which the seller notes as paint code 702 for those keeping score. Yes, there will be a test later. The ad claims this Benz isn't a show car, but that it's solid enough to take out in public without embarrassment. On the downside, there are noticeable scratches and wear-through areas on the rear bumper and rocker moldings, but otherwise the paint looks to be in top-notch shape. Factory eight-hole alloys lend the car an all-original look, but could stand some work with a Scotchbrite to bring out their best nature.

In the cabin, the seller notes in the ad that the driver's seat shows wear and, wisely (sneakily?), doesn't show us how extensive it is. The rest of the cabin presents well, and the desirable rear-facing foldable third row is present and accounted for. If we're picking nits, we can point out that the (real) wood on the center console around the shifter is showing signs of losing battles with the sun. The seller, clearly appreciative of the car overall, claims it "wears its patina with pride."

On the plus side, a more modern stereo has been installed, but it's a Mercedes unit, so it keeps the vibe alive. Other plusses to consider: the car has a clean title, a passing smog test grade, and carries current registration, so there's no back-due malarkey for the next owner.

Apparently, under its current owner, this Benz has not been pressed into daily driver duty, as the seller claims to use it "as a Sunday driver and for the occasional school run." That's an acceptable compromise to keep the wear and tear at a minimum while still letting the car get some exercise. Based on the seller's description, though, this seems like a solid car to daily, and what a classy ride it would be.

Naturally, that class comes at a cost: $10,250. It should be noted that Mercedes' E-Class estates and their earlier W123 siblings are highly desirable in the present market, making them pricier than the average bear.

The question for you is whether or not this seemingly well-sorted estate is worth the seller's $10,250 asking. What do you think? Is that a fair deal for a classy classic? Or is that too much for something taking pride in its patina?

Los Angeles, California, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5751. AI-enabled virtual mental health startup Siggy launches from stealth

AI-enabled virtual mental health startup Siggy launches from stealth" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Siggy, an AI-enabled virtual mental health provider, has launched out of stealth with $6 million in pre-seed funding.

Siggy aims to build a complete mental health system that delivers continuous, personalized care. It combines therapy, prescription management and AI for intake and between-visits support. Intake is free; patients only pay if they accept treatment. Siggy is backed by Village Global, alongside several angel investors.

Patients begin with a free, anonymous conversation with Siggy’s AI that doesn’t require an account or login. It remembers everything, so patients don’t have to repeat their history when they eventually see a provider. An AI-powered preliminary diagnosis is reviewed by clinicians, and a patient is presented with treatment options. After a call with a human provider, patients log into Siggy to add existing providers or relatives to the system and confirm their treatment goals.

If a patient accepts treatment, they see a human clinician monthly with the option of engaging with Siggy’s AI between visits, which draws on their full history. Siggy’s therapy AI agent does not answer medical questions, instead directing them to human providers. It can help patients work through their goals, remind them of specific exercises and be a general sounding board. There are also safety protocols in place to flag higher-acuity needs to human providers.

Siggy can connect to wearables and offers a one-minute daily check-in on sleep, mood, how the body feels and medication side effects. These data combined form the Siggy Score, a single number out of 100 that patients and their clinicians see every day. When Siggy sees a pattern building, it flags the change to the patient’s clinician with the context behind it. Weekly check-ins in the first two months of treatment let clinicians decide whether to hold a dose, increase it or change a medication.

“It can build a really detailed picture of you rather fast, and the idea is if there are behavior patterns that are recurrent…[Siggy can] begin to help people gain agency and recognize them before they happen,” Siggy co-founder and CEO Jonathan Sabbagh told Fierce Healthcare.

Sabbagh co-founded Siggy with Edvard Engesaeth, M.D., now COO, an early Journey Clinical investor who co-founded Nurx. Siggy’s chief medical officer is Daniel Montville, M.D., a Mayo Clinic-trained psychiatrist. Sabbagh previously worked for two decades in finance before finding out he was living with chronic burnout and undiagnosed PTSD from childhood trauma.

Sabbagh sought psychedelic-assisted therapy and went back to school to study psychology before dropping out to co-found Journey Clinical, a large psychedelic-assisted therapy network in the U.S. with over 3,000 practices. “I saw a real gap where people drop off treatments, they don’t have continuous care,” Sabbagh told Fierce Healthcare.

About 16% of American adults take antidepressants, and a fifth annually drop out before completing the recommended course of treatment. “Prescriptions aren’t the problem … it really is a fragmented system where you don’t have therapists ever speaking with your prescriber,” Sabbagh said.

Siggy can prescribe medications, except controlled substances. Clinicians will taper patients off medications when they are ready. Longer-term, Siggy hopes to be able to identify other conditions through analytics on speech, labs, genetic testing and biomarkers, such as diabetes or ALS. It also hopes to build out neural datasets to support clinical trials for technologies like brain implants.

Siggy is covered by major commercial payers in all 50 states, including Blue Cross Blue Shield, Aetna, UnitedHealthcare and Humana. It also takes Medicaid, Medicare, TRICARE and is free for veterans.

“Our goal is to provide continuous care for life. We don’t really see meds as a feature. It is a combination of building persistent AI identity, being able to track progress and to make essentially personalized decisions that very few could afford,” Sabbagh said.
