#5749. Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings

Elevance Health sues over 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Elevance Health is the latest insurer to sue the feds over its performance in the latest round of star ratings for the Medicare Advantage program.

The company sued the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services in District of Columbia federal court on Friday, arguing that the 2027 star ratings calculations violated the Administrative Procedure Act in using 11 measures that it says were adopted unlawfully.

In the lawsuit, the company said that the harm from including these measures is "concrete and substantial." If they were excluded, it would increase the scores for 12 of its Medicare Advantage contracts and generate $516 million in additional quality bonus payments.

"Their inclusion therefore does more than violate statutory requirements in the abstract: it depresses Elevance Health’s ratings and reduces the funding available for the following payment year," per the lawsuit. "That funding could support broader supplemental benefits, lower premiums and cost-sharing, and improved care coordination."

"CMS’s unlawful methodology puts those resources out of reach," the company said.

In addition to the financial impacts, Elevance argues that the star ratings scores can also lead to reputational damage that makes it harder for them to compete in the MA market. It also provides prospective enrollees with inaccurate information, the company said.

"Beneficiaries cannot make informed coverage choices when the government’s own comparison tool conveys a distorted assessment of the plans available to them," Elevance said.

"An artificially depressed rating does not merely place Elevance Health at a competitive disadvantage; it undermines the very comparison the program is supposed to enable," the company continued. "A rating lowered by unlawful measures presents beneficiaries with a misleading signal of relative quality, rather than a lawful basis for distinguishing among competing plans."

CVS Heath's Aetna is joining the ranks of insurers suing over its results in the 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

The insured filed suit Monday in Georgia's Southern District Court, and argues that 28 measures used by the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services to calculate its scores were "unlawfully adopted" and should have been excluded.

The court is the same venue that ruled in favor of Clover Health over the summer, and set aside 20 measures that were used to determine its 2026 scores. That decision, which the feds have appealed, kicked off a broad recalculation of 2026 scores that looked different for insurers outside of Clover, triggering a wave of litigation.

In its lawsuit, Aetna cites the Clover decision in finding that CMS extended beyond its statutory authority in deploying those 20 measures.

The company said that calculating its scores using the 28 measures in question will likely cost it $2 billion in 2028 quality bonus payments and rebate retention. Those are funds that the insurer could "apply toward expanding member benefits, reducing premiums and investing in quality improvement."

Aetna adds that in addition to the monetary loss, having lower scores negatively impacts the company's reputation and its ability to attract potential members.

"The harm to CVS Health is not exclusively financial; lower ratings carry meaningful reputational harm, which can in turn impact membership," per the lawsuit. "By incorporating unlawful measures into the 2027 Star Ratings calculation, CMS cast CVS Health’s ratings in a poorer light in the eyes of Medicare Advantage consumers relative to peer MAOs, undermining CVS Health’s ability to compete for Medicare beneficiaries through enhanced benefits and the like."

Aetna did not respond to a request for comment at the time of publication.

Based on an analysis from Stephens, 69% of Aetna's MA members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 81%. That threshold is the key to securing quality bonus payments.

Aetna's complaint follows others focused on the 2027 scores from Centene and Alignment Healthcare (see those stories below).

Centene has become the latest insurer to sue the federal government over its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings.

In a lawsuit filed Thursday in Georgia's Southern District Court, the company argues that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services violated the Administrative Procedure Act in determining its latest scores. Centene says that CMS ran afoul of "core statutory safeguards" in deploying 17 measures.

The use of those measures ultimately caused "significant injury" to the company, according to the complaint. For 2027, 9% of Centene's members are enrolled in plans with at least four stars, which declined from 18% in the prior year.

"In implementing each of the individual Star measures that Centene challenges, CMS violated applicable law and thereby caused Centene to lose out on hundreds of millions of dollars in revenue that Centene could use to strengthen benefits, reduce premiums, and improve health outcomes," the company said in the lawsuit.

CMS argues that the court has already sided with a similar argument in its decision in favor of Clover Health earlier this year. In that case, which led to a broad recalculation in scores, the courts set aside 20 measures that were used in determining Clover's star ratings for 2026.

If the 17 measures in question had been excluded in calculating Centene's scores, the company estimates it would receive $448 million more in quality bonus payments for the 2028 plan year, funds it would deploy to expand benefits, lower premiums and enhance programs like care coordination.

In addition to the financial impact, a drop in star ratings also carries a reputational hit, Centene said.

"Beyond the financial impact to the company and its members, depressed ratings also inflict significant reputational damage and diminished membership," the company said in the complaint

Centene had not returned a request for comment at the time of publication.

Alignment Healthcare sued federal regulators, alleging that its 2027 Medicare Advantage star ratings were determined "in flagrant violation" of statute.

In the lawsuit filed Friday in District of Columbia federal court, the insurer says that the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services "has completely departed from Congress’s vision of leveraging predictable, valid measures developed with input from providers, plans, and the public" in establishing the methodology for the star ratings.

The company argues that rather than establish "consistent, stable specifications" for metrics through the traditional rule-making process, the agency instead detailed key measures through a series of "technical notes." This violates the Administrative Procedure Act, per the lawsuit.

The program's star ratings are the critical determinant in quality bonus payments to insurers as well as a tool that aims to make it easier for enrollees and brokers to identify benefits with the highest quality.

"Consistent application of the statutory requirements governing Star Ratings is therefore essential not only to plan payments and competition but also to ensuring that beneficiaries can rely on the Star Ratings as meaningful and comparable measures of plan quality," Alignment said in the lawsuit.

The company adds that multiple recent court decisions have set aside CMS' star ratings methodology, notably a ruling over the summer in favor of Clover Health that tossed 20 metrics used in the 2026 calculations. A Georgia federal judge determined that 10 measures utilized exceeded CMS' authority, while 10 other measures were promulgated inappropriately.

CMS recalculated the scores across the board following the ruling, but only set aside the 10 measures that the courts determined were outside the bounds of its regulatory authority. The agency maintained that approach in the 2027 calculations, Alignment argues.

"Congress created the Star Ratings program to allow Medicare Advantage beneficiaries to make informed decisions about plan enrollment based on clinically validated measures of plan quality that had been subjected to notice and comment," the company said. "CMS’s contrary approach fails to carry out the vision that Congress set forth by statute."

The Trump administration has appealed the Clover decision, so that legal case remains ongoing.

In addition, Alignment argues it suffered reputational and financial damage as a result of its 2027 scores, which plummeted compared to the prior year. Just 25% of its members are in plans with four or more stars for 2027, down from 100% in 2026.

Shares in the company tumbled following the release of the ratings, though stock prices have rebounded since.

Alignment sued the feds following the Clover decision to secure a recalculation for its scores that aligns with the court's decision, and in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said that the lawsuit aligns with that perspective.

"Our lawsuit seeks to ensure that the Star Ratings program operates as Congress intended and that Alignment is properly compensated for the care we provide," Alignment said in the statement. "We respect the judicial process and will present our case in court. Because this matter involves pending litigation, we will not comment on the specific legal arguments beyond our filings."

"Our focus remains on delivering exceptional care, improving health outcomes and advocating for a stable, lawful regulatory framework that supports seniors and strengthens the Medicare Advantage program," the company said.

#5748. 2028 Honda CR-V Will Go Hybrid-Only, Following The Toyota RAV4's Lead

We've been living with affordable hybrid vehicles for almost three decades now, but it's taken a surprisingly long time for some of the best-selling cars in the U.S. to go hybrid-only, even for models that have offered reliable hybrids for years. Toyota started doing it with the current Camry and new RAV4, and now Honda has confirmed that the next-gen CR-V will only be sold with hybrid powertrains.

Honda says the seventh-generation CR-V will be revealed next year, in time for the SUV's 30th anniversary, and it will ride on a new platform and use the company's new two-motor hybrid system, which our own Andy Kalmowitz got to try out in a Civic prototype last year. This new powertrain is said to be more powerful and more efficient, and the modular architecture is more rigid while being lighter-weight. It should be cheaper for Honda to produce, too, and a new electric all-wheel-drive system will be a big improvement.

Somehow we didn't get a hybrid CR-V in the U.S. until the 2020 model year, when the fifth-gen model was facelifted. Since then, annual CR-V hybrid sales have grown almost tenfold, and it currently makes up more than 50% of total CR-V sales. With the price difference between the hybrid and the regular CR-V being so relatively minor, and the gains in driving experience being so large, it makes sense to just hybridize the entire lineup. This year will be the third straight year in which Honda sells more than 400,000 CR-Vs, and upping the average efficiency of that many cars can make a big difference.

Many U.S.-spec CR-Vs are currently built in Ohio (the rest being made in Indiana), and Honda just announced plans for a new $3 billion plant in Ohio that will focus on hybrids, though gas-only and fully electric cars will be built there as well. Production there won't start until 2029, but Honda envisions an annual capacity of 240,000 units. Honda says it will introduced 15 new hybrids by 2030, most of which will be oriented towards the American market.

One of those hybrids will be the model at the top of this slide, or at least something similar to it. That rakish fastback is officially called the Hybrid Sedan Prototype, and it's probably just the next-gen Accord that's due to come out in the very near future. It wears what will be Honda's new design language, and you don't have to imagine how it'll look on the CR-V — lightly camouflaged prototypes have already been spied, as you can see from the Instagram post below.

I think it's looking pretty good, though it's a shame that the CR-V will seemingly lose its signature vertical taillights. We probably won't have to wait long to see the CR-V; expect an actual auto show debut, simply because a car this popular can actually take the spotlight and be of interest to the regular people who still go to shows. A Detroit Auto Show debut in January seems like a safe bet, but Honda could wait for Chicago in February or New York in March.

#5747. Assessing and Managing HIV's Higher Heart Disease Risks

— People with HIV are twice as likely to develop cardiovascular disease

Understanding how HIV can shape cardiovascular disease risk may help clinicians tailor care to patients' needs, which starts with statins for most adults with HIV.

"HIV holds its own as a risk factor for developing cardiovascular disease," Virginia Triant, MD, MPH, of Massachusetts General Hospital in Boston, said in a presentation at the IDWeek annual meeting.

A 2018 meta-analysis showed that people living with HIV were twice as likely to develop cardiovascular disease as those without HIV, Triant observed. Those with HIV also faced greater risks of myocardial infarction and stroke she added, putting HIV nearly on par with the cardiovascular relative risks found in the INTERHEART study for hypertension, diabetes, and smoking.

Traditional factors play a part in the elevated cardiovascular risks linked to HIV. People with HIV have greater rates of hypertension, diabetes, and dyslipidemia than their HIV-negative counterparts, Triant noted. Research also shows an 85% lifetime history of smoking among those with HIV.

That doesn't explain the whole story, Triant pointed out. Antiretroviral therapy brings its own set of cardiovascular risks.

Early protease inhibitors like darunavir were linked with cumulative myocardial infarction risks, and recent use of the nucleoside reverse transcriptase inhibitor abacavir was tied to increased myocardial infarction risk. It's not clear whether integrase strand transfer inhibitors also boost cardiovascular risk, with conflicting results from the RESPOND and HIV-CAUSAL studies.

After accounting for traditional risk factors and antiretroviral therapy, there's still a missing piece to the cardiovascular risk puzzle, Triant said. That piece may be the inflammation that comes with HIV.

The SMART study pointed to inflammation's role in cardiovascular disease, she noted. An analysis of data from the REPRIEVE study reinforced inflammation's role, even in people with well-controlled HIV and low to moderate cardiovascular risk.

Despite growing understanding of how HIV uniquely shapes cardiovascular risk, "this has not until very recently translated into differing and tailored clinical guidance for people with HIV," Triant stated. "I really see this as a challenge in delivering equitable care for cardiovascular care for people living with HIV."

Existing cardiovascular risk prediction scores for the general population fall short among people with HIV, incorporating only traditional risk factors without including HIV-related factors, Triant said. Even among those with HIV, cardiovascular risk varies by sex, race, ethnicity, and geographic location.

Among those with HIV, the Pooled Cohort Equations (PCE) that debuted in 2013 underestimated cardiovascular risk in women and those in low to moderate risk groups. The more comprehensive 2023 PREVENT equations mirrored the PCE risk underestimation for people with HIV, with major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE) rates greater than PREVENT predictions.

Given underestimated risk, people with HIV have been less likely to receive cardiovascular preventive care that matches guidelines, Triant said. The guidelines for the general population are also less than what's needed for people with HIV, she said.

That began to change with cholesterol guidelines from the American Health Association (AHA) and other groups that added consideration of HIV as a cardiovascular disease risk factor. A 2019 AHA scientific statement that specifically focused on people with HIV highlighted risk-enhancing factors such as prolonged viremia, low CD4 counts, delays in starting antiretroviral therapy (ART), and metabolic syndrome.

Still, "what was really missing from the field was a large trial with robust data to support a recommendation for cardiovascular prevention for people with HIV," Triant said.

Then came REPRIEVE in 2023. That phase III clinical trial included only people with HIV on ART who had low to moderate cardiovascular disease risk and didn't meet general guidelines' thresholds for statin therapy. REPRIEVE showed that statin therapy was linked to a 36% reduction in MACE risk compared with placebo.

These results weren't driven by a subgroup. "It was reduction whether you looked by demographics, or traditional risk factors, or traditional HIV-related factors," Triant explained.

REPRIEVE was the first randomized trial that showed a cardiovascular prevention benefit that was different from the general population. "This is really paradigm-shifting," she noted. A CDC analysis found that full implementation of REPRIEVE's approach in people with HIV and low to moderate cardiovascular disease risk could lead to 7,418 fewer first major adverse cardiovascular events over 5 years.

The REPRIEVE findings have spurred practice-changing guidelines for people with HIV, Triant said.

In 2024, an HHS panel rolled out recommendations calling for daily moderate-intensity statins in people with HIV ages 40 to 75 years with low to intermediate (less than 20%) 10-year risk of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD). In those with less than 5% risk, the HHS panel favored starting at least moderate-intensity statins.

The American College of Cardiology and other societies followed that with a dyslipidemia guideline in 2026 that recommended that everyone with HIV ages 40 to 75 years on ART receive statin therapy to reduce ASCVD event risk and slow coronary atherosclerosis.

"This is really a striking recommendation, and an important one for the field," Triant explained. "Notably, this is regardless of calculated baseline risk, regardless of LDL, for people in this age group."

Clinicians might want to consider starting statins at age 30 for those with HIV, she added. "I would strongly consider HIV to be an ASCVD risk enhancer, even at that age, even though we don't have the trial data," Triant said.

Even with these newly tailored guidelines, implementation will be a challenge, she noted. Statin uptake in people with HIV was suboptimal before REPRIEVE, with 80% of patients eligible for statins under earlier guidelines not getting them.

"Dismantling disparities that we know exist in cardiovascular prevention and care remains a critical challenge," Triant said. "The recent guidelines are likely to be practice-changing for people with HIV, but only if implemented on a broad scale."

#5746. What's The Worst Way You've Abused A Rental Car?

Unless you only travel within driving distance of home or are willing to spend the time and money on shipping, we will all end up in a situation where renting a car is a necessity. There may be plenty of cities with fantastic public transportation systems, but you just need a car to venture out and explore the countryside. Many of us are far more care-free behind the wheel when the vehicle is owned by an abstract corporate entity and not a person with a face.

What's the worst way you've abused a rental car? I'm not talking about returning it with an empty tank. That's just a surcharge that rental companies are more than happy to add to your bill. Abuse is real, lasting damage that's an absolute nightmare to reverse. Let me use a hypothetical beach day in the South of France as an example. You and a few friends take a decent Peugeot 408 to a sleepy village on the Mediterranean. After a fun day of sand and surf, everyone piles back into the sedan right off the beach, covering the interior with sand. That's the appetizer. On the drive back, you get impatient sitting behind a truck on a two-lane road. You pull into the oncoming lane but don't get entirely clear, and you sideswipe the truck. The 408 is far worse off, with its entire right side scratched up. That's abuse!

Besides outright crashing a rental car, the worst thing you could do is race a pack of them. Rental car racing actually is a significant part of NASCAR lore. The mischievous tradition has endured stock car racing's evolution from a moonshiners' pastime to a billion-dollar sport. Several current Cup Series drivers have admitted to racing each other and their team members. In many instances, rental car racing is just a fun way to salvage a rained-out test day. Everyone just piles on to the track once the showers stop and run laps to dry the racing surface. On race day, it's pushing the limit on the drive out of the track. Joe Gibbs Racing driver Chase Briscoe told The Athletic in 2025, "Honestly, the craziest thing, I think, is watching the team guys leave a racetrack. It is like the best racing and just the most chaos you've ever seen in your life."

Remember to leave your stories of rental car abuse in the comments section below. Be sure to mention the make or model of the car that you returned to be someone else's problem.

#5745. HealthTap unveils virtual primary care program for Medicare Advantage plans

HealthTap unveils virtual primary care program for Medicare Advantage plans" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

HealthTap is rolling out a new program for Medicare Advantage plans that seeks to help members form a connection with a primary care doctor that carries forward.

The program leans on a combination of proactive outreach and access to a patient's historical medical records to support plans in identifying needs or risks, filling care gaps and coordinating follow-up or other necessary care. It's built to directly support individuals who may not have a PCP on file.

HealthTap reaches out to those members proactively, the company said, and helps them schedule an initial virtual visit with a provider who stays connected for preventive services, chronic condition management and medication concerns.

"The angle that we're taking is: wouldn't it be cool if, in fact, your virtual primary care physician was just like a bricks and mortar primary care physician?" said Rick Cochran, senior vice president of health plans at HealthTap, in an interview with Fierce Healthcare.

In an ideal scenario, a patient who has a consistent primary care physician who they see over the course of multiple years and who they come to trust, he said. HealthTap is looking to build that experience with virtual primary care docs, and reach patients who are disconnected from the health system with critical services.

Cochran said that the team, at times, receives feedback about concern that a program like this could upset in-network practices. However, he said that HealthTap views these service lines as complementary, and the company as offering another way for insurers to reach members who are underserved and in need of help.

"There's a bunch of people who haven't been receiving care that we can work with," he said. "We're not attempting to disrupt anything. We're attempting to complement things."

Through the program, HealthTap can connect with members via text messaging, email, direct mail or phone, at the plan's discretion. The member can then schedule the appointment for themselves and choose a board-certified primary care physician based in their state.

Between video visits, the member can also connect with their physician through secure messaging, HealthTap said.

In aiming to make the experience feel as much like traditional, brick-and-mortar clinics as possible, Cochran said that members are allowed to switch their physicians and find the one that best matches their individual health needs.

And to better support the physicians, HealthTap pulls from health information networks to pull key medical records, including relevant data from before an individual enrolled in their current plan. That set-up also means that the patient can carry their relationship with a HealthTap doc forward should they switch plans, Cochran said.

Cochran said that COVID-19 really kicked down the door to virtual care for seniors, and there's significant opportunity in MA for a program like HealthTap's, as these patients often have multiple comorbidities and may face barriers to seeing a doctor in person, such as the lack of transportation.

The company did an initial deployment with one MA plan and found that 35% of the initial outreach cohort scheduled an appointment, and 19% completed their first visit within six weeks. Satisfaction scores for both patients and physicians are high, Cochran said.

The approach gives doctors more time to spend working "to make people better, not crank through visits," he said.

"Our physician satisfaction score is very high because our physicians have the time to spend with the patient to ensure that the patient's needs are met," he said.
